“ Abner Chou undertakes the bold and dynamic challenge of understanding and em­
bracing the interpretive perspective of the biblical authors. Join him in the venture. 
See with new eyes. Understand the Bible. Have it change your life.”
—Jim Hamilton
Professor of Biblical Theology, The Southern Baptist Theological Seminary
“This book is a remarkable work on every level. Dr. Chou has a grasp of the sancti­
fied science of hermeneutics, where he has learned from the biblical writers them­
selves— the prophets of the Old Testament and the apostles of the New Testament. 
By comprehensive knowledge of the details that yield the true interpretation of bib­
lical texts from Genesis to Revelation; by clear grasp of the progressive nature of di­
vine revelation; by masterful insight into the flow of redemptive history and biblical 
theology, Chou provides a new clarity on the unity and continuity of prophetic and 
apostolic hermeneutics, thus showing lucidly that the New Testament writers used 
the Old Testament contextually “per its original authorial intent.”
This work, however, is not only hermeneutical method stated. It is hermeneutics 
applied. To show the power of accurate hermeneutics, Chou applies them to the 
most difficult texts in which the New Testament writers use the Old Testament.
This book will prove to be a rare treasure not only to theologians, but to exposi­
tors who face the hard challenges that seem to betray a unified hermeneutic. Dr. 
Chou, dismissing simplistic solutions as inadequate because they lead to misin­
terpretation, presents strong arguments for the more complex approach of inter- 
textuality that yields the true interpretation to the diligent student of Scripture. 
This is a book that should be in the hands of every Christian who takes God’ s 
Word seriously.”
—John MacArthur 
Pastor, Grace Community Church
“ A perpetual conversation among biblical scholars concerns Scriptures use 
of Scripture, both the New Testament’ s use of the Old Testament and the Old 
Testament’ s use of the Old Testament. Though essays and books reveal differ­
ing, even competing, explanations of Scripture’ s use of Scripture, something of a 
rapprochement among diverse schools of thought seems to be emerging. Abner 
Chou’ s contribution to this conversation reflects this as he engages leading evan­
gelical scholars and finds wide swaths of agreement with them as both they and 
be take the Scriptures seriously. Whether Chou’ s explanations of how the Bible’ s 
writers use earlier Scriptures convinces readers to embrace his understanding of 
difficult texts, his most central thesis ought to convince readers. For Chou, taking 
Scripture seriously includes being able to trace and reproduce how the Old Testa­
ment prophets and the New Testament apostles and prophets used the Scriptures 
that existed prior to their own times. Our proper use of Scripture stands in conti­
nuity with how Scripture’ s writers used Scripture.”
—Ardel B. Caneday
Professor of New Testament & Greek, University of Northwestern

PREFACE
W
hat would cause a college student to stay in his dorm room for all 
of spring break, eating nothing but ramen and popcorn? For me, it 
was the discovery of the New Testaments use of the Old and all the im­
plications it brought forth. I was taking a class in advanced hermeneutics 
at The Master s University when I began to read about the complexities of 
how the apostles read the Old Testament. Concepts like corporate soli­
darity, authorial intent, redemptive history, echoes, and allusions showed 
me the profound depth and complexity of Gods Word. I also knew of 
the challenges. People accused the apostles of being strange in how they 
interpreted the Old Testament. All of that began one of my missions in 
life: to investigate this matter thoroughly and (hopefully) vindicate the 
biblical writers. So, I stayed in my dorm room and read all spring break. 
That began a journey where I ended up obtaining masters degrees and a 
doctorate in this area.
A little over a decade from my undergraduate days, I have the privi­
lege of writing in a book what I have learned. The result of this is an effort 
that goes far beyond myself and I have many to thank. The teacher of 
the advanced hermeneutics class, Dr. William Varner, was the one who 
launched my thinking in this area and nurtured me all the way through. 
I am incredibly grateful to the Lord for him. Another William, Dr. Wil­
liam Barrick, picked up the baton and shaped my thinking as well. Both of 
them have served as a model of faithful biblical scholarship in the context 
of a faithful ministry to the Lord. Again, I am immensely grateful to God 
for their labor of love on my behalf. Even before writing a word on a page, 
many were already involved in my life to prepare me for this moment.
I am not the best writer, and that really is an understatement. Thus, for 
me to write a book demands an entire network of support. Foremost is my 
family. They bore with me patiently through good days and bad. They gave 
me hugs during the toughest times of writing. When your children under­
stand the editing process by age six, you know they have gone through a 
lot! So thank you, Nehemiah, Naomi, and Meital for your encouragement. 
Even our youngest, Hannah, only two months old, participated. I will not 
forget those nights while I held her while editing the manuscript. She may 
not remember this time but it will be a precious memory to me. Moreover, 
my wife, Johanna, played the most instrumental role in all of this. She en-
9

--- Page 11 ---
10 Preface
couraged, strengthened, cared, listened, and prayed for me. She read and 
responded to my chapters. Her work in so many ways was crucial to this 
book, and so I want to honor her for it as well. I am truly blessed, and the 
book you hold in your hands is a demonstration of that.
I also want to thank The Master s University faculty and staff for their 
help in this matter. Not only did two administrative assistants (Amy Kid­
der and Megan Low) lend me their immense help, but other faculty had 
to endure my tireless discussions about what I was writing. I also want 
to thank my research assistant, Chris Williams, whose help was invalu­
able in the research and editing process. Special thanks also goes to Peter 
Goeman, who also gave me insightful and encouraging comments on my 
manuscript. I always chuckle that even social media provided assistance. 
As with other writing projects, a Facebook group, Nerdy Language Ma­
jors, gave me valuable feedback, affirmation, and encouragement. Finally, 
the Lord used the students of the college to strengthen me. Not only were 
they patient with me but they also supported me. Notes, letters, energy 
drinks, and emails all came to my office in a moving display of the caring 
community I have at my “workplace.” My labor is primarily for these stu­
dents, whom I desire to know the Word of God and the God of the Word 
deeply and to live for him fully.
Along that line: As you read this book, if your appreciation for Gods 
Word grows ... if you are increasingly impressed with its unity and com­
plexity . . .  if your hunger to know it increases . . .  if you become better 
aware of how to study it, then I have done my job. My prayer is that God 
would establish the work of my hands to that end (Ps. 90:17).

THE QUEST FOR AUTHORIAL LOGIC
Even when there is use of the Old Testament with no apparent 
interest in prophetic fulfillment, there appears to be a redemptive- 
historical rationale at work behind the scenes. —G. K. Beale1
W
e may encounter the subject of hermeneutics in seminary, college, 
or a class at church. In the classroom, we learn hermeneutics deals 
with the essential principles by which we accurately understand the Scrip­
tures.2  However, the topic is not just some academic subject. Its importance 
extends far beyond the classroom. Knowing Gods Word is foundational 
for godly living (Ps. 1:2; 119:11; 2 Tim. 3:16-17; 2 Peter 1:3). Our thinking 
about hermeneutics does not just stay in the realm of academia but ulti­
mately shapes how we live and whether or not we please God. What is at 
stake when we study hermeneutics? Simply put, it is our entire Christian 
life and ministry. Hermeneutics is not a negotiable issue; it is essential for 
the Christian walk. God puts a premium on “getting it right” (Neh. 8:8; 
2 Tim. 2:15) and condemns those who twist the Scripture (2 Peter 3:16).
WHAT IS TRULY CHRISTIAN HERMENEUTICS?
So what comprises a “ good hermeneutic?”3  We may be familiar with the 
terms “literal-grammatical-historical” hermeneutics or the notion of seeking 
the “authors intent” as opposed to our own meaning. The Scripture teaches 
that the Holy Spirit moved men to write his Word such that their words are 
the very message of God (2 Peter 1:20-21). Traditionally, we believe we ought 
to understand the ideas the (dual) author intended through the normal rules
1. Beale, “Jesus and His Followers,” 398.
2. Osborne, Hermeneutical Spiral 21.
3. The singular of hermeneutic in this work refers to a particular practice of interpreta­
tion. The general field of hermeneutics (note plural) refers to the set of principles that 
one should have in studying Scripture.
13

--- Page 15 ---
14 The Quest for Authorial Logic
of language and the facts of history.4  This formulates the basis by which we 
evaluate right and wrong understandings of Scripture. Accuracy occurs when 
our reading of a text matches the (dual) author s ideas, and misinterpretation 
occurs when we misconstrue that intent. This demands examination of his­
torical background, context, grammar, and individual words.5
Conversely, how do we know our traditional definitions are cor­
rect? Several major works have philosophically defended the principles of 
literal-grammatical-historical hermeneutics. They show we are morally 
bound to understand the Scripture in light of Gods intention.6  They make 
this case based upon linguistics, the nature of communication, and speech- 
act theory.7  These books rebut the postmodern frameworks of text-centered 
hermeneutics or reader response, which state any possible notion within the 
text or whatever the reader sees comprise legitimate meaning.8
The philosophical approach is useful to engage these ideas. Never­
theless, hermeneutical philosophy still rests upon our theology which is 
based upon our understanding of Scripture.9  Thus, in the end, the Bible 
becomes foundational for our hermeneutic. That is fitting since the Scrip­
ture speaks to the subject. God demands accurate interpretation of his 
Word (e.g., Acts 17:11; 1  Tim. 4:13-15; 2 Tim. 2:15; 1  Peter 2:2). The Bible 
is concerned about hermeneutics.
So we ultimately should go back to the Bible to learn how to study it.1 0  
Such an approach is not novel. In fact, evangelicals have traditionally used
4. Osborne, Hermeneutical Spiral, 21; Thomas, “The Hermeneutical Landscape,” 20-21.
5. Zuck, Basic Bible Interpretation: A Practical Guide to Discovering Biblical Truth, 14-16.
6. Hirsch, Validity in Interpretation; Vanhoozer, Is There a Meaning?
7. Vanhoozer, Is There a Meaning?, 198-263; Stein, “Benefits of an Author-Oriented 
Approach,” 451-66. Speech-act theory refers to characterizing speech as an action. 
Accordingly, speech has three major components: locution (what is said), illocution 
(what is meant), and perlocution (intended reaction). This loosely corresponds to a 
physical action: locution (what one did), illocution (what that communicates to the 
recipient), and perlocution (the ways one is supposed to respond to the act). A punch 
is a physical action which may communicate anger, and the responses could include 
dodging the punch. In the same way, texts have the same mechanisms which relate to 
authorial intent.
8. Barthes, The Pleasure of the Text; Payne, The Fallacy of Equating Meaning with the Hu­
man Authors Intention; Gadamer, Truth and Method; Fish, Is There a Text in This Class?
9. Vanhoozer, Is There a Meaning?, 204-6.
10. A major issue with this approach is how we can derive principles and doctrines from 
the Scripture for the purpose of hermeneutics in the first place. If we are still deter­
mining our hermeneutic for Scripture, how can we interpret it to do so? While that 
question is outside the scope of this book, I can refer the reader to the philosophical 
defenses of hermeneutics for answers. In sum, God created language and its opera­
tion is embedded in the way we communicate. This is why we can understand texts 
and even pursue authorial intent. As we read the text of Scripture, the Bible explains 
why we could always do this. It solidifies the nature of communication and the reality 
of authorial intent. Furthermore, Scripture reinforces the absolute moral necessity 
to seek the authorial intent of the Bible. See Vanhoozer, Is There a Meaning?; Hirsch, 
Validity in Interpretation.

--- Page 16 ---
15The Quest for Authorial Logic
the doctrines of inspiration, inerrancy, and illumination to ground their 
understanding of hermeneutics. Reymond states, “the Scriptures doctrine 
of Scripture, espousing its own revelatory and inspired character, binds us 
to the grammatical/historical method of exegesis.”1 1  Similarly, Zuck com­
ments that in light of the human aspect of inspiration, “Each biblical writ­
ing—that is, each word, sentence, and book—was recorded in a written 
language and followed normal, grammatical meanings, including figura­
tive language.”1 2  The list of this type of argumentation goes on.1 3  Evangeli­
cals have rightly stressed that biblical hermeneutics ultimately come from 
the Bible. God sets the rules for how his Word ought to be understood and 
that should comprise a “Christian hermeneutic.”
However, in the process of formulating our hermeneutic from the 
Scripture, we run into a significant problem. It begins with the question 
of how our “Christian hermeneutic” precisely operates. We may know the 
principles and convictions, but how does that play out when we approach 
a text? We can put together all the word studies, historical backgrounds, 
and grammar but how does that actually produce the authors intent or 
meaning? Moreover, how do we connect our interpretation of our text 
with theology? How do we know which principle to draw from a text? 
How do we know whether an author intended a certain theological idea 
or not? What should we learn from the stories of Scripture? Is the point 
of David and Goliath that we can slay our own giants? If not, what is the 
real idea of that text and how do we know? We encounter a similar co­
nundrum when we ask how to preach or teach Christ from the Old Testa­
ment. Should we read Christ into every text even if he is not in view in 
the original context? Once again, what is the bridge between what the text 
says and the theology it conveys? These questions show we not only seek 
to learn from the Scripture hermeneutical principles but also hermeneuti­
cal practice (i.e., how to apply those principles in our study of Scripture).
The Bible provides an answer to those questions as well. Conversely, 
this is where the problem arises. The way the biblical writers used the Scrip­
tures may be a little more “troubling,” particularly when we look at the New 
Testaments use of the Old. The apostles seem to read the Old Testament 
“creatively.” For example, Paul seems to believe the rock in Israels wilderness 
wanderings was Christ, when the Old Testament makes no mention of this 
(1 Cor. 10:4).1 4  Matthew applies an Old Testament text to the Messiah, even 
though it was originally about Israel (Hos. 11:1 in Matt. 2:15).1 5  That same 
gospel writer later claims Jeremiah prophesied about Judas, even though the

quotation comes from Zechariah (Zech. 11:13, in Matt. 27:9).1 6  Paul uses an 
Old Testament text, which says that people are cursed for not keeping the 
law, to condemn those who actually keep the law (Gal. 3:10; cf. Deut. 27:26). 
Peter cites Psalm 109:8 to argue that the church should elect a new apostle to 
take Judas place, when that psalm does not refer to Judas at all (Acts 1:20). 
In each of these instances, the apostles seem to ignore the original context 
of the Old Testament. These examples are just a minute sample of the prob­
lems within the New Testaments use of the Old.1 7  Do they indicate there is 
something more than a literal-grammatical-historical hermeneutic for our 
hermeneutic to be truly “Christian”?
ASKING THE RIGHT QUESTION
Thus, our desire to have a biblical hermeneutic has led us into quite a dilem­
ma. Ironically, by studying the Bible to learn how to avoid misinterpretation, 
we seem to run into it. Should we do as the apostles say but not as they do?1 8
We are not the first to go down this path. Scholars have wrestled with 
this question and have come up with a variety of solutions. Some argue since 
the apostles were inspired, we do not have the ability to repeat their herme­
neutical methods.1 9  Others maintain the apostles had a new hermeneutic, 
which warrants a certain degree of freedom to reinterpret the Bible.2 0  They 
argue the New Testament writers illustrate we need to read the Scriptures in 
new ways and that such spiritualization and allegorization is a truly “Chris­
tian hermeneutic.” Still others contend the apostles upheld the context of the 
Old Testament. They claim that if we examine the Old Testament further, 
this would be clear to us.2 1  Though there are various views, everyone agrees 
hermeneutical discussions inevitably arrive at the New Testaments use of 
the Old.2 2  The way the biblical writers used the Bible is the crux interpretum

instincts ... if we don’t follow them, we are either admitting that the New Testament 
authors were misguided in showing us how Jesus is connected to the Old Testament, 
or that their hermeneutic is theirs alone and cannot be reproduced today.”

the OT as it really is.

apostolic hermeneutics and then move it to the side. We must ask what we can learn 
from this about the nature of the Bible and what it means to interpret it today.”

in fully answering how we have a Christian/biblical hermeneutic. It is an is­
sue we cannot ignore.2 3  How can we genuinely claim to have a hermeneutic 
based upon the Scripture which ignores the Scriptures where the biblical 
writers interpreted previous revelation? A hermeneutic that does not take 
all Scripture into account is not a biblical hermeneutic.
Silva highlights even greater dangers in disregarding this matter:
If we refuse to pattern our exegesis after that of the apostles, we are in prac­
tice denying the authoritative character of their scriptural interpretation— 
and to do so is to strike at the very heart of the Christian faith.24
Accordingly, if we really want a hermeneutic based upon all of Gods 
Word, we must deal with this issue. The matter of the New Testaments 
use of the Old is not just an erudite academic discussion. Rather, it is at 
the core of who we are as interpreters of the Scripture. Do we learn how 
to understand Scripture from its writers or is there some reason why their 
hermeneutic is different than our own?
Responding to this issue is easier said than done. It involves a whole 
host of other matters including (but not limited to) textual criticism, liter­
ary theory (e.g., intertextuality, echoes),2 5  and the Greek translation of the 
Old Testament.2 6  It also includes historical backgrounds, in particular, the 
hermeneutical methodology of the apostles contemporaries and second 
temple Jewish literature.2 7  Having all that in mind, one can proceed to do 
an exegetical analysis of both Old Testament and New Testament texts. The 
interpreter must understand the contexts of both texts and figure out how 
they precisely interact. On top of that, the exegete must think through a 
wide range of interpretative options,2 8  biblical theological implications,2 9  as

18 The Quest for Authorial Logic
well as systematic theological concerns.3 0  Dealing with all of these issues is 
dizzying. The New Testaments use of the Old is a complex puzzle to be sure.
However, while all the matters above carry great importance, they can 
sidetrack us from the matter at hand. More information does not always 
bring more clarity (cf. Eccl. 12:12). Rather, we need to ask the right ques­
tion to obtain a useful answer. Beales quote at the beginning of this chap­
ter points us back to such a fundamental question: What was the author 
thinking? How did he reach his conclusion?3 1  That is the question we need 
to ask. All of the factors above revolve around that issue. Even more, this 
is the heart of the topic of the “New Testament s use of the Old.” The very 
word use refers to the way the apostles thought about and applied the Old 
Testament.3 2  Thus, the author s logic is the essential matter.
A variety of scholars have affirmed this assertion.3 3  Discussing this 
matter from the vantage point of biblical theology, Hamilton states:
The biblical authors used biblical theology to interpret the Scriptures avail­
able to them and the events they experienced. For the believing community, 
the goal of biblical theology is simply to learn the practice of interpretation 
from the biblical authors so that we can interpret the Bible and life in this 
world the way they did.34
On the literary level, Hays also acknowledges the author s rationale is 
the key question. He also acknowledges very little has been done to eluci­
date this idea:
Even those studies concerned with theological issues have little to say about 
Paul as interpreter of Scripture. This is a regrettable state of affairs, because 
the question of how Paul read Scripture is of great importance for grasping 
the logic and purpose of his arguments. Is there some method or hermeneu­
tic that can account for Pauls exegesis?35
Hays quote calls on us to ask and answer the issue of the author s 
logic, which he observes we have often failed to do. Before we can call

the apostles odd, reject their hermeneutic, or accept their hermeneutic, 
we need to understand what they actually did. Only then can we see if we 
should do as they do or as they say or, as I will argue, both.
THE QUEST FOR AUTHORIAL LOGIC
Asking the question of the author s rationale leads us to what I would like 
to term “the quest for authorial logic.” Beales quote at the opening of the 
chapter unveils this before us. In addressing what the apostles thought, he 
notes a “redemptive-historical rationale” working in the background. The 
quest for authorial logic concerns bringing the biblical writers logic that 
is in the background to the foreground. It is our search to understand the 
underlying methodology and reasoning that guides their reading of Scrip­
ture. This quest is far from new or revolutionary.3 6  Even so, it is helpful to 
create a clear niche for this type of study, which is at the heart of the New 
Testament s use of the Old.
We should further articulate the nature of this endeavor. One may 
begin discussing the authors rationale by describing what the apostles 
thought. Some have described what the biblical writers thought about 
a variety of Old Testament passages or a variety of theological topics.3 7  
Others talk about what the apostles thought relative to their interpretative 
presuppositions.3 8  These observations are important and the questions of 
“what” are vital. It is difficult to evaluate the logic of apostolic hermeneu­
tics if we have not determined what they thought, or the key presupposi­
tions involved in their conclusions.
However, the quest for authorial logic deals with more than this. 
Scholars want to know how the biblical writers derived their presupposi­
tions, how they chose to use that presupposition with a given passage, and 
even how they chose to use a particular text in their argument.3 9  For in­
stance, why did Matthew use Hosea 11:1 to prove his point when he could 
have equally cited Exodus 4:23 which has similar language? What makes 
Hosea 11:1 a better choice than another passage for Matthews purpose? 
These questions are important, and to brush them off is to tacitly admit 
that the apostles are random. We need to think through these issues.
36. See quotes above in fn. 32; see also, Moyise, Paul and Scripture, 1. In discussing 
Pauls own hermeneutic, Moyise states, “However, what is potentially more useful 
than just citing Pauls answers to first-century questions is to study how Paul inter­
preted Scripture”

140-41.

Thought of St. Paul,” 428.

20 The Quest for Authorial Logic
Consequently, this shows the quest for authorial logic should not just 
answer a “what” question but also the “how” question. This is a quest to 
know how or the process by which the biblical writers interpreted a text, 
derived presuppositions, associated them with certain texts, and came up 
with their assertions. This book intends to begin to answer the “how ques­
tion” in regards to the New Testament s use of the Old.
AN INITIAL SUGGESTION:
THE EXISTENCE OF THE PROPHETIC HERMENEUTIC
While it may appear completely counterintuitive, I suggest an investigation 
of the apostles use of the Old Testament begins in the Old Testament itself. 
One can certainly see why one might begin in the New Testament in deal­
ing with the New Testaments use of the Old. Since we are figuring out how 
the apostles used antecedent revelation, it is reasonable to pay attention to 
their claims, methods, and presuppositions. Nonetheless, we will inevitably 
end up in the Old Testament because the apostles point us in that direction. 
The apostles deal with prior revelation and so to grasp what they meant and 
thought, we need to understand the source. I suggest greater attention on 
the Old Testament may help us unlock the thoughts of the apostles.
This inkling is not without merit. Accepted methodology requires an 
investigation of the Old Testament.4 0  Scholars advise us to take heed of the 
interconnectedness and complexity of that part of the Bible.4 1  They fre­
quently appeal to the idea observed by C. H. Dodd that quotations made 
by the New Testament writers actually point to entire contexts in the Old 
Testament.4 2  Based upon this, those investigating the New Testaments 
use of the Old pay attention to how a reference to the Old Testament 
provides a window into bigger theological ideas and themes. Those 
concepts—including corporate solidarity, exile, and Gods plan—can of­
ten help explain how the apostles were thinking.4 3  Scholars acknowledge 
the Old Testament contributes to our understanding of the apostles.
We can take these observations a step further in our discussion of 
authorial logic. I propose the interconnectedness and intricacy of the Old 
Testament reveals something deeper about the prophets themselves: They 
had their own hermeneutic. Those familiar with the New Testaments use 
of the Old often refer to the “apostolic hermeneutic,” a term discussing the 
New Testaments writers interpretative methodology. Perhaps an Old Tes­
tament counterpart exists. One factor that supports this is how scholars

have recognized the reality of intertextuality in the Old Testament. The 
word “intertextuality” is a key term in this book and, for my purposes, 
discusses how the biblical writers allude to other parts of Scripture. It spe­
cifically refers to how the inspired authors expounded upon previous rev­
elation in their own writings.4 4  Such activity in the Old Testament argues 
for the existence of a “prophetic hermeneutic.” The Old Testament writers 
themselves were exegetes and theologians who understood and correlated 
their texts with previous revelation. This formed intentional “networks of 
texts” in the first canon.
In light of these correlations, the observation of Dodd gains even 
greater traction. Individual Old Testament texts are windows into larg­
er contexts because they are intentionally part of a series of passages the 
prophets have woven together.4 5  The apostles thought through certain 
passages with certain biblical theological ideas because the prophets had 
already made those associations. The Old Testament writers derived cer­
tain concepts from their careful exegesis of prior revelation and integrated 
those concepts into their own writings. These become the presuppositions 
and backbone of the apostolic rationale.
Thus, the apostles are not arbitrary; their thoughts and assumptions are 
directly tied to interconnected Old Testament texts woven together by the 
prophets. However;  if this is true, then the apostles follow the prophetic herme­
neutic and logic. The continuity between prophetic and apostolic hermeneu­
tics provides the modus operandi of the apostolic rationale. While we may be
44. Fishbane, Biblical Interpretation, 7-14; Broyles, “Traditions, Intertextuality, and Can­
on,” 167. See also, Huizenga, New Isaac, 43-58. As should be clear in this chapter and 
will be made clearer in the next, I use the term simply as one text alluding to another 
without any postmodern baggage attached. Some argue such a use of intertextual­
ity is incorrect for it originated and was purposed to discuss deconstructive ideas. 
Thus, the term should be jettisoned unless one deals in those perspectives. See Meek, 
“Intertextuality,” 280-91. Ironically, those who deny authorial intent insist on using 
a term in the way the author supposedly intended. Furthermore, as Huizenga points 
out, this criticism is not valid in this case for two reasons. First, the critique is often 
against biblical scholars who illegitimately use the term to discuss the issues of his­
torical criticism and sources. My use in this work argues against this and in a sense, 
deconstructs the higher critical school. Second, Huizenga observes even Kristeva, the 
founder of intertextuality, engages texts and intertextuality the way biblical scholars 
have dealt with it. He rightly notes the issue is not what intertextuality is as a property 
of texts but rather if the purpose and foundation that originally surrounded the term 
are true. Is deconstructionism legitimate? As will be later discussed, my answer is 
no. At the same time, the characteristic of intertextuality, the interconnectedness of 
texts, is still true apart from that baggage. In fact, the way biblical scholars, like Hays, 
use the term legitimately fits within the authors intent of that term. Inner-biblical 
exegesis and allusion are also suitable descriptors; however, they fall somewhat short 
since they often imply a one to one textual correlation as opposed to the fact that 
a text could be interwoven with a network of texts. Hence, intertextuality, with the 
qualifications given, is still a useful term.

tempted to look at the apostolic hermeneutic, the key to the authorial logic 
of the New Testament may very well be in the prophetic hermeneutic.
THESIS: CONTINUITY OF PROPHETIC,  
APOSTOLIC, AND CHRISTIAN HERMENEUTICS
Let us not lose sight of the original issue. I have argued hermeneutics is im­
portant for our Christian life. That causes us to ask what is a truly Christian 
hermeneutic. We need to figure out the convictions the Bible requires us 
to have when approaching it, and how to apply those principles to bridge 
exegesis and theology. Within that, we need to understand how the apostles 
and prophets are thinking and how that resolves the tension between what 
the Bible prescribes hermeneutically and what its writers practice.
I contend the answer to these matters revolves around the following 
statement: The prophetic hermeneutic continues into the apostolic herme­
neutic, which is the Christian hermeneutic. We can learn how to study the 
sacred text from what the biblical writers instructed us to do as well as from 
seeing them use Scripture, provided we understand what they were doing.
This is where the quest for authorial logic plays an essential role in 
the discussion. Grasping the biblical writers rationale helps us to see they 
were not hermeneutical hypocrites. Instead, they practiced what they 
preached with immense precision and thereby laid out for us how to read 
Scripture better. The way they read is the way they wrote and the way we 
should read them. By this, their hermeneutic is our hermeneutic.
The rest of the book builds a case that we can and should learn from the 
master interpreters of Scripture, the very writers themselves. Consequently, 
each chapter will answer how the biblical authors were interpreting and 
reasoning as well as how that shapes our hermeneutical practice. (I will, 
though, answer the latter question more thoroughly at the end of the book.)
With this in mind, the next chapter begins our quest for authorial 
logic by establishing important foundational issues in approaching the 
endeavor. Based upon this, the third chapter will discuss the prophetic 
hermeneutic and observe that the Old Testament writers were exegetes 
and theologians in their own right. They carefully interpreted Scripture, 
and via new revelation expounded upon its theological themes and im­
plications. As a result, as opposed to writing “better than they knew,” the 
prophets wrote better than we give them credit for. The fourth chapter 
shows that the prophets intentionally wrote with a trajectory moving to­
ward, and setting up for, the New Testament.
In the fifth chapter, we will observe that the apostles continued the 
logic developed in the Old Testament. They did not change the meaning 
of previous revelation but under the superintendence of the Spirit, fleshed 
out its implications in the current era. In fact, as the sixth chapter points 
out, this interpretative approach is pervasive in the New Testament. The 
apostles followed the logic of their Old Testament counterparts, and as a

--- Page 24 ---
23The Quest for Authorial Logic
result are uniform in the way they handle the same texts. Such hermeneu­
tical consistency is part of the fabric of New Testament theology.
The seventh chapter shows how the hermeneutical continuity between 
the prophets and apostles moves to us. The Christian hermeneutic is no 
different than what the biblical writers engaged in. Their hermeneutical 
practice confirms the traditional exegetical method. Their intertextual 
logic also shows how they developed theology. The biblical writers often 
advanced biblical theological themes by picking up on previous revelation 
and developing certain ideas therein. This gives us a strategy to bridge 
exegesis and theology. By seeing how their writings expounded upon the 
ideas and implications of past revelation, we can see the theological topics 
they discussed and developed.
Consequently, as we think the biblical writers thoughts after them, 
we immerse ourselves in their logic, we read the text the way they read it, 
and their hermeneutical rationale becomes our own. The prophetic and 
apostolic hermeneutic is, thereby, the Christian hermeneutic.
Those versed in the New Testaments use of the Old will see that 
my thesis resonates with Beale, Kaiser, Carson, Hamilton, Caneday, 
and Bock.4 6  That is true. These individuals (and more) have immensely 
shaped my thinking on the topic. Although all of us will deviate from 
each other on some points, these are variations on a theme: The apostles 
used the Old Testament contextually. That is at the core of how we all 
explain the New Testaments use of the Old, and I intend to argue sys­
tematically in this book why that core is justified. However, my goal in 
this is not merely to verify a certain viewpoint of the New Testaments 
use of the Old but to thereby demonstrate that the way we read Scripture 
is thoroughly justified.
Thus, this book uses the New Testaments use of the Old to teach us 
the nature of hermeneutics and interpretation. My mission is to vindicate 
the prophets and apostles and to use them to help shape our own under­
standing of Gods Word. They are not hermeneutical ignoramuses who 
have abused the Scripture. We do not know better than them. Rather, be­
ing moved by the Holy Spirit, they were brilliant—and we ought to hum­
bly follow them. Their faithful hermeneutic provides us the certainty that 
the way we were traditionally taught to interpret the Bible is the method 
the Bible upholds. Literal-grammatical-historical hermeneutics is not a 
modern formulation but how the biblical writers read the Scriptures. The 
Christian hermeneutic follows the prophets and apostles, and is thereby a 
hermeneutic of obedience.

DID JESUS MISUSE SCRIPTURE?:
THE POWER OF PRESUPPOSITIONS
S
ome may wonder why we need this chapter. After all, why can we not 
jump into the analysis of the prophetic and apostolic hermeneutic? 
Perhaps I can illustrate the value of the following discussion in contem­
plating the question, “Did Jesus misuse Scripture?” I suspect most of us 
would react strongly against such an assertion. However, one scholar sug­
gests something along that very line. He discusses how Jesus responds to 
the Sadducees in Luke 20:34-38. In that text, Jesus appeals to Exodus 3:6 
in order to support the resurrection. That scholar questions how can the 
statement “the God of Abraham, the God of Isaac, and the God of Jacob” 
mean there is a resurrection.1  In light of this, he concludes:
Jesuss use of Exodus 3:6 in Luke 20:34-38, to say the least, is not an example 
of grammatical-historical exegesis. Evangelicals tend to protect Jesus and 
the apostles from the charge of engaging in such uncontrolled exegesis. It 
is argued that the New Testament authors employ the Old Testament for 
apologetic purposes. Hence, the logic goes, we can safely say that Jesus and 
the New Testament authors would never have done such wild things with 
the Old Testament if their purpose was to convince others of the gospel. This 
logic is completely misguided.2
1. Enns, Inspiration and Incarnation, 114.
2. Ibid., 132.
25

--- Page 27 ---
26 Groundwork for the Quest
To be clear, this view is extreme and this individual scholar certainly does 
not represent evangelical scholarship. To the contrary, the vast majority of 
evangelicals do not hold to these ideas. Nevertheless, his statements help us 
begin to see the value of presuppositions. After all, in handling his assertion, 
we have to admit the logic within the argument is pretty sound. Was God re­
ally talking about resurrection when he said the words of Exodus 3:6? In that 
regard, this scholar seems to have a point. What then is wrong with the above 
idea? The problem is not with the logic internal to his argument but rather 
the presuppositions upon which the argument is grounded. The above exam­
ple has us (knowingly or unknowingly) accept a series of assumptions which 
inevitably lead to the conclusion Jesus misinterpreted the original intent of 
Scripture.3  This illustrates the power of presuppositions. Certain preconcep­
tions can truly make or break the entire quest for authorial logic.
If you are wondering what those presuppositions are, then you have 
begun to see why we need this chapter.4  This chapter will discuss certain 
foundational issues in the New Testament s use of the Old and affirm key 
presuppositions held by the vast number of evangelical scholars. My goal 
is to articulate these crucial ideas, show why they are biblical, and defend 
them against objections raised by other scholars. In doing so, we make 
clear that we cannot only have the right approach to the quest for authorial 
logic but also a right approach to studying the Bible in general.
FIRST PRESUPPOSITION: AUTHORIAL INTENT
The first foundational issue of the quest is the matter of authorial intent. 
After all, the quest for authorial logic cannot exist (or would be meaning­
less) if the authors intent is not the substance of the Bibles true mean­
ing. Because of their high view of Scripture, evangelicals have tradition­
ally held to authorial intent. This is reflected in the writings of evangelical 
scholars like Ramm and Stein to Kostenberger, Duvall and Hays, as well 
as Vanhoozer.5  They articulate how authorial intent delineates evan­
gelical hermeneutics.6  They also show how it distinguishes evangelical

Presuppositions and Method 21
approaches to the New Testaments use of the Old from postmodern ap­
proaches.7  Thus, authorial intent is a critical and defining presupposition 
for evangelical hermeneutics and the quest for authorial logic.
While some might find the idea obvious and elementary, it has been 
increasingly questioned in recent days, prompting the need for discus­
sion. Even more, while we might presuppose the centrality of authorial 
intent, we also need to make sure that our method is consistent with that 
conviction. If it is not, we cease to seek the author s logic and fail the quest. 
Hence, going through this discussion is important for our hermeneutic in 
general as well as for our approach to the quest for authorial logic.
As just mentioned, some have questioned whether authorial intent 
is valid. Those in the deconstructionist, postmodern camp point out the 
philosophical as well as pragmatic problems of having the reader access 
the author s intent via a text. In essence, a breakdown of communication 
occurs between author, text, and reader. The author cannot remain in con­
trol of the text once he has written it. After all, the author does not travel 
with his texts to clarify or enforce his ideas to the readers. Instead, the text 
disconnects from him and as a result, can no longer exclusively communi­
cate the author s ideas.8  The collapse continues as the reader cannot really 
connect with the text.9  He has his own presuppositions and preconcep­
tions which inevitably are forced upon the text. Thus, the reader can never 
truly “read” a text much less understand the author s intent. He only sees 
what he wants.1 0
If the above is true, the quest for authorial logic is irrational for two 
reasons. First, the quest is impossible since we cannot determine the au­
thors intent or logic. Second, the quest is pointless. We wanted to know 
the writers logic because we were concerned with the “right” interpreta­
tion of Scripture. Conversely, if we can only see what we want to see, then 
our concern with hermeneutics is nonsense. Even if we figured out the 
authors logic, it would change nothing. We would still interpret the Scrip­
ture according to our own fancy anyways.
Such skepticism makes it all the more important to affirm the foun­
dational presupposition of authorial intent. Exhaustively covering this 
subject is outside the scope of this book. Other works have done so.1 1  At

this point, we can summarize their findings by showing how the Scripture 
defines the hermeneutical roles of author, text, and reader.
The doctrines of revelation and inspiration demonstrate that the dual 
authorship of Scripture determines the legitimate meaning of Scripture. 
Second Peter 1:21 reminds us God moved certain men to speak, such 
that their message is actually from God.1 2  Mans words precisely com­
municate Gods own ideas. The way the apostles quote Scripture affirms 
that alone is the meaning of the text. They interchangeably discuss what 
the “prophet” spoke (Rom. 10:16) with what “God spoke through the 
prophet” (Acts 28:25) with what the “Scripture spoke” (Gal. 3:8).1 3  This 
affirms human intent is Gods intent and this unified intent is the legiti­
mate meaning of Scripture.
The Bible also speaks about the nature of the text of Scripture. Titles, 
such as the oracles of God (Rom. 3:2; Heb. 5:12), the Word of God (Lk 8:11; 
John 10:35; Acts 4:31), and the council of God (Ps. 107:11; Prov. 19:21), 
establish the text is inextricably linked with its divine author. For that rea­
son, Paul declares all Scripture is Gods very communication (2 Tim. 3:16). 
The text is not a series of interpretative possibilities or the ideas of the 
reader but the intent of the Lord. That meaning cannot be broken (John 
10:35).1 4  The text is not a blank slate upon which one can impose his ideas 
but fixed to the author s intent.
Along that line, Scripture has much to say about the reader. God con­
demns any twisting or perversion of his Word (Deut. 4:2; 2 Peter 3:16). He 
holds the reader to rightly dividing the Scripture (2 Tim. 2:15). Readers do 
not have hermeneutical freedom but hermeneutical accountability. Fur­
thermore, God empowers his people to rightly understand the Scripture. 
The Spirit, who moved men to write Gods Word, also dwells in the heart of 
believers (2 Peter 1:21; 1  John 2:20). The Author is not disconnected from 
the reader but is in them, and illumines them with wisdom to understand 
the text (cf. 1  Cor. 2:11-16; Eph. 1:15-18). Moreover, believers desire to 
know Gods Word as it is on their hearts (Jer. 31:33).1 5  Thus, Christians are 
not hopelessly lost in their own preconceptions, they are fully able to grasp 
Gods Word rightly and are expected to do so. God charges them to rightly 
understand the author s intent in the Bible.
Thus, in contrast to postmodern skepticism, Scripture shows autho­
rial intent is central. This brief discussion has provided us the under­
pinnings to claim God has spoken through man in his Word and God­
honoring interpretation is when we understand that intent. Failure to do 
so is a result of sin and can lead to severe condemnation from the Lord

Presuppositions and Method 29
(cf. 2 Peter 3:16). The evangelical conviction of authorial intent is justified 
by Gods Word. It is indeed foundational for biblical hermeneutics.
Accordingly, when dealing with how we interpret Scripture, our desire is 
not to discover new meanings but the dual authors intent. Whenever we ap­
proach a biblical text, our aim must not primarily be “what is in it for me?” or 
“what could it mean?” or “what does it mean to me?” but rather “what did the 
author mean?” In addition, this highlights our dependence upon the Spirit 
and the need for sanctification in studying the Scripture. Postmodern skepti­
cism has some truth. We can be distracted when we study the Bible, and sin 
can drown out the intent of the dual author. The solution is not to abandon 
authorial intent but rather to live holy lives and rest upon the Spirits illumi­
nating and sanctifying power (cf. James 1:21; 1  Peter 2:1-2). This is precisely 
why prayer and spiritual preparation are required before coming to the text.
At the same time, if authorial intent is our hermeneutical conviction, 
then our approach to the quest for authorial logic should be consistent with 
that; namely, the author should be the starting point and the center of the 
discussion. That may appear obvious but it is important since modern meth­
odologies differ on how this presupposition works. For example, some would 
suggest the starting point should be on the hermeneutical practices of the 
biblical authors contemporaries.1 6  After all, the biblical writers are influenced 
by their culture and so to better understand them, we ought to study the in­
terpretative methods of their peers. Upon doing so, some scholars point out 
how Judaism at the time of the apostles read the Old Testament text noncon- 
textually. They conclude from this that the apostles did the same.1 7
While that may sound reasonable, the approach is inconsistent with 
our concern for authorial intent. If our fundamental investigation stud­
ies the background of the author as opposed to the author himself, we 
may have compromised our concern to hear what he has to say. Beale and 
Carson rightly warn us against assuming a sort of historical determinism 
whereby the biblical authors are a helpless byproduct of their society.1 8  
After all, the biblical writers claimed they could discern through and even 
resist their cultural norms (cf. Deut. 4:18-21; 18:9; Rom. 12:2; 1  Tim. 1:4; 
Titus 1:14; 1  Peter 4:1-5). Just because the rest of society interprets a text a 
certain way, does not mean the authors of Scripture followed suit.
Similarly, we can fall into the same trap with the various paradigms 
or models. For example, some people appeal to “typology” (or that certain 
Old Testament realities prefigure heightened New Testament counter­
parts) as a paradigm to show how the apostles used the Old Testament.1 9  
That model may be a valid and helpful framework in dealing with the

New Testament s use of the Old. However, does this mean every time the

termine the authors intent rather than observing what he precisely says. 
Kostenberger and Patterson correctly caution us from forcing a model on 
every instance of the New Testament s use of the Old.2 0
Ultimately, these errors revolve around our tendency to jump to conclu­
sions. We may see some initial correlations with the writer s contemporaries 
or a particular framework and then assume the author thought the same 
way. Based upon this, we might condemn their hermeneutic or verify our 
own theological position. However, we may not have factored all the details 
of the prophets or apostles writing. The misconception here is that we be­
lieve we have found the author s logic when we have really substituted a sys­
tem or theory for his intent without making sure he was reasoning that way.
This is not to say models are bad or we should not study the hermeneu­
tics of a biblical writers contemporaries. To the contrary, all of those things 
are helpful. I too have a paradigm of the continuity of prophetic, apostolic, 
and Christian hermeneutic. I will also shortly appeal to the hermeneutics 
of the biblical writers peers. Nevertheless, I am arguing authorial intent 
should be our methodological starting point as opposed to starting with 
those frameworks. As Kostenberger and Patterson remind us, authorial in­
tent is the determining factor in the study of the New Testament s use of the 
Old.2 1  So if we are convinced of authorial intent, we should give the author 
a fair hearing as an individual on his own terms. As we will see, throughout 
the discussion of this chapter as well as the entire book, we will go back to 
the question “but what does the author mean?” and that is precisely what we 
must do. Instead of commencing our investigation from a preset paradigm 
(e.g., typology or contemporary hermeneutics), we need to actually discov­
er what each author claimed in the various situations where they appeal 
to Scripture. That means observing the details of what they say and thus, 
withholding judgment till we have as many of the facts as possible. A com­
mitment to authorial intent demands that our method should be robustly 
inductive. From this, we can formulate certain conclusions and models.
SECOND PRESUPPOSITION:
MEANING AND SIGNIFICANCE
The next presupposition deals with the distinction between meaning and 
significance. This issue arises in dealing with the New Testaments use 
of the Old. What does the term “use” mean? For example, Enns claims 
“use” refers to the matter of interpretation or how an author understood
20. Kostenberger and Patterson, Invitation to Biblical Interpretation, 704-5.
21. Ibid., 705.

--- Page 32 ---
Presuppositions and Method 31
what the text meant.2 2  He likewise assumes when a passage of Scripture 
uses prior revelation, it explicitly provides the interpretation of that text.2 3  
Hence, he would argue Matthews citation of Hosea 11:1 is his view of the 
Old Testament texts meaning. As such, Matthew believed Hosea 11:1 was 
a sort of prophecy predicting Christs departure from Egypt even though 
the original context has no such idea. Based upon this, he concludes Mat­
thew misinterpreted Hosea by reading Christ into the text rather than see­
ing what the original author meant.2 4
In dealing with Erins’  assertion, we need to tackle the presupposition 
behind his conclusion. Does the biblical writer always give his interpreta­
tion whenever he uses a text? Our own “use” of texts illustrates it is not as 
simple as that. For example, we may “use” the Pastoral Epistles to show 
how the church should conduct its affairs. However, technically Paul is 
speaking to Timothy and Titus. He plainly states that (1 Tim. 1:4; Titus 
1:2). If we “use” these texts in our own churches, are we claiming a mean­
ing that Paul explicitly did not intend? Are we adding to his meaning?
I do not believe preachers and teachers who “use” texts in these ways 
grossly misinterpret Gods Word. They know Paul wrote these letters to 
Timothy and Titus. However, they are also aware the apostle intended 
these words to carry implications beyond the immediate situation. This is 
the nature of Scripture (2 Tim. 3:16; Rom. 15:4) and the apostle himself is 
aware of this (Col. 4:16; 1  Thess. 5:27; 2 Tim. 2:2). Hence, we are justified 
in “using” the Pastoral Epistles for our churches even if they were origi­
nally written to Timothy and Titus. Our “use” of these types of texts does 
not nullify the original intent but rather sees its intended ramifications for 
all time. In fact, in order to correctly identify those universal implications, 
one has to understand the author s original ideas.
This discussion illustrates the distinction between meaning and sig­
nificance.2 5  This is a critical presupposition in evangelical scholarship. 
Experts in this area of study, like Beale, Carson, Kaiser, Stein, and Bock 
all discuss the need to factor in the distinction between meaning and 
significance.2 6  In fact, Beale and Kaiser both state that these definitions 
are critical in being careful and nuanced with the way the apostles use 
the Old Testament.2 7  These observations evidence the importance of this

presupposition. To properly and fairly evaluate the hermeneutic of the 
biblical writers, understanding meaning and significance is a must.
With this in mind, we should define the ideas of meaning and signifi­
cance. The former refers to the particular ideas of the original author in the 
text (e.g., Paul wrote to Timothy or Titus). The latter denotes the various 
valid repercussions, inferences, or implications stemming from the authors 
meaning. Significance can include (but is not limited to) the ramifications 
of a texts meaning on our lives today or its bearing on a theological topic. 
Significance is derived from what the author said, logical inferences of those 
ideas, and the assumptions required for those ideas to operate.2 8
Stein helps us further grasp the ideas of meaning and significance. 
He states one can talk about significance in terms of “willed pattern of 
meaning” where an author s ideas set up a framework for grander impli­
cations. Thus, a traffic law may deal with “cars” but the “willed pattern of 
meaning” regards not just cars but any motor vehicle.2 9  One could also 
discuss this in terms of the “letter” of the law versus the “spirit” of the 
law. The letter speaks of what the law technically stipulates but in doing 
so projects a broader idea (the “spirit”), which has wider ramifications 
and applications. One can also talk about the specific idea of a text versus 
the principle implied in a text. Another way to think through this is the 
notion that ideas (meaning) have consequences (significance). Scholars 
classically speak of meaning and significance in terms of a single intent of 
the author but multiple applications of his intent.3 0  All of these illustrate 
the ideas of meaning and significance.
One particularly useful model is found in Vanhoozer s discussion of 
speech-act. Just like other models, speech-act recognizes the distinction 
between meaning (illocution) and significance (perlocution). However, it 
reminds us how the authors intent controls both. To be sure, an action 
can provoke a variety of responses. One can flinch or duck in a variety of

ways at a punch. In the same way, a text could have legitimate relevance on 
a variety of issues moral or theological. Nevertheless, the authors intent 
serves as a control on these responses. The one who punched anticipated 
certain reactions. Likewise, the author wrote meaning to set up for certain 
types of ramifications and implications. His intent governs the parameters 
of these repercussions. Thus, meaning and significance are part of the au­
thors intent. Overall, all the expressions above illustrate how meaning and 
significance exist on a theoretical level.
Meaning and significance though are not just ideas in hermeneutical 
theory. Scripture itself sets up these categories. The biblical writers recog­
nize understanding the ideas within the text are not the same as actually 
living out its implications. Hence, hearing the word (meaning) is distin­
guished from doing it (significance, cf. James 1:21-22).3 1  For this reason, 
the biblical writers advocate reading the Scripture with wisdom (cf. Prov. 
1:2-7; Col. 1:9; James 1:22; 3:17-18).3 2  They desire us to read understand­
ing what Scripture says (meaning) and also how it applies to everyday 
life (significance). Similarly, certain words in Scripture seem to refer to 
notions of meaning and significance. For example, in Hebrew, certain ex­
pressions denote specific commands or judgments (rn^o) of God whereas 
others communicate the ideas of principles or edicts (pn) that are foun­
dational to those particular demands.3 3  The former seems to denote the 
particular ideas in the law (meaning). The latter seems to denote the more 
universal principles or values that show the broader ramifications of a law 
(significance). The above examples indicate meaning and significance are 
notions in biblical thought and hermeneutics. These ideas are important 
and will be significant (pun intended) for the rest of the book.
This brings us back to the main point of this discussion. The reality of 
meaning and significance shapes the way we understand the word “use.” 
Does the biblical writer directly only provide his interpretation of the text s 
meaning every time he “uses” Scripture? Our discussion above shows this 
is not the case. “Use” can refer to how an author interprets the meaning of 
a text as well as how he applies its significance in a variety of ways.
Thus, just because a scriptural author cites another passage does not 
mean he overtly provides his understanding of the texts meaning. That

34 Groundworkfor the Quest
could be the case. It could also be possible his writing reflects a particular 
implication (significance) of a previous text, which presumes a certain in­
terpretation of that passage.3 4  Even more, within the idea of significance, 
the author could be appealing to a variety of implications. People might 
assume the only ‘application’  of an Old Testament text in the New is 
prophecy and fulfillment. While that can occur, the writer could appeal to 
the Old Testament as an analogy (cf. John 3:14), example (1 Cor. 10:1-4; 
Heb. 3:8-19), an authoritative supporting argument (cf. Acts 15:15), or 
as the source for a certain theological idea (Gal. 3:16).3 5  The term “use” 
could encompass any of these ideas (and more). However, since this is the 
case, we ought to avoid the hermeneutical misconception that every time 
a writer cites Scripture, he explicitly provides his interpretation or dis­
cusses a fulfillment of prophecy. Doing so straitjackets the author into our 
own assumptions rather than hearing what he claims. That runs counter to 
the quest to find the biblical writers’  logic and potentially makes us guilty 
of taking them out of context—the very fault we have accused them of.
Equally, when we talk about whether a biblical writer uses a text ac­
cording to the author’ s intent, we should nuance that in terms of meaning 
and significance. We can ask if the biblical writer appealed to an idea in 
the primary argument of prior revelation. We can also ask if the bibli­
cal writer made a valid inference from a prior text. If the answer to any 
of these question is “yes,” then the use of the text is within the original 
author’ s intent because it abides within the text’ s original meaning or its 
intended range of significance. Understanding this will be critical for un­
derstanding how the Old sets up for the New as well as how the New le­
gitimately uses the Old.
In any case, at this point, one may wonder with so many hermeneuti­
cal possibilities, what are we to do? Quite a few scholars have categorized 
the variety of “uses” of the Old Testament in the New to assist in quantify­
ing the author’ s logic.3 6  These efforts are helpful to be sure. However, they 
also pose a danger. As discussed above, we can easily end up forcing an 
author into a specific “use” when his rationale may not exactly match the 
idea. After all, the authors did not have in their minds and write with the 
categories we come up with thousands of years later. They do not have to 
fit our categories. Rather, we need to accurately communicate their rea­
soning. This takes us back to our conviction about authorial intent and 
the central question of the quest for authorial logic. What was the author 
claiming? How was he thinking? This section reminds us a variety of pos­
sibilities exist in how an author “used” a text. We need to hear what they 
say and discuss their logic in terms of meaning and significance.

THIRD PRESUPPOSITION:
REALITY OF INTERTEXTUALITY
In light of all the different “uses” of a text, we need to be able to identify the 
author s pattern of thought. What hints does a writer leave us in the text 
that can clue us into his rationale? This introduces us to another important 
presupposition about the quest: the interconnectedness of Scripture.
In answering these questions, an introductory formula (e.g., “just as it 
is written”) or the argument in context could help us initially understand 
how the author thought.3 7  Examining the context of the alluded passage 
would also aid in this endeavor. However, critics rightly point out such 
information is not always sufficient for us to solidify the authors logic.3 8  
It is too limited and still leaves open a lot of possibilities for how a writer 
may use previous revelation.
Such criticisms though assume that when a writer uses Scripture, 
the only textual factors involved are the text he has written and the text 
he alludes to. We might assume these “two texts” are all we have to work 
with to figure out the writer s logic. However, a host of scholars remind 
us that this is not always true. The reality of intertextuality comes in at 
this point.3 9  No text is an island. Nicole calculates that one in approxi­
mately twenty verses in the New Testament is a quotation from the Old. 
If one factors in not just quotations but also allusions (where a New 
Testament passage sounds similar to the Old), the ratio can go as high 
as one verse out of ten.4 0  Scholars, like Fishbane, House, Broyles, Kaiser, 
and Waltke make similar remarks concerning the Old Testament.4 1  More 
than likely, the author in context has not just made one allusion but oth­
er allusions to other texts. Hence, those other allusions in context could 
provide textual data to help understand the authors rationale. Further­
more, Beale, Carson, and Bateman remind us the specific text the writer 
refers to might be interconnected with other texts which in turn are 
connected with other passages.4 2  Thus, scholars remind us that both the 
various texts the author alludes to in context and the way those texts are 
interconnected with other texts may generate a network of texts. As one 
of my students put it, “everything is connected.”

Thus, as opposed to just having “two texts” to work with, we may have 
far more to understand the writers logic. Moreover, all of these inter­
connected texts may establish a pattern or paradigm for how the author 
thought through a text. He may have followed the way his predecessors 
utilized and expounded upon previous revelation. If this is the case, fig­
uring out the authors logic is far from subjective. Rather;  it is textually 
expressed by the intertextuality in Scripture.
Did the writers of Scripture have this idea of intertextuality in mind?4 3  
Several lines of evidence show they did. First, ancient hermeneutical prac­
tices demonstrate readers long ago combined texts together. For example, 
the principle ofgezerah shevah states “where the same words are applied to 
two separate cases it follows that the same considerations apply to both.”4 4  
The idea is that if two different texts share the same wording, then perhaps 
they are linked. This principle, extant by the first century ad, implies some 
of the earliest readers of Scripture associated texts together.4 5  It was part of 
their methodology.
Second, Jewish writings by the time of the New Testament illustrate 
the ancient readers did not merely link “two texts” together but rather 
wove multiple texts together. For instance, in 1  Enoch, the visions of Isaiah, 
Ezekiel, and Daniel are merged together in describing a heavenly court­
room scene (cf. 1 En. 14; 24-25; 46-48; 71).4 6  Documents at Qumran also 
exhibit the same proclivity to combine texts. llQMelchizedek combines 
a reference to the year of jubilee (Lev. 25) with various Messianic pas­
sages including Isaiah 52:7, 61:2-3, Daniel 9:25, and Psalms 82 and 110.4 7  
4QpIsaa  reads Jeremiah 23:5, Zechariah 3:8, and Isaiah 11:1 together be­
cause they all refer to the term the “branch.”4 8  More examples exist of how 
those in the intertestamental period read texts together.4 9  By appealing to

the rest of the discussion shows) fleshes itself out in in the author s intention and text.

Third, the evidence is not just external to the biblical writers but in­

we should not automatically conclude they did so the same way their con­
temporaries did; nonetheless, we may say they did something similar. For 
example, when scholars look at Ezra 1:1, they note how the verse alludes 
to several texts. Ezra 1:1 matches the wording of 2 Chronicles 36:21.5 0  In 
addition, Ezra 1:1 explicitly mentions how Jeremiahs prophesy is fulfilled 
(cf. Jer. 25:11-12). Hence, that verse also links with the book of Jeremiah. 
Finally, Ezra 1:1 mentions how YHWH “stirs up the spirit of Cyrus”—a 
phrase that occurs multiple times in Isaiah (cf. Isa. 41:2, 25; 44:28; 45:1).

Jeremiah, and Isaiah in a single verse. This illustrates how an Old Testa­

tural texts together. In Nehemiah 9:1-37, the Levites recount all of Israels 
history, which is worded the way Moses, Solomon, and the prophets por­

2 Kings 17:13-18).5 2  The Davidic covenant (2 Sam. 7:5-9) is another ex­
ample of this. The passage seems to allude to a variety of texts including 
the Exodus and wilderness wanderings (2 Sam. 7:5-7), the book of Judges 
(2 Sam. 7:10-11), and the Abrahamic covenant (compare 2 Sam. 7:9 with 
Gen. 12:2).5 3  This too illustrates how the Old Testament writers correlated 
multiple texts together in their own writings.
This also happens in the New Testament. We can point to the famous 
example of Matthews use of Hosea 11:1. Matthews use of Hosea 11:1 is 
in the context of other uses of prior revelation, including Micah 5:2 and 
Jeremiah 31:15. In addition, commentators observe Hosea 11:1 itself al­
ludes back to Exodus 4:22.5 4  Matthew interweaves a variety of texts into 
his argument to reveal his perspective to the reader.5 5
vehicles of thought, and context is largely what gives them meaning. So when a Jewish 
reader saw that the words of a passage expressed another truth if they were read in a 
different, divinely revealed context, he concluded that such a phenomenon could not be 
coincidental; all such intertextual connections—and therefore the fuller or multiple sig­
nifications of the text that those connections brought to mind—must have been in the 
mind of God when he inspired the human author to state those words in the first place.”

A similar pattern appears in Paul. For example, in Romans 9, Paul 
makes multiple allusions to various Old Testament texts in his discussion 
of Gods work amongst Jews and Gentiles. In the short span of six verses, 
he quotes from two different passages in Hosea (2:1,25; cf. 9:24-26), three 
different texts of Isaiah (Isa. 1:9; 10:25; 28:22; cf. Rom. 9:27-29), and per­
haps alludes to Daniel as well (Dan. 5:28; cf. Rom. 9:28). Another example 
is in Romans 9:27-28. There, Paul combines allusions from Isaiah 10:22; 
28:22, with the wording of Hosea 1:10 (Hos. 2:1 [Heb. 2:3]). He grouped 
these texts together because they all share the same language of a remnant 
in Israel.5 6  All of this evidences Paul referred to multiple texts because he 
thought that these texts were intertextually interconnected.
Hebrews provides us a final example of how the biblical writers 
wove multiple scriptural texts together. The writer of Hebrews quotes 
from Psalm 95 in discussing the nature of “rest” (Heb. 4:1-7). In doing 
so, the author ties Psalm 95 with Genesis 2:2 and the concept of Sabbath 
rest (Heb. 4:4). He also links Psalm 95 with the books of Numbers and 
Joshua because all three passages talk of the wilderness wanderings and 
conquest.5 7  5 8  Thus, when the author of Hebrews contemplates Psalm 95, he 
does not merely think of that text but also Genesis, Numbers, and Joshua. 
5 8  He thinks multitextually.
The above provides some indication the ancient readers and writers 
viewed texts with a greater idea of context than we might have initially 
thought. I will provide further examples of this as the book continues; 
nonetheless, this discussion points out the hermeneutical misconception 
of locking ourselves into the “two text” mentality. The author could have 
“two texts” in mind (his own and the text he alludes to). However, he also 
could have many more texts in view as he wrote.
This notion of the interconnectedness of Scripture is nothing new to 
evangelical scholarship. With a high view of Scripture, evangelicals believe 
the Bible is consistent and thereby interconnected and compounding.5 9  
Consequently, as mentioned in the previous chapter, evangelicals have 
rightly cited Dodds observation that Old Testament texts are a window 
into larger biblical theological ideas.6 0  Carson and Bateman remind us of 
how multiple passages set a historical trajectory towards the New Testa­
ment.6 1  In his handbook on the New Testament s use of the Old, Beale cites 
the need to investigate how an Old Testament passage is interconnected 
with others as one deals with the way the apostles used previous revela-

tion.6 2  Their emphasis on the way Scripture is connected reinforces the

presupposition is when dealing with the New Testaments use of the Old. 
Thus, we need to be open to the possibility that there are more than “two 
texts” involved in the discussion.
A NOTE ON METHODOLOGY
In light of the above discussion, how do we detect intertextuality? This is not 
a presupposition as much as a question on method; nevertheless, having this 
as part of our foundation is essential. A helpful way to respond to this ques­
tion is with the illustration of connecting the dots. When teaching childrens 
Sunday school, I encounter three major types of students. One type always 
followed instructions and made the correct connections between all the 
dots. This kind of student produced an impeccable picture (e.g., a picture of 
Abraham nearly sacrificing Isaac). Another group sometimes skipped some 
dots and/or did not correctly connect the dots together. Thus, the picture 
was distorted (e.g., Abraham is missing his hand). The final set of children 
connected the dots they desired their own way and thus the picture was 
made to their own liking (e.g., an airplane instead of Abraham and Isaac).
This illustrates the nature of answering how we trace the author s in­
tertextual rationale. We need to make sure we have all the texts (dots) that 
the biblical writer may have been alluding to. We also need to make sure 
we understand how those texts connect together. The following proposes 
a method to handle both those issues.
How do we make sure we have all the “dots” (texts) involved? This 
is a question of detecting intertextuality. Hays criteria for intertextuality 
is considered standard amongst scholarship.6 3  His list includes the: avail­
ability of the source text, volume of echo (linguistic clarity of the allusion), 
recurrence (did the author repeat the allusion elsewhere?), thematic co­
herence (does it fit contextually with the authors argument?), historical 
plausibility (would the readers have understood it that way?), history of 
interpretation (do other scholars agree?), and satisfaction (does it contrib­
ute well to the writers purposes?).6 4
More specifically, we can implement Hays criteria as follows. I pro­
pose the author would have left some word, phrase, or context that would 
trigger an allusion back to a previous text. One must prove such a word or 
phrase is a legitimate trigger. I call this “linguistic distinctiveness” or how 
a term is unique enough to point to a (set of) texts but at the same time 
does not point to other texts. Overall, we are demonstrating the “trigger”
62. Beale, Handbook, 44.
63. Hays, Echoes of Scripture, 29-32; Beale, Handbook, 32-34; Waltke, Old Testament The­
ology, 126.
64. Hays, Echoes of Scripture, 29-32.

--- Page 41 ---
40 Groundworkfor the Quest
is poignant enough to cause the reader to recall a certain text(s). This is 
called ‘ Volume” in Hays criteria.
If one can prove linguistic distinctiveness, then he must demonstrate 
the trigger is intentional. The reader may recall a previous text but did the 
author desire that? In dealing with this, the logic is similar to C. S. Lewis 
“liar, lunatic, or lord.” Lewis statement refers to how Jesus’ s assertions are 
either false (liar), unintended (lunatic), or true (lord). In the same way, a 
trigger is either blatantly false because the author is ignorant of alluded 
text, merely incidental because the allusion is unintentional even if the 
author knew of the text, or intended because the author willed it. Proving 
intentionality goes back to issues of whether the author knew of the text 
(availability) and whether the context is suggestive of it (thematic coher­
ence, recurrence, satisfaction). Ultimately, one must make the argument 
of linguistic distinctiveness and intentionality to prove a connection be­
tween texts. Even more, he must ensure he has identified all connected 
texts. To put it in terms of the metaphor, we must collect all the dots by 
collating all the texts involved in a given situation.
Collecting dots (texts) is not enough. We still need to connect them. 
This moves us to the second issue posed above. At this point, we run into 
the problem of how the author “used” prior revelation. Within this, we 
should not fall into the hermeneutical fallacy of confusing meaning and 
significance. An author could provide a correct or incorrect interpretation 
of a prior passage. He could also provide a correct or incorrect application 
of a text. We need to walk through those possibilities and express the exact 
relationship he establishes between his text and the alluded text.6 5
We also need to grasp the exact relationship between each text to which 
he refers, connecting each dot (text) together. If an author alluded to mul­
tiple texts, then we need to evaluate his use of each text and how his “use” 
of those texts works together in his argument. If an author appealed to a 
text, which in turn alludes to other texts, our job is to trace how each text 
connects with the other. We can then see how that progression factors into 
the author s intent. I would suggest in this process, we will begin to see how 
the prophetic hermeneutic becomes the apostolic hermeneutic and thus be­
comes our hermeneutic. Nonetheless, at this point, we need to make sure we 
examine each intertextual interaction in light of meaning and significance.
In sum, a practical method exists for identifying and interpreting the 
intertextual connections in Scripture. The need for this arises from the 
reality the Bible is thoroughly interconnected. Our job is to identify and 
interpret all the dots correctly. However, this is not merely so that we can 
study about hermeneutics. Rather, since we are concerned about context 
in studying the Bible, this is a method that helps us ensure we have the 
complete literary context the author desires us to have. “Connecting the 
dots” is what we do in our study of every biblical passage.
65. Beale, “Rejoinder to Steve Moyise,” 158-61.

Presuppositions and Method 41
RETURNING TO JESUS'S USE OF SCRIPTURE:
HOW DO PRESUPPOSITIONS PREDISPOSE?
With the entire above discussion in mind, we can return to examine Jesus s 
use of Scripture. Did Jesus misuse Exodus 3:6 in proving a resurrection? 
Having thought through certain key presuppositions involved in the quest 
for authorial logic, we can now factor them into this specific example.
When making the claim that Jesus misused Exodus 3:6, a key presup­
position involved is that Jesus “uses” the text to provide a direct procla­
mation of a resurrection. The aforementioned scholar questions how the 
words “God of Abraham, Isaac, and Jacob” could mean a resurrection. Af­
ter all, resurrection was not the “topic of conversation” in that text.6 6  How­
ever, as we have seen, that is not the only option. “Use” can encompass not 
only meaning but also significance. Could Jesus be drawing an important 
implication from the Old Testament text?
That is a possibility worth considering for two reasons. First, the in­
troductory formula does not make a claim Jesus is providing the mean­
ing of Exodus 3:6. Jesus asserts Moses showed the existence (é|if|vuaev) 
of the resurrection through the Exodus passage (Luke 20:37). The phrase 
stresses Mosess authority but does not necessarily specify whether or not 
resurrection was Moses’ s primary meaning in Exodus 3:6.6 7  Second, Jesus’ s 
explanation does not claim Exodus 3:6 explicitly meant resurrection. Jesus 
points out that Gods statement “the God of Abraham, Isaac, and Jacob” 
would not be genuine unless he was the God of the living. This is not di­
rect interpretation but implication. Hence, Jesus deals with significance 
and not meaning.
If Jesus appealed to an implication of Exodus 3:6, was that appeal val­
id? This leads to dealing with another faulty presupposition. The scholar 
has us accept that “God of Abraham, Isaac, and Jacob” is a phrase merely 
expressing Gods self-identification to Moses.6 8  In doing so, this scholar 
has us believe only “two texts” are involved: the texts in Luke and Exodus. 
However, such an interpretation may oversimplify what the phrase means 
in context. Jesus’ s statements involve more than the two texts suggested 
above. The very mention of “God of Abraham, Isaac, and Jacob” indicates 
one must go back to Genesis since that book originally talks about the 
patriarchs. Furthermore, Jesus refers to how the patriarchs died which is 
discussed in Genesis (cf. Luke 20:38). Hence, one must examine not just 
Luke and Exodus but also Genesis.

67. Pao and Schnabel, “Luke,” 369. The following phrase “as He says” (J)s Xéyei) asserts a 
comparison between Jesuss claim and what the passage states. Again, such language 
does not explicitly state an interpretation, but rather draws an implication by com­
parison.
