--- Page 43 ---
42 Groundworkfor the Quest
Accordingly, when we look at Genesis, we discover that the phrase 
“the God of” is a covenant declaration. Sarna rightly notes it does not 
merely describe Gods self-identification but consistently deals with how 
God will keep his covenant promises (cf. Gen. 24:12, 48; 26:24; 28:13).6 9  
More specifically, the way the phrase is used communicates Gods loy­
alty to fulfill promises he made with the patriarchs themselves. God not 
only promised to give Abrahams descendants land, seed, and blessing but 
also promised those things to the patriarch himself (Gen. 12:1-3; 13:15). 
Hence, when God declares he is “the God of Abraham” to Isaac, he states 
he will continue his promises to Isaac because of what he promised Abra­
ham (Gen. 26:24). In context, being “the God of Abraham” denotes his 
faithfulness to that patriarch personally.7 0  Accordingly, the phraseology 
denotes how God will fulfill his promises to the patriarchs themselves and 
thus, why he works with subsequent generations.
Consistently Moses describes the deaths of the patriarchs in a way 
that gives hope they would still experience those blessings. Moses de­
scribes Abrahams death as being “gathered to his people” (Gen. 25:8). 
Some have suggested this refers to burial practice.7 1  That may be in part 
but is certainly not the whole of it. For example, Joseph records he will 
lie down with his fathers and then be buried later on (47:30).7 2  In that 
text, the wording of being gathered to the fathers is a separate event from 
burial. Hence, commentators take the language to refer to how the “de­
ceased joins his ancestors in the realm of the dead.”7 3  With that, Moses 
implies the patriarchs are not dead and gone but alive and awaiting the 
future promise (cf. Heb. 11:13).7 4
All of this evidences God was committed for the patriarchs to expe­
rience the fulfillment of his promises. His being “the God of Abraham, 
Isaac, and Jacob” expressed such a determination. This sets up for how 
that phrase is used in Exodus. In context, the statement does not merely 
identify who God is but establishes his covenant loyalty. God remembers 
his covenant with the patriarchs (Ex. 2:24-25) and so initiated a relation­
ship with the current generation.7 5  Gods faithfulness to Israels forefathers 
drives his deliverance to fulfill his covenant promises.
If this is part of the idea of Exodus 3:6, then Jesuss use of that text 
is not misguided. How can God claim he is determined to be faithful to 
the patriarchs—for them personally to experience the fulfillment of his 
promises—if they are forever dead and gone?7 6  Rather, for God to keep

Presuppositions and Method 43
his promises to them requires a resurrection. If there is no resurrection, then 
Gods covenant faithfulness is nullified. Thus, for God to legitimately claim 
to be “the God of Abraham, Isaac, and Jacob” and all that it entails, he must 
be the God of the living and not the dead (Luke 20:38). Jesus further claims 
that “all live to God” which matches how Moses described the deaths of the 
patriarchs.7 7  They do still live on, awaiting the time when God will fulfill all 
things to them. Jesus has followed Mosess logic carefully and has drawn a 
legitimate implication from Exodus 3:6. He uses this to prove those who 
deny the resurrection, deny Gods faithfulness to his covenant. Interestingly 
enough, those of Jesus’ s time period reasoned along these lines as evidenced 
in a variety of their writings.7 8  Our Lords audience was well aware of how he 
was thinking and knew he had “spoken well” (Luke 22:39).
Hence, Jesus’ s logic is quite sound. Such an example illustrates the im­
portance of presuppositions. If we accept certain notions (e.g., “two texts” or 
a lack of distinction between meaning and significance), then it forces us into 
certain conclusions. However, if these assumptions are not valid, then there 
are more factors to consider leading us to an entirely different conclusion.
All of this relates back to the chapter’ s earlier discussion to inductively 
look at authorial intent. As we mentioned before, the foundational issue 
is giving the author a fair hearing and seeking to see all the evidence be­
fore coming to a conclusion.7 9  The question is whether we artificially have 
blocked ourselves from doing so based upon our assumptions. In the case 
of Jesus and Exodus 3:6, removing false preconceptions and the restrictions 
they bring allows us to reach the opposite conclusion of what was asserted 
earlier. Therein lies the power of presuppositions and why this entire discus­
sion is important. Decisions we make at this level shape our entire analysis. 
Hopefully, we have begun to formulate convictions that allow us to give the 
authors of Scripture a fair hearing. In doing so, even in this example, we 
begin to see how Moses contextually developed Gods commitment to the 
patriarchs and how this sets up for our Lord to later use that text. The her­
meneutic of the Old Testament leads to the New Testament.
SYNTHESIS: PRESUPPOSITIONS,  
HERMENEUTICS, AND THE QUEST
This chapter has covered the importance of presuppositions. Although 
seemingly insignificant, our preconceptions can greatly influence our
and experience the land promise if they are in the afterlife? This too demands a 
resurrection.

44 Groundworkfor the Quest
conclusions. We primarily discussed three major presuppositions along 
this line. First, we reinforced our conviction concerning the centrality of 
the author s intent. Second, we highlighted the distinction between mean­
ing and significance. Third, we remembered the reality of intertextuality.
As stated earlier, our goal in this discussion is to see how this shapes 
our understanding of the quest for authorial logic and our own herme­
neutic. Regarding the latter, these presuppositions are not just the presup­
positions to figure out how the New Testament uses the Old but how we 
approach Scripture generally. Fundamentally, if we claim to believe in the 
centrality of authorial intent, our method must be deliberately committed 
to that aim. Hence, when we approach the Scripture, our goal must not 
be to find the theology we desire or to gain a certain positive experience 
from reading the text. We should not be selfish in our reading of Scripture. 
Instead, our fundamental objective is to hear what he has to say through 
the human author. Encouragement and theology will come from this to 
be sure, but it will only come from what God said if we actually listen to 
his Word and not our own desires imposed on the passage. A conviction 
about authorial intent makes us those who discover the intent of the text, 
as opposed to those who place demand upon it.
Similarly, we should also factor in the distinction between meaning 
and significance. On one hand, we need to identify what the author meant; 
on the other hand, we need to identify the ramifications that has on our 
life. Exegesis is necessary, but exegesis without application is deficient. 
Every text demands a response and so every text is applicable in some 
fashion. We just need to make sure the implications we draw from a text 
cohere with the original meaning of the text. Put differently, would the 
author agree that our application of the text really stems from his intent? 
The discussion of meaning and significance helps us to be more conscious 
hearers and doers of the Word.
The presupposition of intertextuality reminds us how far context 
goes. “Context is king.”8 0  The question we have encountered in this chap­
ter is whether our understanding of context is thorough enough. Have we 
bought into the misconception that every text is an island? Have we been 
rigorously examining the potential intertextuality of the text? Have we re­
ally connected all the dots together correctly? If context is so essential, it is 
incumbent in our daily study of Scripture to make sure we are cognizant of 
every layer of context the author has woven together for us. In these ways, 
I hope the presuppositions listed above make us more faithful students of 
Gods Word in our everyday Bible study.
However, this book is equally about the quest for authorial logic as it 
is about hermeneutics in general. I am not suggesting in this chapter the 
author always reasons multitextually or every time he “uses” a text he ap­
peals to its significance as opposed to meaning. Rather, my goal is to defend
80. Cf. Stein, A Basic Guide to Interpreting the Bible, 158.

--- Page 46 ---
Presuppositions and Method 45
key evangelical presuppositions that guard us against putting illegitimate 
restrictions on the author. We have seen how assumptions can actually 
cause us to come to opposite conclusions. Hence, having the right founda­
tion, method, and terminology paves the way for us to thoroughly examine 
how the authors of Scripture were hermeneutically reasoning. Our job is to 
‘connect all the dots correctly” so that we can see how the author thought 
through the Scriptures. As we have observed, such a method already indi­
cates the legitimacy of the connection between prophetic hermeneutic and 
the apostolic hermeneutic. With this in mind, let the quest begin.

THE PROPHETIC HERMENEUTIC
T H E  PROPHET AS EXEGETE AND THEOLOGIAN
A
s we begin to ‘connect the dots,” the quest for authorial logic turns 
to the hermeneutical practices of the prophets. Within this, we en­
counter a variety of perspectives regarding who the Old Testaments writ­
ers were and how they thought and wrote. From the viewpoint of liberal 
scholarship, the prophets were people who composed bits and pieces of 
Israels religious traditions.1  Others think of them as those who merely 
recorded Gods Word as he dictated it to them.2  To others, they were some 
sort of historians.3  Perhaps to some of us, the Old Testament writers were 
people who wrote some interesting stories, good poems, and confusing 
prophecies that are full of moral lessons.
So who are the prophets? This question again takes us back to the cen­
tral issue of the quest: authorial intent. I would argue when we consider 
the factors mentioned in the last chapter, a different picture emerges than 
the ones listed above. Hamilton, Rydelnik, and Beale have identified this 
“different picture” of the prophets in their own studies of Old Testament 
intertextuality. In essence, they contend the prophets were scholars of 
Scripture.4  This chapter intends to support that general thesis. In my own 
words, the intertextuality of the Old Testament demonstrates the prophets 
are exegetes and theologians.

48 The Prophetic Hermeneutic
Admittedly, these authors are a “motley crew,” from a herdsman (e.g., 
Amos) to people in the royal court (e.g., Isaiah). Despite such diversity, these 
people were united in how they thought about Gods Word. They knew what 
God had revealed and showed care in handling the scriptural texts. They 
were good exegetes. Furthermore, they were aware of the theological impli­
cations of past revelation and developed those concepts via new revelation 
in ways consistent with the original intent. The Old Testament writers were 
also therefore theologians. Thus, the prophets were not simplistic in their 
thinking and writing. Rather, being guided by the Holy Spirit, they were 
precise and sophisticated. This chapter aims to establish this concept of “ ex- 
egete and theologian” as the core of the prophets rationale.
A WORD ON THE NATURE OF  
OLD TESTAMENT INTERTEXTUALITY
Before examining the intertextual interactions within the first canon, we 
need to make a necessary observation about the nature of intertextuality 
in the Old Testament. The previous chapter has already discussed the ex­
istence of intertextuality in the Old Testament. That being said, Old Testa­
ment intertextuality at times can work differently than what we might be 
familiar with, different than how we have seen intertextuality appear in 
the New Testament. The apostles frequently provide introductory formu­
lae in quoting the Old (e.g., Matt. 2:15; Acts 15:15; Rom. 3:10-18; 10:16). 
Even without an introductory clause, they at times still quote clear seg­
ments of texts (e.g., Rom. 3:20; 1  Cor. 2:16; 1  Peter 3:10-12). These are the 
surest instances of intertextuality. However, these are not the only ways 
intertextuality occurs. Even in the New Testament, the apostles make allu­
sions without the use of an introductory formulae or a citation.5  We may 
not be initially as familiar with this but it is nonetheless present.
Being aware of this helps us to understand Old Testament intertex­
tuality. To be sure, the prophets can employ substantial quotations or in­
troductory formulae (e.g., Jer. 26:18; Joel 2:32). Nonetheless, less explicit 
intertextuality also occurs in the Old Testament. For example, Proverbs 
commands its readers to bind wisdom to their necks (3:3; 6:21). Although 
those verses do not have an introductory formula, most recognize the 
verses allude to where Moses commands Israel to bind the law to them­
selves (Deut. 6:8).6  Similarly, the writer of Kings describes how Elijah goes 
down to Mount Sinai for forty days and nights and experiences Gods 
presence (1 Kings 19:8-13). Though the writer does not blatantly compare 
Elijahs experience with Moses’ s time on Mount Sinai, scholars recognize 
he intended an association.7  References to Gods lovingkindnesss ("ion) in
5. Beale and Carson, “Introduction,” xxiii-xxv; Hays, Echoes of Scripture, 28-33.
6. Garrett, Proverbs, Ecclesiastes, Song of Songs, 14:80.
7. Broyles, “Traditions, Intertextuality, and Canon,” 169-70.

34:6-8.8  In those passages (Ps. 86:16; 103:8; 136:1; 145:8), the psalmist 
does not provide a formal quotation, but the link is still present.
Accordingly, the prophets use unique words, phrases, motifs, concepts, 
places, and events to generate allusions. These less overt appeals are still part 
of how the prophets interfaced with past revelation. Thus, scholars are cor­
rect in saying intertextuality is pervasive in the Old Testament; we just need 
to know how it works.9  The prophets do not always openly declare to us they 
are alluding to a text. Sometimes they just embed the allusion in their writ­
ings and assume we will pick it up. This goes back to our discussion in the 
previous chapter about finding those linguistic and contextual similarities 
to make a case that the writer alluded to previous revelation.
In addition, chronology is an important consideration when dealing 
with Old Testament intertextuality. This goes back to the central notion of 
authorial intent and Hays criteria of availability. The human author would 
not have access to revelation that came after him. Hence, understanding 
the dating of biblical books is important. It ensures we observe how an au­
thor develops revelation antecedent to him as opposed to subsequent. This 
is in part what Kaiser reminds us in his principle of antecedent theology.1 0

the intent of the writer or those that he has selected to reflect his perspec­
tive (e.g., a certain individual in a narrative) to determine the prophetic her­
meneutic. A character within a story may misuse revelation (cf. Gen. 3:1, 
4); however, that does not necessarily mean the author failed to understand

Davids time, how could he refer to the revelation found in that book? The answer to 
this is threefold. First, at bare minimum, biblical writers might have had access to an 
accurate tradition that became part of the biblical books later cited. In this way, we can 
say they cited from that “book” (even if it was using an earlier form). Second, one might 
also argue that along with the previous point, God reminded and revealed the neces­
sary information to that writer. Third, more likely (which effectively makes the first two 
points moot), the historical books were composed close to the time of the events they 
describe. As such, biblical writers could refer to those books for they were (to some 
degree) present at their time. For this idea, see Rooker, “The Book of Judges,” 288-94.

historical books, and the standard conservative dating for Isaiah and Daniel.

THE PROPHET'S KNOWLEDGE OF SCRIPTURE
The first step in understanding the prophets and their hermeneutic is to 
establish they really knew the Scriptures well. After all, if every Old Testa­
ment author wrote without any knowledge of the Bible, then it would be 
foolish to talk about any sort of “hermeneutic” or theological complexity. 
However, both deductive and inductive evidence point to the reality the 
prophets grappled with prior revelation and saturated their own writings 
with their thinking about past revelation.
On a deductive level, the prophets proclaim the Scriptures' authority 
as well as their delight in it. Moses requires careful attention to preserving 
the truths of the law (cf. Deut. 4:1-2).1 3  He reminds the people to submit 
to Gods decrees. He also proclaims the devastating results of disobedi­
ence (Deut. 27:11-26). He demands people read the Law regularly, which 
would have the effect of making them knowledgeable about Gods Word 
(e.g., Deut. 17:18-20; 31:10-13). The author of the Pentateuch thereby re­
tains a high view of Gods revelation.
The rest of the Old Testament maintains this attitude. Joshua echoes 
this same sentiment. Israel is to meditate on Gods Word day and night 
(Josh. 1:8). Psalms written by David and Asaph attest to how they view 
the sacred writings as glorious and how they contemplate Gods Word (Ps. 
19:7-14; 78:1-5). Isaiah records that the faithful will consult Gods Word 
and tremble before it (Isa. 8:16-20; 66:2). Many other prophets exhibit 
their high view of Scripture when they lament over Israels disobedience 
to Gods law (Ezek. 16:1-58; Lam. 4:13; Amos 1:1-2:16; Hab. 1:3-4). The 
writer of Chronicles looks favorably on individuals like Josiah who desired 
for people to obey the law (2 Chron. 34:33; 35:1-25). In the exile, Dan­
iel thinks upon the book of Jeremiah (Dan. 9:1-3). After the Jews return 
to the land, the author of Ezra positively recounts Ezras determination 
to study, live, and teach the law of God (Ezra 7:10). Likewise, the writ­
er of Nehemiah applauds Israels efforts to understand and obey the law 
(cf. Neh. 8:13-18). He also condemns the nation for failing to do so fully 
(Neh. 13:14-31). From the time of Moses to the end of Old Testament his­
tory, the prophets uphold a high view of Scripture, claim they think upon 
it deeply, and applaud those who follow in their footsteps.
Based upon this evidence, it is a little strange for the prophets (or the 
apostles who share the same convictions for that matter) to have this out­
look and then turn around, be unaware of the Old Testament, write without
12. Osborne, Hermeneutical Spiral, 163.
13. Merrill, Deuteronomy, 114; Craigie, Deuteronomy, 130.

--- Page 52 ---
51The Prophet as Exegete and Theologian
any biblical theological perception, and twist what little they know. Would 
they really be ignorant of what they championed and commanded others 
to know? Would they have a shallow understanding of what they deeply 
mediated upon? Would they distort what they condemned others for dis­
obeying? There is a massive inconsistency in our own logic if we would 
suggest any of the above.
Rather, the above discussion should incline us to view the prophets 
as people who cared about how Gods Word was treated. The examples 
above show they were passionate about Gods Word (cf. Ezra 9:5-15). 
Consequently, their zeal for the Scripture should also incline us to see that 
when the prophets discuss events or concepts echoing prior revelation, 
they were aware of that passage and were appealing to it.1 4  They deeply 
meditated upon the Scripture and it inevitably influenced their writings.
This deductive reasoning bears out when we examine the Old Testa­
ment. The prophets knew the Scriptures. The following is just a brief survey 
of this. I do not intend to come close to being exhaustive. That would go 
beyond a chapter and would require tomes. Other works attempt to more 
comprehensively trace the connections within the Old Testament and I 
would refer you to them.1 5  Rather, my goal is to introduce the reality that 
every book of the Old Testament alludes to other revelation. This brief pre­
sentation, along with the work of other scholars, should indicate to us the 
prophets were much more knowledgeable than we may give them credit for.
The Pentateuch contains numerous connections within itself. This 
includes allusions to the Sabbath (Ex. 20:8; cf. Gen. 2:1-2), the promises 
to the patriarchs (Ex. 2:24; cf. Gen. 12:1-3), creation (Ex. 20:11; cf. Gen. 
1:1-31), Eden (Lev. 26:12; cf. Gen. 3:8), and Gods dominion (Gen. 1:26-28; 
cf. Num. 24:19). Joshua refers to Gods law (Josh. 1:8) as well as to Gods 
promises to past patriarchs (cf. Josh. 24:1-4). Judges fleshes out the nature 
of covenant disobedience and punishment found in Leviticus 26:19-25 and 
Deuteronomy 28:23-25. Ruth extends the genealogies of Genesis in the
14. Contra Broyles, “Traditions, Intertextuality, and Canon,” 158-59. Some scholars will 
argue that Israel did not appeal to the scriptural text itself but rather traditions about 
certain events, practices, and people. They refer not to the text but to the traditions 
within Israel. While we ought to be sensitive to this concern, several qualifications 
arise. First, the linguistic similarities between scriptural passages make it unlikely 
that a scriptural writer appealed to a separate tradition as opposed to the text itself. 
Second, if biblical authors claimed to meditate upon the Scripture, why would we 
not expect them to quote it as opposed to an external tradition? Third, this model 
assumes that Scripture (contrary to the prophets’ own claim) was not available until 
much later in Israel’s history. This is not the case. Hence, while Old Testament writers 
can refer to events or practices apart from a text, there is reason to believe that they 
would actually appeal to the written record.

prior revelation. In fact, that book summarizes the entire scope of Old 
Testament history leading up to the postexilic period (Neh. 9:2-37). This 
passage contains the wording and perspective of other texts that recount 
Israels past (cf. Ps. 78:1-72). These connections indicate Old Testament 
historical literature is not simply history or a collection of stories but rath­
er, an expression of how past revelation works out in the world.
Wisdom literature is also replete with Old Testament allusions. 
Psalms makes frequent allusions to Gods law (Ps. 81:3; cf. Lev. 23:24) as 
well as Gods promises to his people (Ps. 67:1; cf. Num. 6:25). Certain po­

114:1-8). They heavily deal with the Davidic covenant (Pss. 72:1-20;

20:10; cf. Deut. 6:8; 25:13 respectively) as does Ecclesiastes (Eccl. 5:2; cf.

show that the Song actually relates to Genesis.2 0  Others have pointed out 
connections between Psalms and Job (Job 7:17; cf. Ps. 8:4). Accordingly, 
wisdom literature is intertextual.

In like manner, the major and minor prophets refer to past revela­
tion. Most of prophetic literature explicitly alludes to various laws in the 
Pentateuch (Isa. 1:17; Jer. 3:1; Ezek. 8:9-10; cf. Ex. 22:22; Deut. 24:1-4; 
Lev. 11:29-31, respectively). On top of this, Isaiah makes allusions to the 
first Exodus in light of presenting a new and ultimate one to come (Isa. 
43:18-19; cf. Ex. 14:1-31). Jeremiah alludes to Isaiahs depiction of the 
final king (Jer. 23:5; cf. Isa. 4:2) as well as develops Mosess understand­
ing of a new covenant to come (Jer. 31:31-33; cf. Deut. 30:1-6). Ezekiel 
refers to that same expectation (Ezek. 36:26-28; cf. Deut. 30:1-6) as well 
as the growing concept of God as Shepherd throughout the Old Testament

cf. Ezek. 40-48). Joel appeals to Obadiah (Joel 2:32 [Heb. 3:5]; cf. Obad. 1:17) 
and makes an allusion to Gods name revealed in Exodus (Joel 2:13: cf. Ex. 
34:6-8). Jonah echoes Joel and Exodus along that same line (Jonah 4:2; cf. Ex. 
34:6-8; Joel 2:13).2 2  Nahum echoes Jonah (Nah. 1:3; cf. Jonah 4:2) and alludes 
to the ‘good news” found in Isaiah (Nah. 1:15; cf. Isa. 40:9). Micah (4:1-5) 
and Amos (9:11-15) reference the Davidic covenant (2 Sam. 7:11-14) to 
show its ultimate fulfillment. In the process, some of these prophets even 
recall key descriptions of Solomons kingdom (cf. 1  Kings 4:25-5:4; Amos 
9:11-15; Mic. 4:1-5). Habakkuk refers to Deuteronomy and Psalms (Hab. 
3:1-8; cf. Deut. 32:2-3; Ps. 18:12; 114:3) as well as alludes to Abrahams faith 
mentioned in Gen. 15:6 (cf. Hab. 2:4).2 3  Zephaniah makes reference to other 
prophetic texts of judgment (Zeph. 1:3; cf. Isa. 6:10; Jer. 4:25; 9:10; Ezek. 7:19). 
Haggai alludes to specific covenantal curses (Hag. 1:6; cf. Deut. 28:38-40) as 
well as messianic promises (Hag. 2:21-23; cf. Isa. 42:1; 43:10). Zechariah, the 
book of God remembering, is replete with intertextuality including allusions 
to Daniel (Zech. 1:17; cf. Dan. 7:1-8; 8:1-12), Isaiah (Zech. 6:12; cf. Isa. 4:2; 
11:1), and Ezekiel (Zech. 2:2; cf. Ezek. 40:3). Hosea and Malachi recall Israels 
origins from Genesis (Hos. 12:12; Mai. 1:2; cf. Gen. 28:5; 25:23 respectively) 
and the latter book recalls Mosess and Elijahs work with a view toward a 
future messenger (Mai. 4:4-6; cf. Deut. 18:15; 34:10-12; 1  Kings 19:8-13).
Again, this is by no means a complete list of all the intertextuality 
within the Old Testament (actually, far from it). However, it does illustrate 
every Old Testament book connects with prior revelation, often times in 
multiple ways. This should begin to reformulate our perception of the 
Old Testament authors. The above evidence shows the prophets were not

The reality that the prophets frequently make allusions to earlier revela­
tion sets the foundation for us to discuss the prophets rationale. In the pre­
vious chapter, I suggested they were not thinking merely of a single passage 
but rather often thought through multiple texts. The above analysis supports 
this idea. It not only illustrates the prophets were aware of and alluded to nu­
merous texts but also that such a mentality is not isolated to a few writers.2 4  
Rather, the list above preliminarily indicates the vast majority of Old Testa­
ment authors were immersed and thoroughly influenced by Scripture. Ac­
cordingly, their understanding of previous revelation may not be as shallow 
and their writings not as simple as some might think.2 5  Some may have been 
herdsmen (e.g., Amos) or shepherds (e.g., David) but that does not mean 
they were ignorant and dumb.2 6  The observations above begin to show that 
the prophetic hermeneutic is not only pervasive throughout the Old Testa­
ment but also contains far more erudition than one may have first imagined.
THE PROPHET AS EXEGETE
Having observed various allusions to previous revelation, we can ask how 
the prophets “used” it. As the previous chapter mentioned, this revolves 
around the issues of meaning and significance. The former category is the 
focus of this section. My goal here is to show the prophets do not change 
the meaning of previous revelation when they discuss it. Just as exegesis 
fundamentally endeavors to articulate the author s intent, so the prophets 
show care in how they interpret and communicate the Scripture. As we 
will see, their writings reflect such accuracy on multiple levels.
Main Ideas
The prophets do not merely make allusions to individual verses, 
phrases, or even words, but also to the main ideas of large sections of 
texts. When they do, our inquiry is whether or not they articulate those
24. In addition, this indicates if the biblical writers used verbiage found in biblical litera­

from the text or a tradition separate from the text. Since their minds are saturated 
in Scripture, it makes sense for them to allude to the text as opposed to something 
outside of it.

concepts rightly. Do they uphold the original meaning of a text, or do they 
alter the ideas of previous revelation?
One example that evidences the prophets hermeneutical accuracy 
concerns their handling of Gods covenant promises. Three core promises 
within the Abrahamic covenant are land, seed, and blessing (Gen. 12:1-3; 
13:14-18; 18:18; 22:16-18).2 7  Israel would inherit a certain region of land 
(Gen. 15:18-21), become numerous and even the progenitor of the ultimate 
king (Gen. 17:6), and thereby facilitate blessing for all the nations (Gen. 
22:16-18).2 8  Do the prophets paint the same picture or something different?
Moses paints this picture. He bases Israels conquest of the land on the 
promise of land (Deut. 9:5) and acknowledges that God has maintained his 
promise of making Israel a great nation per the covenant made with Abra­
ham (Deut. 1:10-11; cf. Gen. 15:5).2 9  Joshua maintains that paradigm. He 
affirms the conquest of the land was part of Gods effort to fulfill his covenant 
promises (Josh. 24:1-14). The prophetic writers of Samuel and Kings also up­
hold the Abrahamic promises. They describe Israel as numerous as the sand 
(1 Kings 4:20; cf. Gen. 22:17) alluding to the seed promise. They note how, 
in the days of David and Solomon, the territorial boundaries of Israel essen­
tially match what was specified to Abraham in Genesis (2 Sam. 8:3; cf. Gen. 
13:14-18). They also stress how nations come to bless God (1 Kings 10:9), 
which was part of the purpose of the Abrahamic covenant (Gen. 12:2-3). The 
prophetic writers of Samuel and Kings understood the Abrahamic covenant 
promises in a fashion consistent with what was originally articulated.
Later prophets also discuss the covenant promises with hermeneuti­
cal accuracy. Isaiah proclaims that Gentiles will come to the land of Israel 
and Jerusalem to learn Gods law (Isa. 2:2-4). The world will be blessed 
as the curse is removed and creation returns to its originally blessed state 
(11:1-10). Micah similarly portrays when the nations are blessed and Is­
rael becomes the great nation it was promised to be (Mic. 4:1-6). Inter­
estingly enough, he even uses imagery found in 1  Kings, which depicted 
how Gods promises flourished in Solomons reign (Mic. 4:4; cf. 1  Kings 
4:25). Thus, Micah not only maintains the same idea found in Genesis but 
also what has been developed throughout the canon. Ezekiel likewise en­
visions a period (Ezek. 36-48) when the Gentiles will be blessed (cf. 39:21) 
and when Israel will dwell in the land (47:13-48:35; cf. Gen. 15:18-21).3 0  
Jeremiah and Amos affirm Israel will be settled in this land to never be 
uprooted again (Jer. 32:41; Amos 9:14). Various biblical writers envision 
an ultimate king who reigns at this time uncontested, in order that the na­
tions would be blessed (Ps. 72:3-17; cf. Gen. 12:1-3). That affirms the spe­
cific nuance of the seed promise that kings will come from Abrahams line

(cf. Gen. 17:6). In all of these cases, we can observe that when the prophets 
allude to the Abrahamic promises, they do not depart from the original 
ideas of those guarantees but only give further description to what they 
will look like when fulfilled. This already hints at the prophets' ability to 
develop theology (see below for further discussion); nevertheless, for this 
discussion, these examples show the Old Testament writers were aware of 
and understood the covenant promises as they were originally presented.
However, the prophets were not merely consistent and accurate in their 
understanding of the Abrahamic covenant but also the law and covenant 
curse. Various passages in the Pentateuch condemn idolatry (Ex. 20:4; Lev. 
19:4; 26:1; Deut. 4:25). Moses enforces this in the Pentateuch itself when 
Israel worships a golden calf (Ex. 32:7-35) or engages in syncretistic prac­
tices at Baal Peor (Num. 25:1-11). The prophets maintain this perspective 
on idolatry throughout the entire canon. Joshua warns the people against 
turning to other gods because the Lord is a jealous God (Josh. 24:19-20). In 
the period of the Judges, Israels idolatry leads to punishment exactly as God 
had warned (Judg. 2:12-15). In the prophets portrayal of Saul, David, and 
Solomon, idolatry was condemned and in fact was part of the reason for the 
splitting of the nation (1 Kings 11:1-13). Furthermore, the kings in Israel 
and Judah are faulted for their adherence to idol worship (1 Kings 16:13; 
2 Kings 17:2) and for not removing the high places (1 Kings 15:4; 22:43; 
2 Kings 12:3; 15:4). The prophets, both “major” and “minor,” confront the 
nation along this line (cf. Isa. 44:1-20; Ezek. 5:11; Hos. 4:12; Zeph. 1:4-5). 
During the exile, Daniel confesses Israels sin in these areas (Dan. 9:4-6). 
Even after the exile, books like Nehemiah and Ezra also acknowledge the 
nations wrong doing in this matter and warn against falling back into such 
disobedience (cf. Ezra 9:8; Neh. 9:16-29). The prophets do not change the 
meaning of the law as they apply it. They maintain its original intent.
In addition, the prophets view history in the paradigm established by 
Leviticus 26:1-46 and Deuteronomy 28:1-68. Those passages discuss how 
covenant disobedience leads to covenant curse. The prophets describe 
their circumstances and Israels overarching history consistently from 
that viewpoint. In the book of Judges, Israels failure to honor God results 
in nations oppressing Israel (Judg. 2:12-13) just as Moses warned (Deut. 
28:25). Throughout the book of Kings, we can observe the implementation 
of covenant curses whether that be in the specific examples of drought 
(1 Kings 17:1; cf. Lev. 26:19), famine (Ruth 1:1-6; cf. Deut. 28:23-24), siege 
(2 Kings 6:24-25; cf. Deut. 28:52), or even eating ones children (2 Kings 
6:28-29; cf. Deut. 28:56). These descriptions are not merely coincidental 
or just a record of history. Rather, the narrator of Kings explicitly states 
in 2 Kings 17:7-18 that all of this was part of Gods judgment against his 
people for their disobedience (2 Kings 17:6, 20). He further declares their 
wickedness ultimately led to the northern kingdoms exile, the final curse 
mentioned in the Mosaic covenant (2 Kings 17:23; cf. Deut. 28:64-65). 
Thus, the author of Kings views Israels history through the paradigm of

57The Prophet as Exegete and Theologian
Leviticus and Deuteronomy. In addition, Isaiah and Joel appeal to Deu­
teronomy 28 in discussing the nations demise (Isa. 1:5-8; Joel 1:1-20). 
Daniel (9:4-13), Asaph (Ps. 78:1-72), other psalmists (Pss. 105:1-106:48), 
and the writer of Nehemiah (Neh. 9:11-21) all adopt this rationale. The 
way Moses describes how history will work in Leviticus and Deuteronomy 
is the exact same perspective of the prophets. That evidences that various 
prophets precisely understood Leviticus 26 and Deuteronomy 28.
That latter point is significant. The entire flow of Israels story of idolatry, 
condemnation, curse, and exile is predicated upon a consistent interpreta­
tion of the law. Scholars recognize this underpinning hermeneutical coher­
ence. In fact, they view Joshua-Kings as Deuteronomic history, which partly 
entails how the theology of Deuteronomy works out in Israels history.3 1  By 
definition, this presumes the writers of Joshua-Kings looked at Israels his­
tory with the same interpretation of Deuteronomy.3 2  The very fact we do not 
have other interpretations of Israels history in the Old Testament testifies 
to the prophets identical understanding of the Mosaic covenant. Thus, the 
unity of redemptive history presumes hermeneutical unity as well. To a large 
degree, the above example illustrates how the cohesiveness of the Old Testa­
ment depends upon the hermeneutical accuracy of the prophets.
Other examples show how the prophets maintain the same general 
idea of a large passage of Scripture. The prophets use of the creation mo­
tif from Genesis 1:1-2:3 is a good illustration. Within the Pentateuch it­
self, Moses remains consistent with what he wrote earlier.3 3  He records 
that God made man (Deut. 4:32) in his image (Gen. 9:6) just as he earlier 
penned (Gen. 1:26-28). The fourth commandment also uses the creation 
story by showing that just as Gods creative work lasted for six days and 
then he rested, so the Israelites should also rest at the end of their work 
weeks (cf. Ex. 20:8-11; cf. Gen. 2:2-4). Such a use of prior revelation does 
not make any change to what was originally stated.
Beyond Moses, other prophets also maintain the Pentateuch’ s per­
spective about creation. David discusses how God made man to rule over 
the animals (cf. Ps. 8:4-9) precisely the way Genesis 1:26-28 describes. In 
fact, some of the same terminology is used by both texts (compare Ps. 8:8; 
with Gen. 1:28). Psalm 33 describes how God created by his Word (Ps. 
33:6), which is exactly as Moses describes in Genesis 1:3.3 4  Psalm 104 also 
addresses the notion of creation. Structurally, the poem closely follows

simply to show Daniels hermeneutical consistency. The ordering of his 
vision evidences a very tight correlation with the creation account. Even 
more, if his allusions portray how God was going to restore original cre­
ation per Genesis 1-2, then Daniel inherently upholds the original intent

of that Old Testament text. Thus, from Pentateuch to prophets and wis­
dom literature, the Old Testament writers maintain the same ideas about 
creation garnered from Genesis 1-2.
We can appeal to Nehemiah 8:8 as a final example of hermeneutical fi­
delity. In that text, Ezra reads from the law of God in such a way to provide 
the people the correct sense of the law. Ezra and the Levites did not view 
their job as to invent new meanings but rather to help the people compre­
hend what the text meant.4 1  The people s response reinforces the notion of 
hermeneutical accuracy. They wept (Neh. 8:9) presumably because they 
knew they had disobeyed the law.4 2  Furthermore, they kept the feast of 
booths to the “letter of the law.” That indicates not only their change of 
heart but also the precision of their teachers (cf. Neh. 8:14-18).4 3  This re­
flects a concern with upholding the meaning of the Old Testament.
The above examples are cases when the prophet alludes to concepts or 
ideas that encompass a large section of text or multiple texts. The proph­
ets allusions can refer to particular verses or phrases from previous rev­
elation. These examples demonstrate the prophets do not change or re­
verse the overarching ideas of those texts. Instead, they remain consistent 
with the meaning originally established. These are not isolated incidents; 
rather, many of the examples above span the entire canon. The collective 
sampling reflects the prophets as a whole share the concern for exegetical 
accuracy. Moreover, the Old Testament at times depends upon such her­
meneutical consistency so as to demonstrate the progression of redemp­
tive history. The flow of Israels story presumes that the prophets do not 
change the meaning of the text but rather adhere to it. The prophets, in 
this way, are exegetes who have thought through Scripture in order to af­
firm it, rather than alter it.
Details
The prophets do not merely understand and maintain the general 
ideas of texts but also pay attention to particular details of those passages. 
The above examples already begin to evidence their hermeneutical preci­
sion in dealing with the details. For example, the way Israel kept the feast 
of booths in Nehemiah 8:14-18 indicates how they abided by the details 
of that law. The people celebrated the feast on the correct days (Neh. 14:14; 
cf. Lev. 23:34), with the correct procedure (Neh. 14:15; cf. Lev. 23:40), and 
even with the correct attitude (Neh. 8:10; cf. Deut. 16:13-15). Hence, 
Nehemiah 8 not only provides evidence of how they understood Gods 
law on a general level but also its particulars. Likewise, the way specific 
curses were implemented in Israels history evidence the prophets rec­
ognized how God acted to keep his warnings to the most precise degree

42. Ibid., 227.

(2 Kings 6:24-29; cf. Deut. 28:23-52).4 4  Even the examples of creation 
show an attention to detail in how later writings even mirror the order or 
structure of the Genesis account (Dan. 7:1-13; cf. Gen. 1:1-28). Put differ­
ently: The prophets accuracy in handling the general ideas of a passage of­
ten entails that they also had accuracy in handling the details. This already 
demonstrates that the prophets were not merely accurate in the overarch­
ing ideas of past revelation but also in the words and phrases involved.
We can provide additional examples to demonstrate the prophets paid 
attention to the details of past revelation. The writer of Joshua reflects the 
conquest illustrates Gods faithfulness. As opposed to any of his prom­
ises faltering, “not one word of all the good promises” has failed (Josh. 
21:45). Commentators note this summary statement encompasses how 
God has upheld the individual details of each of his promises. These in­
clude how he established Levitical cities and cities of refuge (20:1-21:45; 
cf. Num. 35:1-8; Deut. 19:1-21), Calebs inheritance (Josh. 14:6-15; cf. 
Josh. 14:30), the land for the daughters of Zelophephad (Josh. 17:1-3; cf. 
Num. 27:1-23), as well as fighting to give his people rest in the land (Josh. 
22:4; cf. Deut. 12:10; 25:19).4 5  In context, Joshua 21:45 summarizes what 
Joshua has shown all along: The promises that God made are being upheld 
exactly the way God originally stated them. Accordingly, the author knew 
previous promises in all their detail.
The book of Joshua actually brings out another example of hermeneu­
tical precision. This pertains to the law of the king. The law of the king in 
Deuteronomy stipulated a king must not multiply wives, gold, or horses 
(Deut. 17:14-20). The intent in context concerns trusting God as opposed 
to a large dynasty, incredible wealth, or powerful military. Joshuas com­
mand to hamstring the horses most likely is in obedience to the intent of 
that regulation (Josh. 11:6).4 6  The consideration of this command contin­
ues in the prophets viewpoint of Israels history. David reminds Israel they 
should not hope in horses but in God who fights for them (Ps. 20:7). In the 
discussion of Solomons reign in 1  Kings, we can observe how although 
Solomons kingdom was glorious, it was doomed to failure as he slowly 
violates the law. He accumulates gold (1 Kings 9:11-14), horses (1 Kings 
4:26), and then the final blow: He has numerous women (1 Kings 11:1). 
After Solomon broke the law of the king, his kingdom collapses (1 Kings 
11:11-13). Later on in Kings, Jehoshaphat attempts to multiply gold but 
the narrator reveals how God thwarts his plans (1 Kings 22:48). These 
examples show how the inspired narrator implicitly affirms the law of the 
king. This illustrates the prophets not only accurately understood the gen­
eral gist of the law but also the meaning of its particular requirements.
44. Recall the above discussion about 2 Kings 17. The passage shows that the writer of 
Kings was thinking through Israels history through the lens of the covenant.

The law of the king is not the only example of how the prophets pay 
close attention to the details of the law. David demands fourfold restitution 
when Nathan tells him the parable of the stolen sheep (2 Sam. 12:6). This 
is rightly based upon Exodus 22:1. Solomon wisely counsels his son to 
bind wisdom and the law to his neck, just as Moses prescribes in Deuter­
onomy (Prov. 3:3; 6:21; cf. Deut. 6:8).4 7  The writer of Chronicles affirms 
how Amaziah executed those who betrayed his father but did not put their 
sons to death based upon Deuteronomy 24:16 (2 Chron. 25:4). Ezekiel re­
acts strongly against defiling himself because he understood the Levitical 
laws about ceremonial cleanliness (Ezek. 4:14; cf. Lev. 22:8; Deut. 14:3).4 8  
Isaiah calls upon heaven and earth to witness against Israel just as Moses 
stated (Isa. 1:2; cf. Deut. 30:19; 32:l).4 9  The list could go on.5 0  Again, all of 
this illustrates that the Old Testament writers were aware of the details of 
past laws and upheld them in their thinking and writings.
The prophets do not merely handle the law with precision but also 
show the same accuracy with other parts of the Pentateuch and the rest of 
the Old Testament. Isaiahs use of seed terminology in Isaiah 6:13 shows 
his care with promises found in the Pentateuch. Isaiah states although 
great devastation will occur, God promises a holy seed will remain. This 
appears to refer to the righteous remnant in Israel who will be purified as 
Isaiah was purified.5 1  Scholars observe Isaiahs choice of the term “seed” 
is not only appropriate because, in context, he is using a metaphor of a 
“tree” (cf. Isa. 6:13a), but also because it refers to the promises made in 
the Mosaic and Abrahamic covenants (cf. Gen. 22:16-18; Deut. 30:1- 
8).5 2  Moses states that after the exile, Israels offspring—their seed (Deut. 
30:6)—will be cleansed with a circumcised heart, possess the land, and 
dwell there in fulfillment of the Abrahamic covenant (Deut. 30:8-10). 
Isaiahs use of seed in Isaiah 6:13 as well as throughout his book affirms 
that exact picture (Isa. 41:8; 43:5; 53:10; 59:21). They will have dominion 
as promised (Isa. 54:13), inherit the land (65:9), receive and give blessing 
(61:9), and will be transformed (59:21).5 3  The usage of seed in this man­
ner is hardly incidental, for Isaiah nearly exclusively uses the word this 
way.5 4  This demonstrates a deliberate idea in Isaiahs mind about what

Solomon shows care in handling covenants revealed in the historical

(1 Kings 8:25).5 5  Solomon (and the inspired narrator) rightly understood

The Old Testament writers also allude to the major and minor proph­

[Heb. 3:5]; cf. Obad. 17). Obadiah promises that in the Day of YHWH, al­

for he says this pronouncement is “as YHWH has said” (rnrr "m  "i$*o).5 7  
In context, Joel appeals to what Obadiah says without any modification. 
The existence of a remnant shows that anyone who calls on the name of 
the Lord will be saved (Joel 2:32). Joel affirms what Obadiah meant.
Jeremiah employs Micah in his writing. He records how the elders 
saved the prophets life by correctly quoting from Micah 3:12. Jeremiah 
proclaimed that Jerusalem would be destroyed (Jer. 26:1-19) and the lead­
ers of Judah pointed out that Micah said the same thing. This too indi­
cates that the prophets (and at times, even the people of Israel) knew other 
prophets work, down to the individual verses and phrases involved.5 8
My last example of exegetical precision is the eagle motif in the Old Tes­
tament, which demonstrates the biblical writers care with material from the 
Pentateuch, wisdom literature, and the prophets. Early on, God compares 
how he delivered Israel in the Exodus to how he bore the nation from Egypt 
on “eagles wings” (D 'H tía ■ ’ Sp'btf; cf. Ex. 19:4). This highlights Gods protective,
in fulfillment of the Abrahamic promises. The two outliers may be rhetorical to show 
their transformation into the true righteous seed of Israel (cf. Isa. 57:19).

The illustration of the eagle metaphor demonstrates that even when re­
calling a word, the prophets know its original context and reflect that in 
their own writings. Furthermore, it also indicates that the prophets are con­
sistent with each other, even in their application. Isaiahs use of Exodus 19:4 
corresponds with Davids emphasis in Psalm 103. Finally, this demonstrates 
a growing theology in the Old Testament. Gods deliverance in the Exodus 
becomes the basis for the Lords work in peoples lives and ultimately in a fi­
nal Exodus deliverance to fulfill his promises to his people. Such theological 
development begins to highlight the nature of the prophetic hermeneutic— 
solid exegesis of texts naturally flows into theological progression.
Overall, this subsection has provided examples to demonstrate the 
prophets do not merely pay attention to general ideas in the scriptural 
texts but also precise verses, phrases, and even words. Again, this precision 
is not found only in certain prophets but rather is widespread throughout 
the canon. Such accuracy is also not limited to just the use of the law but 
also includes wisdom literature, as well as the major and minor proph­
ets. Scholars have highlighted more instances of such nuanced allusions.6 2  
Thus, ample evidence exists that part of the prophetic hermeneutic is ex- 
egetical accuracy not only in the general tenor of a text but also its details.
Synthesis
The above discussion exhibits how the prophets accurately main­
tained both the main ideas of the Scriptures as well as its particulars. I 
can cite a comment made by Waltke in broader support to what we have 
observed above. In his discussion of Old Testament intertextuality, Waltke 
observes, “In no case, however, do later Old Testament writers reverse Mo- 
sess teaching (cf. Deut. 13, 18).”6 3  His own reference to Deuteronomy 13

piece of deductive evidence that the prophets job was to uphold the law

Scripture said, which demands them to be good readers and exegetes of

ADDRESSING COUNTEREXAMPLES  
TO THE PROPHET AS EXEGETE
We could continue to examine more examples of correct interpretation. 
In fact, the rest of this chapter and the next present further evidence of 
the prophets exegetical precision. Nonetheless, one of the most effective 
ways to demonstrate the validity of the prophetic hermeneutic is to re­
move objections against its soundness. The examples below comprise the 
most substantial passages that cast doubt on the accuracy of the prophetic 
hermeneutic.6 4  In resolving the most significant counterexamples, we can 
posit the weight of evidence leans to the conclusion the prophets used the 
Scripture correctly. That should be the default position.
Jeremiah’s and Ezekiel’s Use of Deuteronomy
Jeremiah 31:28-29 and Ezekiel 18:2-3 provide a potential counterex­
ample to the above discussion. These two passages quote a popular maxim 
in Israel, stating “the fathers have eaten the bitter grapes and the childrens 
teeth received the edge.” In essence, Israel claimed they received the pun­
ishment their parents should have received. The looming judgment was 
not their fault but their parents fault. The saying is based upon Exodus 
20:5 and Deuteronomy 5:9, where God said the punishment for the sins 
of the fathers would go onto the children. Israels proverb interprets those 
verses to teach God punishes the children in place of their parents. Hence, 
he can hold the children guilty for their parents sin. God counters these 
allegations by saying every individual is responsible for their sin from now 
on (cf. Jer. 31:30; Ezek. 18:4). However, that seems to contradict what he 
said in Exodus 20:5 and Deuteronomy 5:9. Is this an instance where God 
changes or contradicts the meaning of previous revelation?6 5
In addressing this, the key is to determine what Exodus 20:5 and 
Deuteronomy 5:9 taught. Do those passages say God will condemn 
the children instead of their parents? Do those verses imply God does 
not hold the individual responsible? A careful look at Exodus 20:5 and 
Deuteronomy 5:9 shows this is not the case. Those two passages reflect
64. These are the most cited passages in such discussions. Cf. Fishbane, Biblical Interpre­
tation; Enns, Inspiration and Incarnation; Enns, “Response to Greg Beale.”
65. Sarna, Exodus, 110.

--- Page 66 ---
65The Prophet as Exegete and Theologian
how the consequences of punishment (“God visiting the iniquity”) can 
spill over to other people. For example, a famine would impact sub­
sequent generations. Exile also would certainly affect ones children. 
Merrill sums up the verse by saying, “The repercussions are so great 
as to impact generations yet unborn if they continue to hate God.”6 6  
Thus, Exodus 20:5 and Deuteronomy 5:9 warn that ones sin has long- 
reaching effects. Nevertheless, all of this is far from saying God held the 
children guilty instead of their parents. These verses do not say what 
Israel claimed they said.
In fact, the wording specifically states the opposite of what Israel 
maintained. The texts state God will continue the consequences of sin 
against those who hate him (‘ tó b , Ex. 20:5; Deut. 5:9). Tigay observes this 
indicates that God prolongs the effects of his judgment only if future gen­
erations prove to be disobedient as well.6 7  As such, these verses teach that 
God holds individuals responsible for their own actions.
If this is the case, then God was not reinterpreting Exodus 20:5 and 
Deuteronomy 5:9. Those texts affirm his emphasis on individual respon­
sibility. Rather, the discussion shows that the Israelites thoroughly misin­
terpreted those passages. They believed God held them guilty for sins they 
had not done—an idea those texts never intended at all. As scholars agree, 
the Israelites did not understand the passage correctly.6 8
Thus, in context, God is not reinterpreting his Word but rather con­
fronting Israel for their misinterpretation. In both Jeremiah and Ezekiel, 
the Lord rectifies Israels error by quoting a form of Deuteronomy 24:16, 
which states that the one who sins will die (cf. Jer. 31:30; Ezek. 18:4).6 9  God 
has upheld individual responsibility. Thus, while the punishment against 
the parents may incidentally harm future generations, Israel cannot ap­
peal to these verses to blame their parents (or even Gods sovereignty) for 
their problems while absolving themselves from guilt (Ezek. 18:19). As 
Cooper and Block observe, the original intent of Exodus 20:5 and Deuter­
onomy 5:9 should have reminded them that if they bear Gods judgment 
it is because they too were “those who hate him.”7 0  Thus, these passages do 
not justify blame-shifting. Instead, Israel should realize that God always 
will heed and respond to repentance (Ezek. 18:21). The Lord will make 
this clear, both in the current age as well as in the new era to come. Israel

would then cease to use the proverb (Ezek. 18:3) because it grossly misrep­
resents Gods declarations.7 1
Hermeneutically, this example brings up several important points. 
First, Israel could misinterpret or misapply previous revelation. They 
believed Exodus 20:5 and Deuteronomy 5:9 implied that God held the 
children guilty for the sins of their parents, contrary to what those texts 
claim. However, that is not the prophetic hermeneutic. Jeremiah and Eze­
kiel are the prophets, not the Israelites they describe. Second, it illustrates 
that one can draw a faulty implication from a text. Not everything falls 
into the parameters of the significance the author intended. Third, God 
through the prophets confronts this wrongful use of Scripture. This illus­
trates the Bibles concern for an accurate understanding of the Scripture 
and theology. Fourth, it is interesting that in the discussion, the prophets 
appeal to an accurate interpretation of another passage to counteract false 
thinking (Deut. 24:16). This evidences their repeated faithful exegesis. Fi­
nally, this passage does not indicate that God revises the meaning of past 
revelation. Rather, the meaning of the passages are upheld and clarified to 
silence misinterpretation.7 2  The counterexample further proves my point. 
The prophets were hermeneutically accurate with past revelation. They 
did not twist the Scriptures, nor did God move them to do so.
DanieFs Use of Jeremiah
Another suggested counterexample concerns how Daniel used Jer­
emiah in the famous “seventy-weeks prophecy.” Jeremiah had prophesied 
Israel should return to their land in seventy years (Jer. 29:10-12). Upon 
thinking about this prophecy (Dan. 9:1-2; cf. Jer. 29:10-12), Daniel real­
izes this time had come and prays the Lord would forgive their sins. In 
response, God sends Daniel an important message. The complete restora­
tion of Jerusalem, when sin is forgiven and the holy place restored, will 
not happen in seventy years but rather “seventy weeks” or “seventy sets of 
seven years” (Dan. 9:24-26).7 3  Some scholars view these “seventy weeks” 
as a symbolic interpretation of the seventy years prophesied by Jeremiah. 
If that is the case, then Daniel reinterprets the numbers in Jeremiah in a 
way he did not originally intend.7 4
71. Block, Ezekiel 1 -24, 561.

all along intended that idea which Ezekiel and Jeremiah supported.

In response, we may initially note that Daniel interpreted Jeremiah 
correctly. Daniel prays because he recognized the seventy years Jeremi­
ah specified had reached their completion (Dan. 9:1-2).7 5  This means 
he understood seventy years as a period of seventy years. In fact, one 
commentator cites this as an example of taking prophecy “literally” or 
according to the normal rules of language and grammar.7 6  Thus, Daniel 
interprets the meaning of Jeremiah quite accurately.
So what is happening in Daniel 9? The answer comes from understand­
ing the prophet is not just thinking about the seventy years in exile. Jer­
emiahs prophecy is not the only text in his mind. Daniel himself claims as 
much. He states he was reading through not one book but “books” (anaoa; 
9:2, note the plural). Similarly, he discusses what was written in the “book 
of Moses” (9:11, 13). In light of this, it is significant that Daniels interces­
sion shares nearly identical wording with Deuteronomy 30:1-6 and 1  Kings 
8:30.7 7  It suggests he was thinking through those texts along with Jeremiah.
What is interesting is that the context surrounding Jeremiahs proph­
ecy of the seventy-year captivity also alludes to those same passages (cf. Jer. 
29:10-14). Jeremiah repeats the language of Deuteronomy and Kings in dis­
cussing the need for repentance, seeking God with all ones heart, in addition 
to describing the complete restoration from exile (Jer. 29:10, 13; cf. Deut. 
30:1-4; 1  Kings 8:48-50). These connections in Jeremiah explain why Daniel 
alludes to Deuteronomy and Kings (with Jeremiah) in the first place. Daniel 
arguably read Jeremiah and knew of the other passages involved. That drove 
his own prayer to reflect the language of Jeremiah, Kings, and Deuteronomy.
The intertextuality of Jeremiah with Kings and Deuteronomy clarifies 
what Daniel is wondering in Daniel 9. First Kings 8:30 and Deuteronomy 
30:1-6 focus upon the ultimate end of exile. By contrast Jeremiah 29 pre­
dicts a return from Babylon after seventy years but does not directly associ­
ate the seventy years with that full restoration. Put differently, the grammar 
of Jeremiah 29 describes how God guarantees Israel will return to the land 
after seventy years (Jer. 29:10) and how it will ultimately be restored per 
Deuteronomy and Kings (Jer. 29:12). The Lord does not, however, guaran­
tee in Jeremiah that they will be ultimately restored when they return to 
the land after seventy years. Accordingly, Daniel wonders how the prophecy 
in Jeremiah 29 works with what was declared in Deuteronomy and Kings. 
Daniels prayer pleads for God to cause all of these prophecies to coincide.
From this, we can observe that the key question Daniel has is not the 
interpretation of Jeremiah 29:10-12 and the seventy years. That is clear to

the prophet. Rather, Daniel deals with multiple interwoven texts and how 
exactly they all fit together. He wonders how the seventy-year prophecy 
relates to Gods larger plan concerning the complete reinstatement of Is­
rael. As such, we have moved from dealing with the meaning of a text to 
significance or implication. Daniel is not wondering about what happens 
in Jeremiahs prophecy, or what is described in Kings or Deuteronomy. He 
is interested in how they all harmonize together. That is what is unclear 
and what he prays about.7 8  As Gentry observes, Daniel prays that this time

of Jeremiahs seventy years being the complete conclusion of exile, it actually 
will be seventy times seven sets of years (weeks) till the end of exile, a much 
longer time (Dan. 9:24).8 2  Israel would return to rebuild the city (cf. Dan. 
9:24-25). However, that does not mark the completion of exile. Redemp­
tive history will be fulfilled only after Messiah dies and further tribulation 
occurs. Through this, Daniel knows how Jeremiahs seventy-year prophecy 
fits with the eschatological statements found in Deuteronomy and Kings.8 3
With all this in mind, we return to whether Daniel misinterpreted Jer­
emiah. Fundamentally, the seventy-weeks prophecy is not a symbolic in­
terpretation of the meaning of Jeremiahs prophecy. Rather, the context in­
dicates the prophecy explains how the return from exile after seventy years 
fits in with the larger plan portrayed by other texts intertextually linked 
with Jeremiah. Thus, we are dealing with the implications of these prophe­
cies (how they fit together) as opposed to their interpretation (what they 
describe). All of this displays Daniels accurate interpretation of Jeremiah. 
He understood the seventy years as referring to seventy years—a mark of 
the grammatical-historical method. He also rightly understood Jeremiahs 
prophecy linked in some way with Deuteronomy and Kings. He was pre­
cise with the details of the text as well as its intertextual context. This is a 
case where exact exegesis of past revelation makes opportunity for biblical 
theological development. Daniels work brings various passages together, 
and that sharpens our understanding of redemptive history and theology. 
The prophets are thus theologians, as will soon be discussed.

69The Prophet as Exegete and Theologian
Isaiah’s Use of Deuteronomy
The final major alleged misuse of the Old Testament concerns how 
Isaiah used Deuteronomy 23:1 in Isaiah 56:4-5. The prophet states the eu­
nuch will be accepted in the community and God will accept their offerings 
(Isa. 56:4-5).8 4  Hence, the eunuchs should not regard themselves any longer 
as a “ dry tree” or fruitless. However, Deuteronomy 23:1 states that eunuchs 
are barred from Gods assembly, and that Levitical regulations prohibit eu­
nuchs from offering sacrifice (cf. Lev. 21:16-23).8 5  It appears God, through 
Isaiah, proclaims a direct contradiction to what was established in Mosaic 
Law. Scholars label this a transformation of the law.8 6  This is not the only 
example of such transformation (cf. Ezek. 44:9-16).8 7  Hence, the response 
in this section is paradigmatic for all similar instances. At this point, we can 
ask if Isaiah really reinterpreted the nature of the Mosaic Law.
We can make two observations about this issue. First, the reason eu­
nuchs would say they were a “dry tree” or cut off from Gods people is 
because of the laws in the Mosaic covenant. In other words, Isaiahs entire 
discussion presumes he understood the law. If the prophet (or even his 
audience) did not understand the law, then the shock value of the example 
becomes meaningless. So Isaiah rightly comprehended the law.
Second, viewing this as a contradiction of the law assumes a couple of 
realities about the law. It presumes God was only concerned with the letter 
of the law and even more, the law was to be a permanent fixture in Israelite 
society by design. However, neither are exactly the case. The law does not al­
ways define absolute moral values. Waltke observes that being emasculated 
is not inherently a sin.8 8  Rather, the law mandates these things to illustrate 
the nature of purity. God requires worshippers to be pure. However, if the 
eunuch can be purified by the Servants atoning work (cf. Isa. 53:1-12), then 
he is actually perfectly clean and can legitimately join the community to fel­
lowship with God.8 9  The purpose of the law is satisfied. Even more, the Mo­
saic covenant itself anticipated a time when such spiritual transformation 
would take place (Deut. 30:1-6). This entails a new covenant that establishes 
new rules, ones not in contradiction with the old but in fulfillment of their 
intent.9 0  Isaiah declares the Servant brings about what the Mosaic Law an­
ticipated. This demonstrates that the prophet not only understands the law s 
meaning but also its significance relative to redemptive history.
Interestingly enough, Oswalt observes these very dynamics in Isaiah and 
states, “The practice of Jesus is instructive here, and we may wonder if his

interpretive method was not shaped by his study of this OT book along with 
others.”9 1  This supposed problem passage ends up illustrating the opposite. 
Not only does Isaiah show a proper understanding of the law, but his logic 
also becomes a framework for Christ s own logic about the law. The prophetic 
hermeneutic sets up for the hermeneutic of Christ in the New Testament.
In any case, a shift in covenants in redemptive history does not mean 
that the prophets contradict previous revelation. Instead, it demonstrates 
how the prophets understood the law in its meaning and its significance 
in Gods plan. Based upon this, prophets, like Isaiah, expressed how that 
covenant reaches its intended climax. Like the prior example of Jeremiah 
in Daniel, this too begins to evidence how the prophets highlight theologi­
cal development in Gods plan and revelation.
Synthesis
The above examples are the major objections (or at least, types of ob­
jections) against the hermeneutical accuracy of the prophets. In examin­
ing them, we have discovered that instead of hermeneutical revisionism, 
the prophets knew what the texts meant. They were also aware of the inter- 
textual contexts surrounding those passages. They even challenged false 
interpretations of those texts. Hence, the prophets knew and maintained 
the ideas of antecedent revelation.
Thus, as opposed to seeing examples of hermeneutical blunders, this 
section showcases the hermeneutical complexity and accuracy discussed 
throughout this chapter. Moreover, in making these connections, the proph­
ets exhibit a theological development taking place in the canon. Daniels new 
revelation about the “seventy weeks” clarifies how the connections between 
Jeremiah, Deuteronomy, and Kings function in portraying the end of exile. 
His work sharpens our understanding of Gods redemptive historical plan. 
Similarly, Isaiah shows how the anticipations of the Mosaic covenant for a 
new covenant are actualized through the Servant. Such intertextuality binds 
new revelation with the old in ways that do not compromise the meaning 
or significance of the old but rather complete and refine the ramifications 
of previous revelation. The Old Testament writers were developing grander 
theological ideas in their writings. This begins to display the prophets as 
theologians—and to that subject we now turn.
PROPHET AS THEOLOGIAN
As we have seen, the prophets do not merely repeat older revelation, but 
also expound upon its theological ideas. The prophets hold up Gods law 
and covenants to condemn Israel throughout its history and thereby show 
Gods faithfulness to judge. Daniel develops the theology of exile and es­
chatology found in Jeremiah, Deuteronomy, and Kings. Isaiah shows more
91. Oswalt, Isaiah 40-66,458.

--- Page 72 ---
71The Prophet as Exegete and Theologian
precisely how the new covenant anticipated by Moses would bring a new 
era. The prophets show how the Abrahamic promises will help to restore 
the world. Ezekiel and Daniel employ the motif of creation from Genesis 
1  to show how paradise is not forever lost. The examples discussed so far 
begin to illustrate how good exegesis leads to profound theology.
Hence, it is natural to move from discussing the prophet as exegete 
to the prophet as theologian, to contemplate not only how they rightly 
interpreted the meaning of Scripture but also dealt with its significance. 
A good model of meaning and significance for this discussion is the ad­
age “ideas have consequences.” What we have seen in previous examples 
is how the prophets upheld the original ideas of a text (meaning) and 
showed the consequences or ramifications those ideas have on various 
theological topics and themes as well as redemptive history (significance). 
Within this, the prophets often honed in on certain implications of a text 
discussed by their predecessors. They gave more detail about how exactly 
those repercussions would flesh out and set up for later prophets to con­
tinue the development of those particular ramifications.
As a result, the prophetic writer at times participates in a chain of 
texts that provides more information about the particular significance a 
passage has on theology and Gods plan. Each text builds upon what the 
prophet s predecessor(s) have done and set up for later prophets to further 
focus those idea(s). This is the nature of progressive revelation at its core.
As the prophets provide detail and further define the theological con­
sequences of prior revelation, they write theological truth and do theol­
ogy. They are thereby theologians. This section will explore this reality in 
further depth.
A Note on Systematic Theology
Sadly, I will not be able to talk as much about systematic theology 
due to space constraints and because the issue of the New Testaments 
use of the Old revolves primarily around biblical theology. Nevertheless, 
the prophets are simultaneously biblical theologians and those who give 
thought to topics in systematics. They not only think about how redemp­
tive history unfolds or how various motifs, concepts, or themes prog­
ress; they also contemplate the nature of Gods character (Ps. 74:12-23; 
77:12-20; 103:8), sin (1 Kings 8:46), and the nature of man (Ps. 8:1-8; 
103:14; cf. Gen. 1:26-28; 2:7) by using past revelation to demonstrate or 
support their point. Often times, their rationale in applying Scripture re­
volves around certain universal, foundational, and unchanging truths, the 
very material of systematic theology.9 2
92. We may contrast this with biblical theology which essentially traces the progression 
of themes and ideas in Scripture. See Grudem, Systematic Theology: An Introduction 
to Biblical Doctrine, 21. Grudem essentially defines systematics as collecting and sum­
marizing all the Bible says about a subject. See Klink III and Lockett, Understanding

--- Page 73 ---
72 The Prophetic Hermeneutic
The use of the law throughout the Old Testament exhibits this reality. 
The prophets use of the law is often grounded upon Gods unchanging holi­
ness. He does not tolerate sin and judges wickedness exactly as he stipulates 
(Isa. 5:16; 6:1-3; Ezek. 36:23; Amos 4:1-2). Such a use of the Old Testament 
brings out the character of God, a topic in systematic theology. Similarly, the 
Old Testament writers appeal to Gods lovingkindness, which recalls Exo­
dus 34:6-8. In that text, the Lord demonstrated grace in dealing with Israel 
and the golden calf.9 3  The prophets meditate on that reality and apply Gods 
lovingkindness to new situations. In doing so, they assume the immutabil­
ity of God and again reflect upon his character (cf. Pss. 86:15; 103:8; Joel 
2:13). Another example concerns the Abrahamic covenant. The prophets, 
in fleshing out those promises, do not merely show how redemptive history 
will progress but also the nature of eschatology, a systematic category. Their 
work, in alluding not only to Genesis but to each other, forms a composite 
picture of Israels eschatological hope (Isa. 2:2-4; 11:1-9; Amos 9:11-15; Joel 
3:18-21). As such, they are deliberating building a theology of the culmina­
tion of history. Such unity and cohesion forms the basis of systematics.9 4
The development of the eagle metaphor is also quite significant for this 
discussion. As stated earlier, the motif consistently refers to the Exodus and 
reflects Gods saving power (cf. Ex. 19:4; Ps. 103:7-8; Isa. 43:16-19). Da­
vid uses the metaphor to show how God works in the lives of individuals 
(Ps. 103:5), and Isaiah specifies the ultimate demonstration of Gods saving 
character: a deliverance that exceeds the original (Isa. 43:1-21). This use of 
Exodus 19:4 reflects both systematic and biblical theological reasoning at 
work. On one hand, the prophets apply the eagle metaphor to individuals 
and new situations based upon Gods omnipotent power and unchanging 
commitment to save, as evidenced by the Exodus. All of Gods people can 
rely upon their mighty God. This logic exhibits the dynamics of Gods na­
ture, something pertaining to theology proper and thereby systematics. On 
the other hand, the logic concerning Gods character paves the way for a 
discussion of a new Exodus, a new and final demonstration of Gods deliv­
ering might on every level.9 5  That is a biblical theological development that 
focuses on one important implication of Gods work in the Exodus. Hence,
Biblical Theology, 59-124. In summarizing Carsons thoughts on the matter, Klink 
and Lockett discuss how biblical theology is not atemporal like systematics but rather 
reading Scripture in its historical development and purpose.

the eagle metaphor exhibits how the prophets understood the significance 
of texts along both systematic and biblical theological lines.
Thus, the prophets not only read the Old Testament with a view to 
topics relating to biblical theology but also to systematics. As the above 
example demonstrates, they are not mutually exclusive. Rather, reflecting 
upon Gods nature shown in the Exodus (systematics) leads to a new de­
velopment in redemptive history (biblical theology).9 6  All of this demon­
strates that the prophets were sophisticated thinkers who knew the theo­
logical import of past writings.
Intertextuality of the Davidic Covenant and the Second David
Having made these remarks, we can move to a more in-depth discussion 
of the prophet as theologian. The Davidic covenant is our first major example 
of this. That treaty's intertextual impact on the Old Testament demands at 
least an entire monograph. This discussion will not be comprehensive. Nev­
ertheless, I can point to others, like Johnston and Grisanti, who have more 
comprehensively written about the Davidic covenant and the Old Testa­
ment.9 7  They show how the Davidic covenant develops in the Old Testament 
in very similar ways to what I am about to say below. Accordingly, the follow­
ing discussion is not novel but illustrates how the prophets are theologians.
Since I cannot be exhaustive, my strategy is to focus upon the intertex­
tuality within the covenant itself and its use by the prophets as they begin 
to develop the concept of a second David. From this, we can see that the 
prophets rightly knew the Scripture as well as understood its theological 
implications. Such an understanding served as the platform for the formu­
lation of theology.
The Davidic covenant is itself highly intertextual. Second Samuel 7:9-14 
contains allusions to a variety of ideas found in prior covenantal texts. For 
example, the Lord incorporates the Abrahamic guarantees into the Davidic 
covenant. God promises to make Davids name great (‘ pin: nú Tito*!, 2 Sam. 
7:9) just like he formerly promised to Abram (Gen. 12:2).9 8  God promises he 
will settle Israel in their land and plant them there (2 Sam. 7:10). Such lan­
guage alludes back to the Abrahamic covenant and Mosaic promises (Gen. 
15:18-21; Ex. 15:17; cf. Deut. 28:6-11). Furthermore, God guarantees a time 
of peace and safety unlike the time of the Judges, a time characterized by 
Israels wickedness and covenant curse (2 Sam. 7:11). The Davidic covenant 
will resolve the tensions found in the Mosaic covenant. Overall, the Abraha­
mic and Mosaic covenants are intertwined into the Davidic covenant.9 9

In addition, with the promise of rest for Gods people (2 Sam. 7:11), 
the Davidic covenant incorporates certain ideals related to the Noahic 
covenant. Three factors support this association. First, the word “rest” in 2 
Samuel 7:11 shares the same root as the name “Noah,” which may clue the 
reader to make an association with that covenant Yirnn, 2 Sam. 7:11).1 0 0
Second, the term “rest” in Scripture can be a loaded term associated 
with a grander theological concept of creational rest. Oswalt s work in this 
area is significant.1 0 1  As he notes, the term “rest” in 2 Samuel 7:11 may 
seem merely to describe Israels state of peace. This matches how Moses 
used the word in Deuteronomy (cf. Deut. 25:19). However, Oswalt also 
observes that the Pentateuch establishes this type of “rest” in a grander 
context. For instance, God describes rest not only in political terms but 
also with a spiritual and relational component. He declares Israels rest 
includes his presence communing with them (Ex. 33:14).1 0 2  Similarly, 
Joshua that affirms Israels rest is not only in the context of military se­
curity but also the fulfillment of all Gods promises (Josh. 23:14). These 
broader descriptions of rest make sense, since God has already correlated 
Israels resting with the Sabbath rest and creation (Ex. 20:8-11). That is the 
foundation for all of Gods descriptions of Israels rest. All of this suggests 
that Israels rest relates to the fulfillment of his promises, even a restored 
creation.1 0 3  Such an eschatological or heightened idea of rest is even found 
in the context of 2 Samuel 7. Earlier in the chapter, the narrator records 
how Israel and David had rest (2 Sam. 7:1). If God was merely looking at 
military security, then he would not need to promise this to David since 
it already existed. Instead, as Grisanti notes, this promise looks to some­
thing that far exceeds Davids current circumstances. It looks to a future 
eschatological rest.1 0 4  Accordingly, Gods promise of rest to David is situ­
ated in something greater than mere national stability. Based upon this, at
enant (and the Mosaic covenant which is connected with the Abrahamic covenant) 
has bearing upon the Davidic covenant.

bare minimum, the Davidic covenant conceptually relates to the Noahic
