Third, the Noahic covenant and its context show the connection is not 
merely conceptual but something far more direct. The Noahic covenant 
participates in a theology of creational rest which sets up for the Davidic 
covenant. The Noahic covenant guaranteed a limited rest in creation (Gen. 
5:29; 8:21-22).1 0 5  Noahs post-Flood experience demonstrates that reality. 
He plants a vineyard and it grows (Gen. 9:20-21) contrary to the toil and 
weeds promised in Genesis 3:17-19 (cf. Gen. 5:29). Accordingly, God has 
somewhat restrained the effects of the Fall to move creation back toward its 
original Sabbath rest. Hence, the Noahic covenant reinforces the creation 
rest paradigm we have observed. Moreover, this concept of rest hones in on 
Israels king. Later on in Genesis, God promises that from the line of Judah 
an ultimate king will come whose name is Shiloh (Gen. 49:10). His pres­
ence is in the context of military domination (Gen. 49:10b). His presence is 
also associated with an abundance of fruitful vines which are strong enough 
to tie a donkey to (Gen. 49:11). The vine has already been an indicator of 
restored creation in Noahs day. It appears again with the king to show his 
role relates to a new creation.1 0 6  In this way, the Noahic promises of rest are 
associated with a king. Such a rest incorporates military peace within a cre­
ational peace. This fits very well with what we see in the Davidic covenant.1 0 7
The Davidic covenant incorporates the theology surrounding the Noa­
hic covenant. The word play initially indicates an allusion. The concept of the 
word “rest” and its usage throughout Scripture is conducive to this connec­
tion. Within that, the development of the Noahic covenant establishes that 
creational rest is associated with a future king. With such expectations, it is 
no surprise the Davidic covenant picks up on those ideas.1 0 8  Having incor­
porated the promises of the Abrahamic, Mosaic, and Noahic covenants, God

has made the weight of redemptive history fall upon the Davidic dynasty.

At this point, we can observe the writer of Samuels use of Scripture 
is quite complex. In a span of a few verses, he alludes to a variety of cov­
enants and texts. He shows the covenants converge into this covenant 
making the one who fulfills the Davidic covenant the one who completes 
the covenantal promises of God.1 0 9  Second Samuel 7 is not a simple story 
about God talking with David. It is rather dense theology that will shape 
the rest of the Bible including our conception of Christ.1 1 0  The prophets 
are not just historians but theologians.
The question is whether the reading suggested above has any merit. 
Does the author of Samuel intend to depict the Davidic covenant as the 
convergence of various covenant promises? Initially, I could argue I am 
not alone in this presentation. Various scholars have affirmed that reali­
ty.1 1 1  Nonetheless, a more convincing way to affirm this complex under­
standing of 2 Samuel 7 is looking at the reign of Solomon in 1  Kings.
The picture of Solomons reign in Kings confirms all of these connec­
tions. In 1  Kings 4:20-5:6 (Heb., v. 20), the author reveals Israel is as numer­
ous as sand on the seashore. This corresponds to the Abrahamic promise 
(cf. Gen. 22:17). Solomons dominion of land from the River Euphrates to 
the border of Egypt echoes the land promises found in the Abrahamic cov­
enant (cf. Gen. 15:18). The writer in 1  Kings also recounts there is peace (4:24 
[Heb., 5:4]) in the land which indicates the covenant curse of the Mosaic 
covenant is mitigated (cf. 2 Sam. 7:11). Finally, the narrator states there is also 
rest (5:4 [Heb., 5:18]) such that everyone sits under their own vine and fig 
tree (4:25). As discussed, the vine was a symbol of rest as seen in Noah (Gen. 
9:20) and later with the prophecy about Shiloh (Gen. 49:11). The author of 
Kings thereby displays the theology of rest incorporated in the Davidic cov­
enant.1 1 2  After God had established rest, Solomon appeals to Hiram to help 
him build a house for God (1 Kings 5:4-5 [Heb., 5:18-19]), which is also part 
of what God promised to do in the Davidic covenant (2 Sam. 7:11b). First 
Kings portrays Solomon as a potential candidate of one who could complete 
the Davidic covenant and bring order and peace to the entire world.1 1 3

The parallels between Kings and 2 Samuel 7:9-11 are significant in 
two major ways. First, while 1  Kings 4:20-5:6 (Heb., v. 20) confirms our 
interpretation of 2 Samuel 7:9-11, the text actually serves to show that the 
author of Kings read and understood the covenant precisely. He understood 
that the Abrahamic, Mosaic, and Noahic covenants converge in the Davidic 
covenant. He even picked up on small details like the vine motif introduced 
earlier in revelation.1 1 4  This makes him an exegete of the text. Second, the au­
thor of Kings also recognized and correctly acted upon the ramifications of 
the Davidic covenant. He understood the Davidic covenant was not merely 
an isolated event of history but something that has theological consequenc­
es for the rest of history. The writer of Kings understood Israels past through 
this grid. This posits the book of Kings as a heavily theological document. 
It is not merely a historical record of Solomons exploits. Rather, it records 
how God works out the promises of the Davidic covenant and how all that 
relates to his plan to combat the curse and restore creational rest. Again, the 
prophets are not just historians but profound theologians who show how 
Gods plan develops to achieve his promises and aims.
Consequently, this brings us to the next part of the discussion. I desire 
not only to discuss the intertextuality of the Davidic covenant with prior 
revelation but also how the prophets use that to discuss a second David. 
With language of‘eternal dominion” and “seed” (:n _ T , a term which we will 
later see alludes to messianic texts like Gen. 3:15), the Davidic covenant 
looks to the future and to a messianic individual. Do the prophets pick up 
on and develop this?
For the rest of this discussion, we will focus upon the work of Amos, 
Hosea, and Micah since they interconnect with each other and with 
Samuel and Kings in discussing the Davidic covenant. In Amos 9:11, the 
prophet talks of the “fallen booth of David” (TH  nso). The description 
contrasts the enduring house of David (th  ma) that God promised in 
2 Samuel 7:11b.1 1 5  Amos alludes to 2 Samuel to show where the Davidic 
dynasty currently stands relative to those promises. It has collapsed. His 
critique matches the critique the author of Kings later provides about the 
Davidic house (cf. 2 Kings 25:1-26).
However, Amos does not leave the reader with the collapse of the Da­
vidic dynasty. He affirms the promises to David will be fulfilled and in doing 
so, develops the theological implications of various texts. He proclaims God 
will plant Israel in the land (9:15) just as God described in the Davidic cov­
enant (2 Sam. 7:10). The nation will plant vineyards (Amos 9:14) evoking 
the imagery of the vine and its association with rest in Genesis 9:20, 49:11, 
and 1  Kings 4:25 (cf. 2 Sam. 7:10). Accordingly, God restores Israel to the

of as ’•ira “the ancient days” in Amos 9:11, so such language would not be out of
place in Micah” (44).

imagery of the vine in a way identical to 1  Kings 4:25 (cf. Mic. 4:4). Micah 
is thoroughly entrenched in previous revelation.
Like his predecessors, Micah does not merely reiterate what they say 
but adds detail to their ideas. Just as Amos discusses the collapse of the Da- 
vidic dynasty, so Micah expounds upon it by saying Davids house returns 
to Adullam, the location where David first began evading Saul in the wil­
derness (Mic. 1:15; cf. 1  Sam. 22:1).1 2 1  The Davidic dynasty has collapsed 
to the point where it must begin anew. It must restart where David began. 
Consequently, just as Hosea envisioned a new David, so Micah proclaims 
the one who renews the Davidic dynasty must be from Bethlehem, the 
birthplace of David.1 2 2  The Messiah will be a new David even originating 
from the same birthplace in order to restart and fulfill the dynasty. Finally, 
Micah continues the picture discussed by the prophets about the eschato­
logical fulfillment of the Davidic covenant. He shows that this era is one 
like Solomons day (Mic. 5:2b [Heb., 5:1b], as anticipated by Kings, and 
an Edenic rest (Mic. 4:4), as anticipated by the prophets. Even more, he 
reveals it is a climactic golden era of world peace all centering on the new 
David (4:1-4; 5:2b [Heb., 5:1b]; 7:14,15, 20).1 2 3  With that, Micah provides 
a more complete picture of how the Davidic Covenant will shape the cli­
max of Gods plan.
The writer of Kings as well as Amos, Hosea, and Micah interact with 
the Davidic covenant and each other to reveal how the Davidic covenant 
will flesh out in redemptive history.1 2 4  One may observe their uniformity in 
understanding previous revelation. The prophets have maintained the same 
story line and discussed the same topics using the same motifs and wording 
to show their continuity with each other. These similarities demonstrate the 
interconnectedness of their writings and consistency with each other.
At the same time, there is still development. The prophets writings do 
not merely reiterate the promises integrated in that covenant but also elabo­
rate on those ideas. In this way, the prophets extend the chain of thought

this future restoration. Rather than providing a single unified profile, the prophets paint 
complementary portraits of the future, each with its own contribution to the whole por­
trait. Their portraits of the restoration resembled a kaleidoscope of images in the pro­
cess of being brought into a crisp, finely tuned picture as revelation progressed” (73-74).

80 The Prophetic Hermeneutic
begun in 2 Samuel 7 through the canon. The initial presentation of the cov­
enant in 2 Samuel 7 states the general realities of the Davidic covenant and 
Gods plan with a view toward the Messiah. Later prophets do not alter these 
ideas but rather specify and focus upon how they will work out in redemp­
tive history. They show the consequences of those concepts and promises. 
Kings demonstrates that no king, as of yet, can fulfill those promises. Amos 
clarifies that although the Davidic house has collapsed, God will still keep 
his promises precisely. He provides further details of what this will look 
like in the future. Hosea affirms this description of how Gods promises are 
fulfilled. Within that, he further develops details about another part of the 
Davidic covenant, the promise of Seed. Hosea describes the need for that 
individual to be a new David in order to fulfill the covenant. Micah sharpens 
the picture depicted by his predecessors. He states that because this king 
is a new David, he will be born in Bethlehem and enter the wilderness to 
fulfill Davids role and resurrect the royal house. At this point in progressive 
revelation, we have a clearer understanding of the impact the Davidic cov­
enant has on Gods plan and the nature of the Davidic king who will fulfill it 
all. By explaining the particular consequences past ideas have, the prophets 
provide us with a rich theology about the Davidic promises, a new David, 
and even a restored creation.
This logical progression sets up for further revelation. Amos, Hosea, 
and Micah do not end the discussion of the new David but rather begin 
it. Other prophets then pick up on this very thread they established (Isa. 
55:3; Jer. 33:17-21; Ezek. 34:24).1 2 5  In fact, this goes to the New Testament. 
The discussion above sheds some light on the significance of Jesus being 
born in Bethlehem and then going to the wilderness to be tempted, just 
like David sojourned in the wilderness of Adullam (cf. Matt. 4:1-11). Per­
haps Matthew picks up on the story line established by the prophets before 
to show how Christ is the true David who will resurrect the Davidic line 
and establish Edenic rest. In this way, the prophets theological sophistica­
tion sets up for the apostles. The prophetic hermeneutic continues into the 
apostolic hermeneutic.
Development of the Vine Metaphor
The vine motif is another great example of theological development. 
While the vine in the previous discussion was a symbol of rest, the vine 
motif discussed here portrays the status and spiritual health of Israel.1 2 6  
Psalm 80:9-17 provides the first instance of this particular metaphor.1 2 7

Asaph describes how God plants the vine so that its roots go to the river 
Euphrates (v. 11). The historical reference covers the time from the Exo­
dus to the time of David (cf. 2 Sam. 7:9-8:3). At present though, Israel 
is in distress and so God has removed the vines hedge of protection and 
allowed animals to ravage it (v. 13). Nevertheless, the psalmist looks for­
ward to a future restoration of the vine (w. 14-15). Hence, Asaph uses the 
vine as a metaphor of Israels past, present, and future.
The prophets continue the vine imagery for Israel. Isaiah picks up on 
Asaphs particular metaphor. Both Isaiah and Asaph record the Lord clears 
the land of stones (Isa. 5:2; cf. Ps. 80:9). God also tears down the hedge 
of protection in both passages (Isa. 5:5; cf. Ps. 80:3). The prophet incor­
porates Asaphs metaphor without modification. At the same time, Isaiah 
not only recounts Asaphs vine but builds upon it. Israel at this point not 
only is ravaged by enemies but is also spiritually wicked. Hence, Isaiah 
describes Israel as a vine with terrible fruit (Isa. 5:2b).1 2 8  This development 
respects the original context. Asaph intended for the metaphor to describe 
Israels condition for its past, present, and future. Isaiah uses the imagery 
for the purpose Asaph prescribed. He shows the implications of the meta­
phor on a particular stage of Israels history. In this way, he does not violate 
the original meaning but works out the significance of the text.
Hosea maintains this line of thought (10:1).1 2 9  The first part of the 
verse upholds Asaphs original metaphor (Hos. 10:1a). Hosea recalls how 
Israel was a luxurious vine. What is this talking about? This does not de­
scribe Israels present state, since at the time the nation was not luxurious 
but chaotic.1 3 0  Instead, Stuart argues Hoseas choice of the term “Israel” as 
opposed to Ephraim indicates he discusses a time when the nation was 
united, the time of its founding and conquest.1 3 1  This is an exact match 
with Asaphs metaphor which also discussed the same era. In addition, 
the second part of the verse upholds Isaiahs development of the meta­
phor (Hos. 10:1b). The vines luxury is not positive, for it produces bad 
fruit of idolatry.1 3 2  As Stuart and Garrett note, Hosea mirrors what Isaiah

Jeremiah continues this chain of application (2:21). The prophet talks

Ezekiel is the last prophet who uses the vine image (Ezek. 15:1-8; 17:8; 
19:10). The people of his time asked how God could exile those within Je­

(Ezek. 15:8).1 3 6  The terse response may puzzle the reader. How does Eze­

Ezekiels response.1 3 7  The prophets consistently used the vine to describe 
how God uniquely chose and treated Israel historically. Hence, it is a fit­
ting metaphor to answer Israels question. Ezekiels development of the 
metaphor highlights how bad Israel had become. Israel is not merely spiri­
tually fruitless or even a foreign vine, they are just the useless wood of the 
vine (Ezek. 15:3). Consistently, if Israel is so decrepit, then all they are fit 
for is burning and judgment. To answer Israels question, Ezekiels use of 
the metaphor asks “How could God not judge the vine?” and “What else 
can God do with the vine/Israel?” The background of the prophetic logic 
of the vine fills out and makes sense of Ezekiel s brief statement. In fact, 
both Block and Alexander note that to grasp Ezekiels response, one must 
have the entire logic of the vine in view. Ezekiels assertions assume what 
the prophets before him have discussed about the vine.1 3 8

--- Page 84 ---
83The Prophet as Exegete and Theologian
The vine metaphor showcases the prophet as exegete and theologian. 
The prophets maintain Asaphs original metaphor both in wording and in 
how he described Israels beginnings. The prophets also follow how their 
predecessors developed the vine metaphor. Thus, the Old Testament writ­
ers were careful exegetes of antecedent revelation. At the same time, they 
were theologians. This deals with how they handled the significance or 
ramifications of Asaphs metaphor. Asaph established the metaphor of the 
vine as a symbol of Israels status throughout all of time. Accordingly, later 
prophets show how that works out. They abide in the way Asaph wanted 
the metaphor to work. They continue the tradition Asaph began. Within 
this, they do not contradict one another but build upon each other to for­
mulate a biblical theology of Israels spiritual decline. The fact the New 
Testament will powerfully use the vine imagery (Matt. 21:33-36; John 
15:1) reflects the theological weight of what these prophets have done. 
They are theologians in their own right.
Intertextuality of Genesis 3:15
This is perhaps the locus classicus of Old Testament intertextuality and 
for good reason.1 3 9  It demonstrates the prophets starting with Moses were 
profound thinkers. They upheld the meaning of prior revelation, and yet 
had incredible sophistication in the way they developed the theological 
significance of a text.
The context of Genesis 3:15 deals with the fall of humanity s first parents 
into sin. Adams disobedience leads to all sorts of consequences on the cre­
ated order (Gen. 3:14-19). However, within this catastrophe, God makes 
an important promise: He will not allow the serpent to win. Contrary to 
the serpents desire to manipulate the woman, God establishes perpetual 
conflict or enmity between those two, as well as their offspring (Gen. 3:15a). 
An individual from the womans descendants will arise to crush the serpent s 
head, while the serpent itself will only give a blow to the heel (Gen. 3:15b).1 4 0
Most scholars accept Genesis 3:15 has the general idea of good ulti­
mately triumphing over evil.1 4 1  However, some question whether there is

84 The Prophetic Hermeneutic
a messianic element within this verse.1 4 2  They wonder whether Adam or 
Eve would have really been able to comprehend such an advanced idea.1 4 3  
This brings us back to the question of “Who were the prophets?” How 
sophisticated were they?
In this case, we should ask if Moses, the author of the Pentateuch, un­
derstood Genesis 3:15 to entail a sort of messianic component.1 4 4  A variety 
of factors suggest this:
1. The grammar of Genesis 3:15 indicates an individual is involved. 
The idea of “offspring” or “seed” (jn_j) seems to be corporate re­
ferring to continuing generations. Nonetheless, the parallel line 
(“he will crush your head”) juxtaposes this corporate notion with 
a distinctly singular pronoun (Kin). Syntactical studies have es­
tablished that when “seed” takes a singular pronoun, it refers not 
to something plural but to an individual.1 4 5  Accordingly, Genesis 
3:15 does not merely speak of a group of people but about a per­
son, specifically one who will represent the group. This is consis­
tent with a messianic understanding of the verse.
2. The parallelism in Genesis 3:15 indicates that this individual is 
the representative champion of his people. The offspring of the 
woman crushes not an offspring of the serpent but the serpent it­
self. This one-on-one conflict reinforces the idea of an individuals 
involvement. It also depicts that person as the leader of the peo­
ple. Just as the serpent is the champion of one group, so the Seed 
is the true leader of his line. He is their climactic representative. 
That is the notion of corporate solidarity.1 4 6  Such parallelism does 
not describe merely a generic generational struggle of good and 
evil. Rather, it depicts a generational struggle culminating with a 
final showdown between the respective heads of each group. The 
winner will be the Seed, even though he will experience some suf­
fering in the process as his heel will be bruised. These notions of 
an individual, climactic champion point to a messianic figure.

3. Moving from Genesis 3:15 to the rest of the book, Mosess focus 
upon genealogies argues he was conscious about the seed promise 
in Genesis 3:15. Moses structures Genesis with a series of gene­
alogies (e.g., 5:1; 6:9; 10:1; 11:27; 25:19). Alexander and Dempster 
rightly contend this exhibits the author s concern with birth and 
generations, a concern that seems to stem from the whole motif of 
the woman having offspring in Genesis 3:15-16.1 4 7
4. Mosess use of “seed” throughout Genesis also argues he under­
stood Genesis 3:15 to have a messianic component. In Genesis, 
God associates blessing with Abrahams seed (Gen. 22:18). This 
blessing refers to the reversal of the Falls curse and restoration 
of creation blessings since that is the only way blessings has been 
used to this point (cf. Gen. 1:22, 28; 2:3; 5:2; 9:1).1 4 8  Such a rever­
sal conceptually matches the reversal promised in Genesis 3:15. 
Hence, Moses, throughout Genesis, at least associates “seed” with 
ideas consistent with Genesis 3:15. Even more, Moses uses “seed” 
to refer to an individual who brings such victory and change. 
Genesis 22:17b-18 and 24:60 proclaim the “seed” will possess the 
gates of his enemies. This is in the context of the Abrahamic cov­
enant and the blessing to all the nations (cf. 22:17a). One might be 
tempted to think that the “seed” in these verses refer to a nation or 
group of people.1 4 9  However, the verbs (and pronoun) surround­
ing the word “seed” are in the singular. As discussed above, that 
indicates the “seed” here is singular and not plural. Moses rein­
forces that he understands “seed” to refer to a singular champion. 
Interestingly enough, these texts share the same grammar, same 
terminology (“seed”), and same motif of conquering the enemies 
as Genesis 3:15. For this reason, Alexander suggests that Genesis 
22:17-18 and 24:60 elaborate on that promise.1 5 0  With that, later 
usages of “seed” confirm a messianic understanding of the verse.
5. Mosess focus upon individuals and royalty throughout Genesis 
also supports a messianic idea of Genesis 3:15. The prophet con­
centrates on individuals like Noah, Abraham, Isaac, Jacob, and Ju­
dah. If Genesis 3:15 was merely corporate in nature, why does he 
pay so much attention to individuals? Furthermore, he casts cer­
tain people in an Adamic light. For instance, God charges Noah

with the same command he gave Adam to be fruitful and multiply

is the very idea discussed in Genesis 3:15. All of this argues that 
Moses was aware of the messianic idea of Genesis 3:15 and shapes 
the narrative of Genesis around it.
The evidence above suggests Genesis 3:15 has a messianic com­
ponent within it. Understanding this not only provides an important 
foundation for analyzing how later prophets use this text but also makes 
an important point about Moses. Moses knew what he was doing. He 
did not record Genesis 3:15 in theological ignorance but with theologi­
cal awareness. He formulates his entire narrative to expound upon this. 
Granted, others will fill in more detail about these ideas; nevertheless, 
they fill in what is initially present in Mosess intent in Genesis 3:15. 
That becomes the grounds for why the prophets will be able to provide 
further detail concerning that prophecy.

cuss Genesis 3:15. This solidifies what he has said in Genesis and further 
sets up for what later prophets will say. For instance, Balaam prophesies

Later writings pick up on this. The book of Ruth also has a genealogy, 
which matches the ones found in Genesis (cf. Gen. 5:1; cf. 38: 28-30). Ac­
cordingly, just as the genealogies in Genesis showed how the line of the 
seed progressed, so the genealogy in Ruth continues that discussion and 
demonstrates that the line of the ultimate seed winnows down to the fam­
ily of David. The author of 2 Samuel confirms this connection. In the Da- 
vidic covenant, God promises David a seed (in _ T , 2 Sam. 7:12). The Hebrew 
word in 2 Samuel 7:12 is the same as the one used in Genesis 3:15 and sub­
sequently through Genesis (22:17-18; 24:60). Even more, 2 Samuel 7:12 
shares the same kind of grammar found in those previous passages. All 
of them have seed in a corporate context but with clearly singular verbs 
and nouns. These features put these passages in parallel. On top of this, we 
have already discussed the various allusions in the Davidic covenant with 
past promises. Second Samuel 7 has a highly intertextual context. Given 
all of these factors, it is likely the author desires us to make a connection 
between Genesis 3:15 and the Davidic covenant. Accordingly, 2 Samuel 
7:12 continues the line of thought of these passages and Davids line now 
becomes the bearer of the promise of Genesis 3:15.

ideal kings worldwide dominion (v. 8). The word “dominion” (rm) is the

24:19 as well as Adams rule in Genesis 1:26-28. The prophetic writer seems 
to identify the ultimate Davidic king as a second or true Adam. This appears 
especially to be the case since his rule is not merely over Israel but “to the 
ends of the earth” (pjK^osarní?, 72:8), just as Adams dominion was to be 
over the created order (cf. Gen. 1:26-27). In addition, the very next verse of 
Psalm 72 discusses how all the enemies will bow down before the ultimate 
king and lick the dust like a serpent. This resonates with what is depicted 
in Genesis 3:15. Psalm 72 seems to see the Davidic rule in terms of Genesis 
3:15 and helps the reader to see the beauty and breadth of its fulfillment.
Later on, Micah describes the ultimate dominion of the second David 
in terms of Genesis 3:15. Every foe will be like the serpent whom the true 
king will defeat (Mic. 7:17). This reiterates the picture found in Psalm 72 
which came from Genesis 3:15. In addition, Isaiah affirms this coincides 
with Gods own conquest of the serpent, the Leviathan, whom he will slay 
like he promised in Genesis 3:15 (cf. Isa. 27:1).1 6 1  Such supernatural vic­
tory is in the context of Israels restoration (cf. Isa. 27:2-13), the judgment 
of the wicked (26:21), triumph over death (25:8), and even resurrection 
(26:19). Genesis 3:15 depicted the key ideas within Gods victory over evil. 
Isaiah helps us to see the dynamics of such a triumph in detail.
Did the Old Testament writers misunderstand or misapply Genesis 
3:15? Far from it, the prophets had immense exegetical accuracy and theo-

logical profundity. Starting from Moses, the prophets knew Genesis 3:15 
was a complex promise. It established Gods agenda for the battle of good 
and evil, culminating in a seed/champion who would vanquish the evil one. 
Later prophets do not change those ideas but flesh out the ramifications of 
those ideas on redemptive history.1 6 2  With that, Genesis 3:15 sets up a theol­
ogy of how a Davidic descendent will have complete victory over Satan, sin, 
death, and all Gods enemies, securing peace for the entire cosmos as the new 
Adam. Such work showcases how the prophets are profound theologians.
Synthesis
Exodus to new Exodus,1 6 3  David to new David, Davidic covenant 
and Edenic rest, and the concept of a second Adam—all of these sound 
like biblical theology and are, in fact, topics within that discipline.1 6 4  The 
prophets were not merely good exegetes who understood the meaning of 
a text well but also good theologians who understood the significance of 
a text. They comprehended texts had a variety of implications and, under 
the guidance of the Spirit, honed in on certain theological implications 
and further defined them. In doing so, they do not provide some “hid­
den meaning” of prior revelation but rather further explain the details and 
ramifications of the meaning that is already present, within the param­
eters of significance the original author established. As a result, they de­
velop profound theological truths and set up for later prophets to further 
explicate those theological ideas.
Some may object to the prophet as theologian because I have not pro­
vided comprehensive examples. However, Old Testament theology itself is 
a testimony to this reality. Old Testament theologians use intertextuality as 
part of their method to trace the progressing themes within the first canon. 
House states part of his method is to note the intertextual connections be­
tween books as part of the unfolding canon.1 6 5  Waltke states, “Understanding 
intertextuality helps the theologian trace the trajectory of a doctrines devel­
opment within the Bible.”1 6 6  He also mentions this is “essential to writing a 
biblical theology.”1 6 7  Although these observations are made from the biblical 
theological end, the inverse is also true. Just as biblical theology in part de­
rives from intertextuality, intertextuality indicates the prophets were biblical 
theologians. While we have discussed Exodus to new Exodus or David to 
new David, this is not merely us “ doing biblical theology” so to speak but 
rather discovering the prophets themselves were the first biblical theologians.

Thus, the observations and methodology of Old Testament theologians 
lends more overarching evidence that the biblical writers were by their own 
right theologians who developed the significance of certain texts of the Scrip­
tures. All of our findings in Old Testament theology are really a discovery of 
the prophets biblical theological thoughts and reasoning. Accordingly, while 
the examples above are not comprehensive, scholars who have gone before 
us help to show that the “prophet as theologian” has substantial legitimacy.
PRESENTING THE PROPHETIC  
RATIONALE AND HERMENEUTIC
We return and ask ourselves “Who are the prophets? How did they think 
and how did they write?” This chapter hopefully challenges us to have a 
high view of these authors. I have argued that the Old Testament authors 
were profound scriptural thinkers, as Hamilton and others have suggest­
ed. The prophets were immersed in Scripture. As a result, they used it 
accurately in various situations and developed it theologically. They could 
apply Gods Word to their current situation (e.g., covenant disobedience 
and failure) as well as advance the theological themes and concepts there­
in via new revelation. In doing so, they discussed topics like creation to 
new creation; Exodus to new Exodus; a second Adam; a new David; and 
the majesty, grace, holiness, and glory of God. In sum, the prophets were 
exegetes who carefully understood the Scripture, as well as theologians 
who profoundly expounded upon its ramifications.
What drives this entire process is a precise hermeneutical core. Funda­
mentally, we observed the prophets read the Scriptures meticulously. They 
knew past texts from their general ideas down to their individual words and 
everything in between (meaning). They also had an immense awareness of 
the implications of these passages (significance). They knew ideas had con­
sequences and were aware of particular theological consequences already 
discussed and defined by previous writers. The depth of their understand­
ing of a texts meaning and significance—their hermeneutic—provides a 
profound platform for progressive revelation. It provides the rich concepts, 
implications, and framework from which the prophets continue the work 
of their predecessors, enhancing the details about a texts implications on 
theology and redemptive history via new revelation. This sets up for others 
to build upon their work, which results in chains of Old Testament texts that 
richly dig into the particular ramifications of a certain passage.
This brings up an important qualification. To be sure, the prophets had 
to receive new revelation so they could develop theological concepts found 
in earlier texts. New revelation furnished further information and details 
about the implications of previous revelation. Such activity is part of the 
prophets ministry as scriptural authors. Although that plays a part in how 
they thought and wrote, we should distinguish that aspect of their rationale 
from their hermeneutic. The hermeneutical question of this chapter is not

--- Page 92 ---
91The Prophet as Exegete and Theologian
whether the prophets gave new revelation (they do) but the hermeneutical 
relationship between old and new revelation. How do the prophets read?
As we observed, new revelation does not find hidden meaning in an­
tecedent texts or change the meaning of a text. We noted this in dealing 
with the Davidic covenant, the vine, and Genesis 3:15. Later prophets do 
not change those ideas. Rather, they show (via new revelation) since these 
ideas are true, then certain consequences will come about in redemptive 
history and theology. New revelation thereby builds upon the implica­
tions the text already has. This is why progressive revelation fits together 
so smoothly. New revelation fits and works within the significance of prior 
texts as opposed to twisting their original ideas or taking them in random 
directions.1 6 8  Hence, the presence of new revelation does not overturn or 
distort the prophets hermeneutical fidelity. Rather, the cohesiveness of 
progressive revelation seems to rely upon it.
In essence, this chapter has endeavored to show the reality of a pro­
fundity driven by precision. The prophets were immense biblical think­
ers and writers because they were so accurate in handling the meaning 
and significance of Scripture. That is what made them good exegetes and 
theologians.
At this point, some may object to these sweeping statements since we 
have not covered every instance of Old Testament intertextuality. For that 
reason, I will qualify these statements by saying that the evidence suggests 
these conclusions. However, we can also recall the examples above do span 
the entire canon. They are not isolated to one prophet or to even one era 
of Israels history. Rather, in the studies of this chapter, we have observed 
the same methodology throughout the canon. Furthermore, we observed 
that the role of the prophet was to uphold the law, as opposed to decon­
struct (Deut. 13, 18). This too more broadly supports that the prophets 
were good exegetes. Even more, as we have observed, the Old Testament 
seems to depend upon the notion of the prophet as an accurate interpreter 
of Scripture. After all, if they varied from what the law or other revelation 
meant, then this would completely alter the theological unity of the first 
canon. Equally, we noted the discipline of Old Testament theology also 
depends upon intertextuality as a conduit for theology. That also attests to 
the widespread evidence for the prophets as theologians. On top of this,
168. This again reiterates the point of Beale, Kaiser, and Bock. See Beale, “Rejoinder to 
Steve Moyise,” 153-59; Kaiser, “Single Meaning, Unified Referents,” 88-89; Bock, 
“Evangelicals and the Use of the Old Testament in the New, Part 2,” 308-10, 317. 
This particularly supports the framework advocated by Kaiser; nevertheless, Bock is 
extremely close for he too sees that new revelation fits within the significance of previ­
ous revelation. That is my point. The prophets established meaning with a parameter 
of implications, and new revelation works within that. Accordingly, while I appreciate 
Kaiser s articulation, my view is not that much different from the fundamental tenets 
of Bock, particularly if Bock would see development in terms of significance as op­
posed to meaning (which may be very well what he intends to say).

92 The Prophetic Hermeneutic
we have dealt with the major objections against my view. All of these fac­
tors thoroughly support the prophets were good exegetes and theologians. 
This notion is not just a result of a smattering of examples but actually a 
core reality that the Old Testament relies upon.
How does this help us in our quest for authorial logic? In evaluating 
the New Testament s use of the Old, having the complete picture of the Old 
Testament is essential. This chapter has argued the prophets were skillful 
and thoughtful in handling Gods Word. They began to put theological 
puzzle pieces together which provide a framework for the apostles. When 
seeing the hermeneutical accuracy and biblical theological complexity of 
the prophets, the assertions of the apostles may not be as big of a jump 
as we might have thought. Perhaps some of the confusion over the New 
Testaments use of the Old stems from under-reading the Old Testament 
writers. Succinctly put, this chapter shows that the prophets do have a 
rationale and it is intertextual, precise, and sophisticated.
Along that line, the reality of the prophetic hermeneutic should not 
only change how we view the prophets but also how we read them. Their 
exegetical precision reminds us why we take great care with Scripture. 
They paid attention to authorial intent not only on a general level but 
down to the very word. This grounds our exegetical method. Our exegeti­
cal attention to detail is not over-reading a text but reflects the prophetic 
hermeneutic, how they believed Scripture communicated. Moreover, the 
idea that the prophets are theologians elevates how we view their writ­
ings. Perhaps our view of the prophets has made us read their works as 
simplistic. We may see the history of Kings or Chronicles as merely a sort 
of recounting of events with some moral lesson. We may see poems as 
merely a sort of encouragement for our souls. These ideas have some merit 
to them; however, there is so much more occurring in the Old Testament. 
The prophetic hermeneutic attests every story, poem, and prophecy is an 
advancement of theology, one that shapes how we understand biblical and 
systematic theology, one that reflects Gods person, promises, and plan; 
one that in turn shapes our lives. We can and should find theology in the 
Old Testament. We just need to see how the prophets writings incorporate 
past revelation to produce such theological development.

--- Page 94 ---
4
THE PROPHETIC HERMENEUTIC
D ID  THE PROPHETS SPEAK BETTER THAN THEY KNEW , 
OR BETTER THAN W E GIVE THEM CREDIT FOR?
Id Testament intertextuality demonstrates the prophets were exegetes
and theologians. This chapter focuses upon an important dimen­
sion of that rationale: directionality. We initially observed this idea in the 
previous chapter, witnessing how chains of Old Testament texts move to 
the New. The prophets were aware of the theological implications of prior 
revelation and how their predecessors developed those ramifications. As 
a result, they wrote to continue that logic and advance theology for later 
authors to expound. In doing this, the prophets intentionally set a trajec­
tory toward the apostles.
This concept is not novel. It mirrors the ideas of Kaiser and Beale who 
speak of how the Old Testament writers “organically” developed prior rev­
elation. For them, the prophets advanced the concepts and ideas already 
found in earlier revelation toward the New Testament.1  Hamilton asserts 
the biblical writers wrote in light of a big picture and their contribution 
to Gods plan from creation to consummation.2  All of these scholars are 
speaking of the notion of progressive revelation and how the prophets 
intentionally participated in that. There is a way that the Old Testament 
intentionally moves to the New Testament such that the New Testament s 
use of the Old is a “natural” development of what has progressed. That is 
what I would like to further discuss in this chapter.

--- Page 95 ---
94 The Prophetic Hermeneutic
The need for additional discussion arises from the fact that not all are 
convinced. Certain scholars question whether the prophets meaning could 
ever match what the apostles concluded. They argue the prophets were not 
completely cognizant of complex theological concepts discussed in the New 
Testament.3  They assert the Old Testament writers were more focused on 
the issues of their day as opposed to the big picture of Gods plan.4  Based 
upon these factors, these scholars contend the prophets did not intend their 
texts to be used in the future. Their writings were more about the past or for 
a certain audience and were never purposed to have ramifications beyond 
that. Instead, for the prophets to connect with the apostles, the prophets 
“spoke better than they knew.” This results in one of two major ways to look 
at the New Testaments use of the Old.5  Some contend the hermeneutics of 
the apostles contemporaries account for the shift in meaning.6  Others ap­
peal to sensus plenior. That view claims the New Testament authors explain 
the “full meaning” of the text which before was unknown.7  In either case, 
one must wonder whether a Christian hermeneutic then should try to find 
“fuller meanings” of the Old Testament.8
Because of these objections, we need to cover the notion of direction­
ality more. The following chapter intends to turn these notions of pro­
phetic ignorance on their head. The reality of the prophetic hermeneutic

Did the Prophets Speak Better than They Knew, or Better than We Give Them Credit For?95
breaks down these stereotypes. It shows the prophets knew a lot. They 
knew past revelation with its theological sophistication. They also knew 
how it fit into Gods plan, and wrote not only to their contemporaries but 
to that greater agenda. They were able to provide the adequate theological 
substance for later writers to develop, and intended for them to do so.
To be clear, I am not suggesting the prophets knew everything that 
would ensue from their writings. They did not know exhaustively how the 
apostles would use their text. Nevertheless, my point is the prophets had 
enough complexity and awareness to legitimately set up later authors to 
use their writing. As a result, when the apostles come and handle the Old 
Testament, their use of previous revelation fits well within the intended 
framework established by the prophets. In sum, the prophets had a re­
demptive historical logic that establishes their texts in a trajectory which 
prepares well for the New Testament.
If this is true, then we have an entirely different situation than the 
one suggested by these other scholars. Instead of the prophets writ­
ing better than they knew, the evidence may indicate that the prophets 
wrote better than we give them credit for. This provides another argu­
ment that the prophets theological logic forms the foundation of the 
apostles final revelation.
KNOWLEDGE OF PAST REVELATION
Post-Enlightenment thinking has questioned the ability of the Old Testament 
prophets to discuss advanced theological ideas.9  The issue of messianic the­
ology is case in point. Some argue messianic expectations only came about 
in the postexilic period when Israel looked forward to the restoration of the 
Davidic dynasty.1 0  Such a late development makes it impossible that texts 
prior to that time referred to or prophesied about Messiah. When looking at 
the “literal sense” of certain Old Testament passages, certain commentators 
conclude they could not have spoken of Christ. One of the key instances of 
this revolves around an example used in the last chapter: the intertextual- 
ity of Genesis 3:15. Certain scholars argue that Genesis 3:15 only refers to 
the continued struggle between good and evil. Similarly, they contend that 
passages associated with that text, like Genesis 49:10 and Numbers 24:10, 
pertain merely to the Davidic dynasty as a whole.1 1  They also assert Psalm 
110, traditionally a messianic psalm and another text linked with Genesis 
3:15, never had the Messiah in view but primarily refers to Solomon.1 2

In light of these conclusions, the reason for the prophets speaking 
“better than they know” becomes apparent. If the prophets did not even 
comprehend particular realities (e.g., Messiah), how could they discuss 
those issues and set up for the New Testament to talk about those theo­
logical concepts? They must have written “better than they knew,” so that 
the New Testament could fill in the gaps the prophets were unaware of.1 3
Conversely, were the prophets so ignorant? The very existence of in- 
tertextuality demands the prophets knew something. The previous chap­
ter addresses this point so I will not belabor the entire discussion here 
other than to offer two reminders. First, the last chapter demonstrated 
that on a broad level, the Old Testament writers were well acquainted with 
previous revelation. They knew specific words and phrases from particu­
lar texts (e.g., Davidic covenant promises and the metaphor of the vine). 
They could follow the rationale concerning particular ramifications of 
those passages and incorporate that into their own writings. This gener­
ates a level of sophistication that argues against the prophetic ignorance. 
They knew and could think through a lot.
Second, we witnessed such sophistication in our discussion of Genesis 
3:15. The intertextuality of that text brings up several points that counter 
the notion of prophetic ignorance about the Messiah. For instance, if Moses 
had no conception of a messianic individual, why does he focus on indi­
viduals in his own narratives? Why does he characterize them as a second 
Adam figure? Why does Genesis prophesy about an individual (Shiloh) who 
rules the world and restores creation (Gen. 49:10)? Why does Balaam de­
scribes an individual whose rule mirrors Adams (Num. 24:17-19). If Moses 
is focused merely on a generic discussion of Israels monarchy, why does he 
focus upon an individual, eschatological, cosmic ruler? His description is 
the polar opposite of anything broad, generic, and historical.1 4
Along this line, Davids and Micahs use of Genesis 3:15 makes it dif­
ficult to argue that it refers to David or Solomon or merely the Davidic

dynasty in general. David speaks of something more grandiose than him­

greater theologians than we give them credit for. We may have made as­

Therefore, the prophetic hermeneutic counters the notion the proph­
ets wrote better than they knew in that the prophets knew a lot more than 
we give them credit for. Their exegetical and theological work in the Old 
Testament shows they wrote about sophisticated theological issues (e.g., 
Messiah) based upon what their predecessors had said. This is what Kaiser 
reminds us in discussing the notion of “antecedent revelation.” He points 
out that when we examine how the prophets drew from previous revela­
tion, we can more clearly see how theological advanced they were.1 8  Ac­
cordingly, they do not speak better than they knew but speak to bigger 
matters than we give them credit for.
KNOWLEDGE OF GOD'S PLAN AND THE PRESENT
Another major support for the prophets “speaking better than they know” 
stems from the assumption that Old Testament writers had a sort of tun­
nel vision and only spoke to their immediate audience. From that presup­
position, certain scholars argue that Psalm 110 can only refer to Solomon 
because Davids words must discuss someone in his own time.1 9  Psalm 2 
originally related to a historical king and not a future ideal fulfillment of 
the Davidic dynasty for the same reason.2 0  Similarly, Isaiahs prophecy of 
the “virgin birth” (7:14) must deal with someone in his time period other­
wise the oracle would have little relevance to his contemporary situation.2 1  
Accordingly, the New Testament must fill in the gaps of their Old Testa­
ment counterparts, since they never thought that their words would have 
future repercussions. They only intended them for their current audience.
However, the hermeneutical activity of the prophets argues they could 
not have been so narrowly minded. The previous chapter already evi­
denced the prophets knew how redemptive history was unfolding. Their 
perspective was not so myopic. We can further bring out this redemptive 
historical perception. Passages that recount Israels history support the 
prophets were cognizant of Gods plan and the direction of history. These 
include the reflections of Moses (Deut. 1-3), Asaph (Ps. 78), the historical 
writers (1 Kings 8:1-62; 2 Kings 17:7-23), Daniel (Dan. 9:1-19), and the 
Levites in Nehemiah (Neh. 9:1-38). Such texts, which span the Old Testa­
ment story line, marshal up substantial evidence the prophets were aware 
of the greater plan of God. They wrote with a covenantal understanding of 
their past, had a consistent perspective of their present, and knew how to 
live in order to contribute to the whole of redemptive history. Thus, their 
writings intentionally extend beyond their original circumstances. They 
are designed to have a greater impact. The following discussion on these

Did the Prophets Speak Better than They Knew, or Better than We Give Them Credit For?99
passages attempts to demonstrate that the prophets had a bigger world­
view than some give them credit for.
Moses's Covenant Perspective
Mosess view of his writings is much larger than just the immediate 
situation. He understands his situation in light of Gods work in the past. 
The opening chapters of Deuteronomy convey Israels history up to this 
point. They remind Israel that in spite of their failures (Deut. 1:18-31), 
God had raised a new generation having great promise (Deut. 2:15). 
From Mosess perspective, Israel has a new opportunity to act with Gods 
blessing (Deut. 3:1-22).
This shapes Mosess viewpoint of the present. Israel must seize this 
opportunity to follow Gods statutes (Deut. 4:1-4), to love the Lord (Deut. 
6:4), and to take hold of Gods promises to their ancestors (Deut. 6:3). This 
would continue Gods plan for the nation (cf. Deut. 4:7). Mosess words are 
not in vacuum. They are with a view to Gods greater agenda.
Accordingly, Moses points Israel to the future. His writings commu­
nicate a framework for how the nation should continue to live after he 
died (Deut. 3:23-29). Within those laws and of note for this study, he an­
ticipates an upcoming king (Deut. 17:14-20). Moses also looks forward 
to what God will do with the nation eschatologically (Deut. 4:30-31; cf. 
30:1-10). Mosess writings have a directionality to them. His theology will 
become part of Israels worldview for events yet to come.2 2
Covenant Perspective in the Monarchic Period
Asaph continues this perspective with great fidelity. Asaph communi­
cates Israels past with descriptions similar if not at times identical to Mo­
sess. Both talk about how Israel did not believe (Deut. 1:18-32; Ps. 78:8, 
22), how Gods wrath was poured against that generation (Ps. 78:33-34; 
cf. Deut. 2:15), and how God was their rock (Ps. 78:35; cf. Deut. 32:4).2 3  
Asaph looks at history the same way Moses did.
For this reason, Asaph continues Mosess covenant perspective on 
events after Mosess death. The time of the Judges marks Israels depar­
ture from the Lord. God disciplines them according to curses listed in 
the Mosaic covenant (Ps. 78:62-63; cf. Deut. 32:25).2 4  However, Asaph 
observes how the Lord continues to move history forward to the Davidic

king (Ps. 78:68-70). Moses already set up for this by instituting the law of

This pertains to how people should live in the present. Asaph reminds

He exhorts them to obey the king and follow Gods law.2 5  This is the way 
for them to faithfully participate in redemptive history. Thus, Asaph main­
tains Mosess perspective on history and instructs people to play their part 
in Gods plan.
In addition, the historical writers share the same logic. Solomons prayer 
continues what Moses and Asaph have begun (1 Kings 8:1-62). He recounts 
the history Moses recorded and reiterates Asaphs perspective that God had 
desired to raise up a ruler over Israel (1 Kings 8:15-16; Ps. 78:70-72). This 
past perspective allows Solomon to comment upon the present. In light of 
Gods work and promises, Solomon asks the Lord to continue a gracious re­
lationship with his people (1 Kings 8:28-53). This includes Gods forgiveness 
when he curses Israel based upon the Mosaic covenant (1 Kings 8:37-39; 
cf. Deut. 28:21-23). Even more, Solomons requests have bearing upon the 
future. He appeals to God to restore the nation after he sends it into exile 
(1 Kings 8:46-50).2 6  How did Solomon know about this? Solomons appeal is 
entirely based upon Deuteronomy 30:1 -6.2 7  By incorporating Deuteronomy 
30 into his prayer, the king, like Asaph, upholds Mosess perspective and not 
only thinks about the present but the future.2 8
Covenant Perspective in the Exilic Period
Accordingly, those in exile recall how the past would help them 
interpret their situation. Daniel, reading Jeremiah, knows Israel should 
return to the land soon (Dan. 9:2). This moves Daniel to pray (9:3). 
The details of his prayer echo verses from Solomons own prayer. Both 
discuss Gods grace, forgiveness, and restoration based upon the tem­
ple (Dan. 9:16-17; 1  Kings 8:46-50). At the dedication of the temple, 
Solomon asks that God would hear the prayer of those who would be

Like with his predecessors, this moves to the future. Daniel desires

of exile.2 9  God answers with the famous “seventy-weeks” prophecy, which 
lays out the significant events of the future to make atonement and bring 
in everlasting righteousness (Dan. 9:24). Once again, Daniel knows the 
big picture, and that allows him to connect past and present which moves 
forward to the future.
Covenant Perspective in the Postexilic Period
Even after the exile, the prophets continue the same covenant per­
spective of history. The writer of Nehemiah provides a comprehensive 
review of Israels history (9:1-38). This record corresponds with what 
was written previous. The Levites in Nehemiah recount how Israel en­
tered exile because of their stubborn rebellion and failure to listen to 
Gods Word (Neh. 9:16-17, 26, 30).3 0  The wording of this matches what 
Moses (Deut. 9:6; 30:1-6), Asaph (Ps. 72:8), Solomon (1 Kings 8:46-50), 
the historical writers (2 Kings 17:13-14), and Daniel (9:6, 11-12) said. 
At the same time, there is hope. Daniel had prayed for the Lord to re­
turn Israel from exile, based upon Gods grace and compassion (Dan. 
9:9,18). The Levites state based upon Gods grace and compassion, they 
are in the land (Neh. 9:31). The Levites describe the present situation in 
light of the past.
For this very reason, the Levites know things are not right. Israel has 
come back to the land but is still in sin. They are still ruled by foreign 
powers (Neh. 9:37). As a result, their covenantal viewpoint leads them to 
conclude they are “slaves today” (Neh. 9:36). House observes the state­
ment alludes back to the days of the Exodus and wilderness wanderings.3 1  
The text confirms it by casting Nehemiahs time period as having the same 
wickedness as that point of history (cf. 9:16-17, 34). The past thoroughly 
influences how the prophets view the present.
Consequently, this compels those in Nehemiahs day to look to the 
future. Gods graciousness from past to the present demands the remnant 
renew the covenant (Neh. 9:38). This looks forward to the fulfillment of 
Gods covenant promises made in the past. They celebrate the Feast of

new wilderness wanderings, they expect that God will be faithful to end 
this sojourn as well. Israel understands where Gods plan has been and live 
in light of where it is going.3 3
Synthesis
I hope to have shown a clear pattern in this discussion. The prophets 
know the big picture. They knew the past per their predecessors and ap­
plied that perspective to the present with a view to the future. This is not 
just in one part of Israels history but in all of its existence. I have been 
selective in illustrating the nature of this paradigm, but we could reiterate 
this same past-to-future mentality in people like David (cf. 2 Sam. 7:1-14), 
Isaiah (1:2-18), Hosea (12:1-14), Micah (1:2-16), and Malachi (2:1-12). 
The “big picture” is not an isolated viewpoint. It is a key part of the biblical 
writers rationale.
This highlights the prophets as both exegetes and theologians as they 
maintained the exact viewpoint of the past articulated by their predeces­
sors and yet could see the theological implications of the covenant on the 
present and future. This again supports the previous chapter s assertion.
Nevertheless, we return to the issue at hand. Did the prophets speak 
only to their current situation? Did they originally intend for their words 
to have no future ramifications or significance? Did they have a sort of 
tunnel vision? The above discussion shows the prophets awareness was 
much broader. They were very mindful of their redemptive historical con­
text. They knew how their ministry fit in Gods plan. Although they did 
not comprehensively understand everything in Gods agenda, they knew 
where Gods plan was generally heading. In light of this, they not only 
wrote for the present but also to contribute to that entire plan. They wrote 
knowing their writings would have ramifications on redemptive history, 
and they looked forward to how that would play out in the future. In that 
way, they set up for others to continue their logic and flesh out the ramifi­
cations of their writings. Their texts have a directionality that intentionally

era as progressing forward to the Davidic king (cf. 1 Kings 8:15-16; Ps. 78:70-72). 
It appears those in the postexilic period were looking forward to the new David 
to come, as opposed to the current foreign rulers who had dominion (Neh. 9:37). 
Israel looked forward to a time when a new king would arise and truly bring them 
home. This too reinforces their forward-looking perspective and awareness of pre­
cise realities they anticipated.

goes beyond their time. The prophets speak to greater situations than we 
give them credit for.
In observing these types of passages, House states the conclusion clearly:
Christian theologians have long believed that the Old Testament can exist as 
a discrete witness, but also that it can be read as literature that leads naturally 
to the New Testament. It is important to admit that it is not blatantly obvi­
ous to all but the most obstinate reader that such is the case. Other reading 
strategies can lead to other conclusions, yet it is just as important to assert 
that reading the New Testament in light of and as a continuation of the Old 
Testament is not a forced pattern.34
ANTICIPATION OF THE FUTURE
The original question of this chapter deals with the prophets ability to 
prepare for the future applications made by their New Testament coun­
terparts. We have argued against two major presuppositions that com­
pel one to accept the notion the prophets “spoke better than they knew.” 
The apostles do not need to force complex theological ideas into the Old 
Testament because the prophets already understood theology and wrote 
theologically. The apostles also do not need to force the Old Testament to 
speak to later significant situations, since the prophets knew their writings 
would impact redemptive history. All of this supports that the prophets 
set up the apostles.
We should put this in more precise hermeneutical terms. The prophets 
intended their texts to have implications upon progressive revelation.3 5  They 
did not know exhaustively how this would work out. However, they under­
stood the general direction and/or ideas that would be involved. This is es­
pecially the case when that direction has been focused and refined through 
repeated intertextual use. Hence, when later authors finalize and define 
those implications further, they are not finding a hidden meaning (sensus 
plenior) or using cultural hermeneutical methods to derive it (contempo­
rary hermeneutic). Rather, the ideas and implications are already there and 
are primed and ready for subsequent authors to use and develop.
A variety of factors support this assertion on an initial level:
1. Quite a few examples we have discussed show how the proph­
ets arranged for later writers to comment on their texts as well 
as a framework for doing so. Whether Asaph’ s vine imagery, the
34. House, “Old Testament Narrative,” 43.
35. To be clear, these are not the only implications they intended their texts to have. Nev­
ertheless, we are focusing upon this subset of the significance of their writings. We 
are dealing with the implications of Old Testament passages that set up for the New 
Testament writers.

--- Page 105 ---
104 The Prophetic Hermeneutic
working out of Genesis 3:15, or the Davidic covenant and the new 
David, the hermeneutical pattern is the same. The prophets knew 
previous revelation and built upon certain theological implica­
tions of those texts with new revelation, preparing for those who 
would develop those ramifications further. With that, later elabo­
ration of their texts are well within the prophets anticipation and 
intent. The Old Testament writers establish an ample theological 
pattern and paradigm for later writers to use.
2. The prophets viewpoint of Scripture and thereby their own writ­
ings confirms this. The prophets claim sacred writ itself is profitable 
for all generations of Gods people (Ps. 19:7-10; 119:89-91). They 
intended future peoples to apply their work to new circumstances.
3. The prophets explicitly state their messages extends to people be­
yond their time. References to a generation yet born (Ps. 22:30- 
31), generations yet to come (Ps. 78:6), or childrens children (Ps. 
103:7) all indicate a forward-looking perspective. In these cases, 
the biblical writers actually intend their current writings to di­
rectly relate to specific future situations.
4. Certain genres facilitate this proleptic outlook. For example, the 
genre of law not only has jurisdiction over the present genera­
tion but also over the ones to come (cf. Deut. 4:10, 25-30; 5:3). 
Poetry was meant to be sung by subsequent generations (cf. 
Deut. 31:19; Ps. 4:1; Hab. 3:19). Of course prophecy (see below) 
anticipates the future.
5. Along that line and perhaps most obvious, the very nature of 
promises and prophecy demand the prophets looked ahead. These 
statements reveal what God will do in the future. The proph­
ets use these claims to exhort their hearers to hope in the Lord 
(Hab. 3:16-19) and to wait for his future work (Mic. 7:7). They 
even look forward to future messengers from the Lord (cf. Deut. 
18:15; 34:10; Mai. 3:1; 4:4-5 [Heb., 3:23-24; cf. Isa. 40:3). They 
knew progressive revelation would continue. Based upon Gods 
promises, they prayed and knew their work was not in vain (Isa. 
64:1-12). Prophecy and promises illustrate the prophets thought 
and wrote well beyond their immediate circumstances, even as 
they addressed their contemporaries. In fact, they used prophecy 
and promise to point their audience to hope in Gods future work.
These brief observations point out the prophets wrote with an aware­
ness their texts would have bearing upon later situations and theologi­
cal issues. They used texts with that assumption and wrote with that

--- Page 106 ---
Did the Prophets Speak Better than They Knew, or Better than We Give Them Credit For?105
assumption as their own statements indicate. Again, this does not demand 
exhaustive knowledge of the future (although in some cases, like direct 
prophecy, they knew quite a bit). Rather, the main thrust of this argument 
is to show that prophets intend for their writings to have ramifications 
beyond them. Old Testament texts have a forward-looking perspective, a 
directionality that sets up for the New Testament s use of the Old.
CASE STUDIES
While deductive arguments are helpful, more extensive case studies fur­
ther demonstrate how directionality works in the prophetic hermeneu­
tic. Case studies also garner additional support for the idea. There is no 
way to be comprehensive on this point. Thankfully, other works have 
come out to deal with the following examples and more. The commen­
tary on the New Testaments use of the Old edited by Carson and Beale 
is foremost in this.3 6  That volume and others illustrate that I am not the 
only one to have observed the prophets directionality, as laid out in the 
discussion below. Even more, their works demonstrate that intentional 
trajectories from Old to New are, in numerous instances, beyond what 
I can cover here.
Nevertheless, what I can cover is hopefully strategic. I have deliber­
ately chosen some of the most difficult instances of the New Testaments 
use of the Old for study. They also are some of the primary examples cited 
by other views like sensus plenior and contemporary hermeneutics. If the 
prophetic hermeneutic can resolve these harder examples, then perhaps 
the prophetic hermeneutic/intertextuality is part of an effective solution 
for other tricky cases of the New Testaments use of the Old.
Hosea 11:1
Matthews use of Hosea 11:1 is a classically cited example of the New 
Testaments misuse of the Old. The sensus plenior view often claims this as 
a support for hidden meaning.3 7  Matthew states Jesuss escape from Herod 
is a fulfillment of “Out of Egypt, I called my son” (Hos. 11:1; Matt. 2:15). 
However, Hosea 11:1 originally deals with the Exodus. Scholars ask two 
major questions about this text. First, did Hosea know his words would be 
applied to something future when they seem to refer to the past? Second, 
would Hosea ever think that his text pertains to the Messiah, since it origi­
nally talks about Israel? This discussion will tackle both of these questions.
36. Beale and Carson, Commentary on the New Testament Use of the Old Testament See  
also Archer and Chirichigno, Old Testament Quotations in the New Testament; Kaiser, 
The Use of the Old Testament in the New; Rydelnik, Messianic Hope; and Kaiser, The 
Messiah in the Old Testament.
37. Enns, Inspiration and Incarnation, 132; Thomas, “The New Testament Use of the Old 
Testament,” 251; Snodgrass, “The Use of the Old Testament in the New,” 36.

--- Page 107 ---
106 The Prophetic Hermeneutic
In dealing with the first issue, we need to admit that scholars are cor­
rect about the meaning of Hosea 11:1. The text refers to Gods unique love 
for Israel when it was helpless as a youth in Egypt. It describes his pow­
erful calling to draw Israel out of captivity. This all proves God had an 
exceptional love for his people—one that demonstrates that the nation is 
his son (cf. Ex. 4:22).
Nevertheless, it is not as simple as that. To be sure, Hosea 11:1 refers to 
the Exodus. However, that is only part of the equation. Equally important 
is how Hosea uses the Exodus in his argument. Beale and Garrett rightly 
raise this question.3 8  What was the prophets purpose in appealing to the 
Exodus? Before we jump to conclusions about Hosea 11:1 we should an­
swer this issue and the prophetic hermeneutic can help fill in those details.
Hosea 11:1 does not discuss the Exodus generically but specifically 
alludes to Exodus 4:22. Both passages mention “son” while discussing 
the Exodus. This is particularly distinctive since the Old Testament does 
not often call Israel “son” much less in the context of the Exodus.3 9  These 
unique similarities indicate a connection between these two texts. Ac­
cordingly, Garrett states, “This verse self-evidently refers to the exodus 
event, and in particular to Ex. 4:22, where Yahweh declares to Pharaoh, 
Israel is my firstborn son.’ ”4 0
Thus, the prophet Hosea uses Exodus 4:22. However, he is not the 
first prophet to do so. For example, Psalm 80 uses Exodus 4:22. The psalm 
employs the term ]? (son) in the context of the Exodus (cf. Ps. 80:8, 17 
[Heb., w. 9, 18]). Commentators recognize the allusion to Exodus 4:22 in 
this passage. Delitzsch notes, “It is Israel that is called ]? in v. 16, as being 
the son whom Jahve has called into being in Egypt, and then called out of 
Egypt to himself and solemnly declared to be his son on Sinai (Ex. 4:22, 
Hos. 11:l).”4 1  Why does the psalmist refer to Exodus 4:22? The biblical 
writer does so to appeal to Gods love so that the Lord would deliver Israel 
again (Ps. 80:17 [Heb., v. 18]). Just as God loved his son and rescued the 
nation the first time, so his love should drive another rescue. This pattern 
of “past deliverance to future redemption” is not novel. Other prophets 
also appeal to the Exodus motif with that exact reasoning (Pss. 74:10-15; 
77:14-15 [Heb., w. 15-16]).4 2
That same paradigm is in the immediate context of Hosea 11:1. The 
verse proclaims how God loved Israel, his son, as evidenced by the Exodus 
(Hos. 11:1). But Israel sinned and God will send them out of the land. 
However, Hosea says they will end up in Assyria and not Egypt (v. 5). 
The mention of Egypt is significant because it parallels Israels upcoming

39. Garrett, Hosea, 219-20.
40. Ibid., 219.
41. Keil and Delitzsch, Commentary on the Old Testament, 5:538-39.
42. Kidner, Psalms 1-72, 268, 278-79.

exile with their time formerly in Egypt. Hosea has made this comparison 
throughout the book (cf. Hos. 2:15 8:13; 9:3; 11:5; 12:13; 13:9).4 3  This par­
allel helps us see why Hosea talks about Gods love in the Exodus in the 
first place. Israels time in Egypt parallels Israels upcoming time in exile. 
That being the case, just as God loved Israel and delivered them the first 
time from Egypt, so he will deliver them a second time from the exile in 
a new Exodus. There will be a repeat performance. God states this at the 
end of Hosea 11. He cannot give up his son because he loves him (v. 8). 
Consequently, the Lord will deliver the nation out of exile (v. 11).4 4  Again, 
why does Hosea talk about the Exodus? It is not merely a lesson in history. 
In the flow of Hosea 11, it shows how Gods love in the first Exodus will 
drive a second Exodus from exile.
Thus, returning to our first question, did Hosea 11:1 at all anticipate a 
future situation? The answer is yes. To say Hosea 11:1 only looks back on 
history would be inaccurate. It would ignore the way the prophets up to 
this point in history have thought through Exodus 4:22 and the Exodus. 
Furthermore, it would ignore the same logic Hosea had in the immediate 
context of the passage. When seen in this vein, not looking at Hosea 11:1 
as the grounds for a future reality actually takes the text out of context, 
both immediately and intertextually.
This moves us to the second question: Does Hosea 11:1 at all relate to 
the Messiah? The answer, again, is yes. We noted above how the prophets 
used the Exodus to give Israel assurance that God would deliver again. 
However, that was not their only use of the Exodus. The prophets also ap­
plied the Exodus to Israels king. This is based upon the fact that the king is 
also Gods son (Pss. 2:7; 116:16). God declares this in the Davidic covenant 
(2 Sam. 7:13). The parallel between Gods son, Israel, and Gods Son, the 
Davidic king, shows they are equated. The king embodies the nation, a 
notion we call ‘corporate solidarity.” Accordingly, God deals with the king 
as he would the entire nation.4 5

in fear and trembling in the day of their deliverance (Hos. 3:5; 11:11).4 8

The Minor Prophets, 34-37. The new David passage is part of the oracles of hope/ 
salvation genre. These hope filled exhortations help to structure the book (1:10-11; 
2:14-23; 3:1-5; 6:1-3; 11:1-14:9). In addition to their optimistic nature, these sec­
tions of the book share quite a few similarities. They all discuss how Israel is called to 
repent and return to the land from which they were exiled (2:14-16; 3:5; 6:2-3; 14:1). 
They discuss how such deliverance corresponds with the Exodus (2:14-16; 11:1-11). 
These texts depict how Israel will return in trembling and fear (3:5; 11:11) and how 
the nation will pursue God (3:5; 6:2-3; 11:10; 14:1-4). The passages communicate 
how the Lord will revive and “resurrect” the nation (6:3; 13:13-14). All of these paral­
lels indicate that the sections can be read together because they all discuss the same 
eschatological outcome. This too shows how Hosea intentionally associates the new 
David (3:5) with Israels restoration (3:5; 6:2-3) and the new Exodus (11:1-11).

the structure of the book) argues they are parallel. See Isaiah 19:16, where the terms

--- Page 110 ---
Did the Prophets Speak Better than They Knew, or Better than We Give Them Credit For?109
The similar language between the passages indicates Hosea believes the 
new David of Hosea 3 is involved in the new Exodus of Hosea 11. Putting 
all of this together, Hosea depicts the new David as the one who, like Mo­
ses, will spearhead a new Exodus for Israel. Hosea links the Exodus with 
the Messiah after all.
Let us return to the original topic. Did Hosea speak better than he 
knew? Did his text only focus on history (the Exodus)? Did his text only 
focus on Israel (as opposed to the Messiah)? We might have viewed Hosea 
11:1 as a history lesson; however, Hoseas writing is far more complicated. 
His theology merges two major implications of the Exodus developed in 
the Old Testament: Exodus to future deliverance (new Exodus), and Exo­
dus in relation to the Davidic dynasty. In bringing these two theological 
themes together, Hosea portrays how Gods love for Israel in the first Exo­
dus will drive a new Exodus, led by a new David. To be sure, Hosea 11:1 
and its entire context do not explicitly predict what happens in Matthew 2. 
Again, the thesis of this chapter does not claim the prophets exhaustively 
understood the future. Rather, my point is that they intentionally laid the 
framework for how later authors will apply their writings. Hosea has done 
that. The elements of a new Exodus, a new David who represents his peo­
ple, and deliverance fit very well with what Matthew will discuss. Hosea 
has put forth the “dots” or the “pieces of the puzzle” for later writers to 
more clearly connect. Arguably, Matthew chose to use Hosea (as opposed 
to quoting Exodus 4:22) for this reason! The apostle wanted to talk about 
the Exodus the way Hosea did. That discussion, though, is for a later chap­
ter. In any case, Hosea did not speak better than he knew but better than 
we give him credit for. His synthesis of different implications of the Exo­
dus contributes uniquely to the biblical theological theme of Gods future 
deliverance for his people.
For that reason, subsequent prophets indeed will build upon what 
Hosea did, and set the stage even more clearly for Matthew. Micah prays 
the Lord would shepherd his people (Mic. 7:4) and deliver Israel with 
Exodus-like miracles (Mic. 7:15). In context, Micah has already iden­
tified that the Messiah is that shepherd (5:4). Thus, scholars conclude 
Micah requests that the Messiah would come lead his people in a new 
Exodus.4 9  Hoseas conception of a new Exodus and new David is part of 
Micahs logic.
Likewise, Isaiah also talks about Exodus to second Exodus 
(Isa. 43:18-19). The prophet reveals the first Exodus will fade away in light
of both passages ("ins and "nn) are used in parallel. Interestingly enough, the back­
ground of that verse may relate to the second Exodus as well.

of the new one to come.5 0  Moreover, Isaiah provides more interesting de­

his Servant (Isa. 49:4-6; 50:9) who is Gods Son (cf. Isa. 9:6 [Heb., v. 5]).5 1  
This again reiterates with further detail what Hosea established. A new 
Exodus will come, spearheaded by a new David, Gods Son.
Overall, Hosea 11:1 in context talks of the Exodus in order to look 
forward to a new Exodus led by a new David. The prophets develop this 
entire paradigm, which facilitates Matthews own conclusions.5 2  The pro­
phetic hermeneutic has carefully woven various factors together to set this 
up for the apostles.
The Rock in the Wilderness (Ex. 13-17)
Another major passage of controversy concerns Pauls statement 
about the rock in the wilderness in 1  Corinthians 10:4. The apostle 
claims Israel drank from the water provided by Christ, who is the rock 
guiding the nation. A cursory reading of the texts in Exodus does not 
seem to provide any such sentiment. There is no mention of the Messiah 
leading Israel in the wilderness, much less any idea that a rock led the 
nation. In light of this, some have alleged that the apostle appeals to a 
rabbinic tradition that states a literal rock followed Israel around in the 
wilderness.5 3  In their mind, Paul must have adopted a cultural herme­
neutic to reach these conclusions.
50. Oswalt, Isaiah 40-66, 155; Motyer, Isaiah, 337. For a more thematic discussion on

Word comes to bear, and the one sitting on the throne is a slain Lamb (53:4-6; cf. Rev. 
5:6, etc.). The strong contrast of this verse with the previous one (‘He said ... But I 
said .. !) expresses that vulnerability and powerlessness. If the Servant described in 
this passage is more than human, he is not less than human. Frustration and feelings 
of futility, all too familiar to everyone who inhabits flesh, were part of the burden he 
came to bear. To become powerless is to experience what the powerless experience 
(see also 50:6; 53:3), and that is the reality of what the Servants blunt retort conveys.

God, why have you forsaken me?’ (Matt. 27:46).” Notice how Oswalt ties the Servants 
weakness with the child motif earlier on in the book of Isaiah. Is this not exactly what 
Matthew does as well, in commenting upon Messiahs deliverance?
